1.3 Possession, home grow, public use, vehicles, product types

Adult (21+) purchase / possession — single-transaction amounts (RCW 69.50.360, opened)

Form Amount per retail transaction (any combination)
Useable cannabis (flower) 1 ounce
Cannabis-infused product, solid form 16 ounces
Cannabis-infused product, liquid form 72 ounces, unless packaged in individual units ≤4 mg THC per unit
Cannabis-infused liquid, low-dose units (≤4 mg THC/unit) 200 mg THC total
Cannabis concentrate 7 grams

Possession of the same amounts is the lawful adult possession ceiling in practice; amounts above the retail limits are not automatically lawful to possess. The older secondary summary that "possession of more than 40 grams is a felony" is outdated / oversimplified after State v. Blake (2021) and SB 5536 (2023): current possession offences are in RCW 69.50.4013 (knowing possession, gross misdemeanour framework) and RCW 69.50.4014 (40 grams or less of cannabis, misdemeanour). Verify the exact current penalty ladder against the opened text of RCW 69.50.4013 / 69.50.4014 at HTML build time — this run did not open those two sections directly, so no penalty figure for simple over-limit possession is asserted here beyond the classification above.

Gifting: Secondary summaries consistently describe a non-commercial gift allowance of half the retail amounts (½ oz flower, 3.5 g concentrate, 8 oz solid, 36 oz liquid) in 24 hours to another adult 21+. The statute for this is RCW 69.50.357 / related delivery provisions — Verify the gift text directly before publishing a gift table; it is not quoted here because it was not opened on this run.

Adult home grow — PROHIBITED

  • Rule: No adult-use home cultivation. Only LCB-licensed producers, and qualifying patients / designated providers / cooperatives under ch. 69.51A, may lawfully grow. LCB's own "growing cannabis at home" guidance states this (cited secondarily; the LCB page itself should be linked in the HTML build — Verify URL at build time as it was not opened directly on this run).
  • Penalty (secondary, to be verified against opened RCW 69.50.401): Unlicensed manufacture/cultivation is described as a class C felony (up to 5 years / $10,000), with the cannabis-specific fine provisions in RCW 69.50.401. Do not publish the $1,000 first-offence / $2,000 second-offence additional fine figures without opening RCW 69.50.401 — secondary sources differ on whether those figures survive the 2023 Blake-fix amendments.
  • 2025–26 attempts failed: HB 1449 / SB 6204 (six plants, 21+) — see §9. Washington is therefore, with the District of Columbia's different model aside, the notable adult-use state with zero recreational home-grow plants.

Patient home grow — see §4 table (6 / 15 / 4 plants).

Public consumption — RCW 69.50.445 (text via official chapter search result; open the section page at build time)

(1) It is unlawful to open a package containing cannabis, useable cannabis, cannabis-infused products, or cannabis concentrates, or consume cannabis, useable cannabis, cannabis-infused products, or cannabis concentrates, in view of the general public or in a public place. (2) For the purposes of this section, "public place" has the same meaning as defined in RCW 66.04.010, but the exclusions in RCW 66.04.011 do not apply. (3) A person who violates this section is guilty of a class 3 civil infraction under chapter 7.80 RCW. Source: http://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=69.50.445 — History note: [2022 c 16 §94; 2015 2nd sp.s. c 4 §401; 2013 c 3 §21 (I-502, approved 6 Nov 2012)]. This covers smoking, vaping and eating in public. Smoking in public places/workplaces is additionally restricted by ch. 70.160 RCW (25-foot rule from entrances etc. — summary only; Part 12 detail).

Vehicles — RCW 46.61.745 (secondary summary of official text; open at build time)

Cannabis in a motor vehicle on a highway must generally be in the trunk, in another area not normally occupied / not directly accessible by driver or passengers (if no trunk), or in an unopened package/container whose seal has not been broken and contents not partially removed. A glove compartment / utility compartment does not qualify as inaccessible. Consuming in a vehicle on a public highway is prohibited under the same public-place logic and the DUI provisions (Part 12). Verify verbatim RCW 46.61.745 text at build time — quoted here only as a secondary summary.

Product types and THC limits

Product Rule
Flower / useable cannabis No per-product THC % cap in RCW 69.50.101; sold by weight (1 oz transaction limit). LCB consumer guidance notes most flower is 12–25% THC (secondary/educational, not a legal cap).
Edibles / ingestible infused products (general adult-use) 10 mg active THC per serving/unit; 100 mg active THC per package; max 10 servings per package (WAC 314-55-095, as summarised in LCB CR-102 memo 31 July 2024 and LCB label guidance). A single concentrate unit ≤1 gram (WAC 314-55-095, petition summary Apr 2025).
Beverages Same 10 mg / 100 mg serving/package framework for cannabis-infused beverages, except the RCW 69.50.360(3)(c)–(d) low-dose liquid carve-out: liquids in individual units ≤4 mg THC/unit are transaction-limited by THC (200 mg) rather than fluid ounces. Hemp-derived THC beverages with any THC are cannabis products — LCB-licensed retail only (see §5).
Vapes / inhalable concentrates Concentrate = >10% THC (RCW 69.50.101(5)). Transaction limit 7 g adult / 21 g recognition-card patient. No separate statewide potency cap for inhalables was located on this run — Verify against WAC 314-55-095 current text; do not assert a vape % cap.
Topicals / CHABA Cannabis health and beauty aids (RCW 69.50.575): topical-only, ≤0.3% THC, not crossing the blood-brain barrier, not for ingestion — exempt from the cannabis-product rules in ch. 69.50. This is the route for general-retail CBD topicals.
DOH medical products (see §4) General Use: same 10 mg / 100 mg. High THC (capsules, tablets, tinctures, patches, suppositories only): up to 50 mg per serving, 500 mg per package, max 10 servings. High CBD: separate DOH category (see DOH page).

Sources: LCB "Reading a Product Label": https://lcb.wa.gov/education2cb/reading_a_product_label ; DOH Medical Cannabis Product Compliance: https://doh.wa.gov/you-and-your-family/cannabis/medical-cannabis/product-compliance ; WAC 314-55-095 petition summary (Apr 2025): https://storage.googleapis.com/co-gcs-replication/organizations/washington_state_liquor_and_cannabis_board/events/2025/2025_04_23-wslcb-board_meeting/2025_04_23-wslcb-board_meeting-meeting_materials-4b-cannabis-concentrates_packaging-rulemaking_petition-recommendation-v1.pdf

1.4 Medical programme

Qualifying conditions — RCW 69.51A.010(6) ("terminal or debilitating medical condition")

(a) Cancer, human immunodeficiency virus (HIV), multiple sclerosis, epilepsy or other seizure disorder, or spasticity disorders; or (b) Intractable pain, limited for the purpose of this chapter to mean pain unrelieved by standard medical treatments and medications; or (c) Glaucoma, either acute or chronic, limited for the purpose of this chapter to mean increased intraocular pressure unrelieved by standard treatments and medications; or (d) Crohn's disease with debilitating symptoms unrelieved by standard treatments or medications; or (e) Hepatitis C with debilitating nausea or intractable pain unrelieved by standard treatments or medications; or (f) Diseases, including anorexia, which result in nausea, vomiting, wasting, appetite loss, cramping, seizures, muscle spasms, or spasticity, when these symptoms are unrelieved by standard treatments or medications; or (g) Any other medical condition duly approved by the Washington state medical quality assurance commission in consultation with the board of osteopathic medicine and surgery as directed in this chapter. Source: RCW 69.51A.010 via Justia 2013 reproduction (https://law.justia.com/codes/washington/2013/title-69/chapter-69.51a/section-69.51a.010/) and DOH patient page (https://doh.wa.gov/you-and-your-family/cannabis/medical-cannabis/patient-information). Note: Secondary summaries add PTSD and traumatic brain injury as approved conditions under (g) — Verify against the Medical Quality Assurance Commission's current approved-condition list before listing PTSD/TBI as statutory conditions; they are not in the (a)–(f) text quoted above. - Qualifying patient (RCW 69.51A.010(4) in the older numbering): patient of a health care professional; diagnosed with a terminal/debilitating condition; Washington resident at time of diagnosis; advised of risks/benefits and that they may benefit. Residency is therefore a statutory element — see out-of-state rule below. - Who may authorise: "health care professional" under ch. 69.51A — physicians, osteopathic physicians, naturopathic physicians, physician assistants and advanced registered nurse practitioners within their licensing scopes (see RCW 69.50.101(40) practitioner list and DOH guidance). The practitioner must use the DOH Medical Cannabis Authorization Form (DOH 630-123, tamper-resistant paper), personally complete/sign the attestation, and may not work out of a cannabis retail store (DOH/secondary summaries — Verify the "may not work out of a store" rule text at build time). First visit in person; video only for follow-up/renewal per secondary summaries — Verify.

Authorisation → database → recognition card (RCW 69.51A.230)

(1) The department [of health] must contract with an entity to create, administer, and maintain a secure and confidential medical cannabis authorization database that allows: (a) A cannabis retailer with a medical cannabis endorsement to add a qualifying patient or designated provider and include the amount … for which the qualifying patient is authorized under RCW 69.51A.210; … (e) A cannabis retailer holding a medical cannabis endorsement to confirm the validity of the recognition card …; (f) The department of revenue to verify tax exemptions under chapters 82.08 and 82.12 RCW; (g) The liquor and cannabis board to verify excise tax exemptions under RCW 69.50.535; … Source: RCW 69.51A.230 via apps.leg.wa.gov PDF (http://apptest.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=69.51A.230&pdf=true) and Justia (https://law.justia.com/codes/washington/title-69/chapter-69-51a/section-69-51a-230/). - Process (DOH): Patient gets authorisation → books a certified medical cannabis consultant at a medically endorsed retailer → consultant enters patient in the database and issues the recognition card on the spot → patient pays a minimum $1 card fee (RCW 69.51A.230; retailers may charge more). Card validity: up to 1 year for adults, 6 months for minors (RCW 69.51A.230(1)(i) summary in search result: authorisations expire 6 months or 1 year after database entry depending on minor/adult). - Database is voluntary for adults, mandatory for minors: Adults may hold an authorisation without entering the database, but then they get only adult purchase limits (see table) and no recognition-card tax benefits. Minors must be entered and hold a card, with a parent/guardian as designated provider (RCW 69.51A.220). - Privacy: Database information "shall not be shared with the federal government or its agents unless the particular qualifying patient or designated provider is convicted in state court for violating this chapter or chapter 69.50 RCW" (RCW 69.51A.230, via Justia summary — Verify subsection number at build time). - Minors (RCW 69.51A.220): Health care professionals may authorise under-18s only as part of treatment of a terminal/debilitating condition; the minor may not grow plants or purchase from an endorsed retailer themselves; both minor and parent/guardian designated provider must be in the database and hold cards. - Designated provider: 21+, named on the patient's authorisation, holds their own authorisation/card, serves one patient at a time, may not use the patient's cannabis for their own benefit (RCW 69.51A.240(1)(d) — class C felony for diversion).

Patient purchase / possession / grow — RCW 69.51A.210 (text retrieved via official apps.leg.wa.gov search result; direct open failed — see header note)

(1) If the health care professional does not include recommendations … the cannabis retailer with a medical cannabis endorsement, when adding the qualifying patient or designated provider to the medical cannabis authorization database, shall enter … that the qualifying patient or designated provider may purchase or obtain at a retail outlet holding a medical cannabis endorsement a combination of the following: Forty-eight ounces of cannabis-infused product in solid form; three ounces of useable cannabis; two hundred sixteen ounces of cannabis-infused product in liquid form; or twenty-one grams of cannabis concentrates. The qualifying patient or designated provider may also grow, in his or her domicile, up to six plants for the personal medical use of the qualifying patient and possess up to eight ounces of useable cannabis produced from his or her plants. These amounts shall be specified on the recognition card … (2) If the health care professional determines that the medical needs … exceed [subsection (1)], the health care professional must specify … that it is recommended that the patient be allowed to grow, in his or her domicile, up to fifteen plants … A patient so authorized may possess up to sixteen ounces of useable cannabis in his or her domicile. … (3) If a qualifying patient or designated provider … has not been entered into the medical cannabis authorization database, he or she may not receive a recognition card and may only purchase at a retail outlet, whether it holds a medical cannabis endorsement or not, the amounts established in RCW 69.50.360. In addition … [they] may grow, in his or her domicile, up to four plants … and possess up to six ounces of useable cannabis in his or her domicile. Source: https://apps.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=69.51A.210 ; DOH patient page confirms the same numbers and adds: no more than 15 plants in a housing unit even if multiple patients/providers live there, except a registered cooperative.

Status Retail purchase (endorsed store) Home grow (domicile) Home possession from own plants
Recognition card, standard 3 oz flower; 48 oz solid; 216 oz liquid; 21 g concentrate 6 plants 8 oz
Recognition card, practitioner-recommended higher need Same retail amounts (statute specifies grow/possession, not higher retail) — Verify whether retail limits also rise 15 plants 16 oz
Authorisation only, NOT in database Adult limits only (1 oz / 16 oz / 72 oz / 7 g) 4 plants 6 oz
Cooperative member (see below) Per card Share of cooperative total, max 60 plants per cooperative Cooperative location: max 72 oz (see below)

Retailer medical endorsements — RCW 69.50.375 + WAC 314-55-080

  • A retailer must hold a medical cannabis endorsement to sell to recognition-card holders at the higher limits, to enter patients in the database / issue cards via a certified consultant, and to sell DOH-compliant products with tax exemptions.
  • Requirements (LCB endorsement reminders, 27 Mar 2026): all employees trained on medical cannabis including identifying authorisations/cards (RCW 69.50.375, WAC 314-55-080); enter patients only in the DOH database; issue only DOH-compliant cards; keep card/records required by DOH/DOR; enter medical sales in the traceability system; check the DOH database every time before granting a tax exemption (status can change). Source: LCB GovDelivery bulletin: https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/WALCB/bulletins/4104f79
  • Consultants: Certified medical cannabis consultants staff endorsed stores; they — not the practitioner — enter the patient and print the card. Consultant certification/training rules are in WAC 314-55-080 and DOH rules — Verify current consultant training hours at build time; not asserted here.

Cooperatives — RCW 69.51A.250

(1) Qualifying patients or designated providers may form a cooperative and share responsibility for acquiring and supplying the resources needed to produce and process cannabis only for the medical use of members of the cooperative. No more than four qualifying patients or designated providers may become members of a cooperative under this section and all members must hold valid recognition cards. All members of the cooperative must be at least twenty-one years old. (6)(a) May grow up to the total amount of plants for which each participating member is authorized on their recognition cards, up to a maximum of sixty plants. At the location, the qualifying patients or designated providers may possess the amount of useable cannabis that can be produced with the number of plants permitted under this subsection, but no more than seventy-two ounces … Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=69.51A.250 ; DOH patient page: https://doh.wa.gov/you-and-your-family/cannabis/medical-cannabis/patient-information - Additional rules: one cooperative per member; one registered location (registered with LCB, WAC 314-55-410); one cooperative per tax parcel (DOH guide); plants must start as immature plants/clones purchased from a licensed producer; 60-day wait before a new member may join after a departure notice. DOH's patient page states cooperative members possess "up to 8 ounces" in one summary line — that appears to be a per-member shorthand and conflicts with the 72 oz cooperative-location figure in RCW 69.51A.250(6)(a); use the statute (72 oz at the location) as controlling and flag the DOH shorthand as a conflict for Part 4/11. - Collectives (pre-2016) were replaced by cooperatives on 1 July 2016; do not describe a "collective" as a current vehicle.

Sales-tax and excise-tax exemptions

  • Excise (37%): exempt only until 30 June 2029, only for recognition-card holders, only at endorsed retailers, only for DOH-compliant products tested to WAC 246-70-040 (RCW 69.50.535(2), opened). All three conditions must hold; otherwise excise applies.
  • Sales tax: RCW 82.08.9998(1) exempts: (a) DOH-compliant products sold by endorsed retailers to card holders; (b) ≤0.3% THC products sold by endorsed retailers to card holders; (c) DOH-identified low-THC / high-CBD beneficial products sold by endorsed retailers to any person (not only card holders); (d) topical ≤0.3% THC products sold by health care professionals under RCW 69.51A.280; (e) cooperative-produced products provided to members. Source: http://apptest.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=82.08.9998&pdf=true — History: [2022 c 16 §155; 2019 c 393 §4; 2015 2nd sp.s. c 4 §207]. The sales-tax exemption has no 2029 sunset stated in the section text retrieved — Verify sunset at build time; secondary summaries say it does not carry the excise sunset.
  • CHABA: sales- and excise-tax exempt per LCB endorsement reminders (RCW 69.50.575).

Out-of-state patients — NO reciprocity

  • Washington does not recognise another state's medical card/authorisation. A qualifying patient must be a Washington resident at diagnosis (RCW 69.51A.010) and hold a Washington authorisation on DOH form 630-123. DOH's patient page and secondary summaries agree: "Healthcare practitioner authorizations, ID cards and other documentation from other states aren't valid in Washington."
  • Visitors 21+ may still buy at adult-use limits (1 oz etc.) with valid ID at a licensed retailer — that is an adult-use purchase, not a medical purchase, with no medical limits or tax exemptions.
  • Ryan's Law (2026): Secondary summaries note "Ryan's Law took effect June 11, 2026, addressing medical cannabis use by qualifying terminally ill patients in specified hospitals, nursing homes, and hospice settings." Bill number and RCW citation were not located on this run — Verify and do not cite a bill number here. This belongs in detail to Part 12 (medical in facilities/schools) in any case.

2016 merger + Schedule III / 280E — Washington analysis (read with Part 0 headline)

  1. What merged: SB 5052 closed the unregulated medical dispensary/collective market and required patients to buy from LCB retailers (with endorsements for medical sales). Washington therefore has no separate medical licence class — "medical" is an endorsement + a DOH product standard + a database status layered onto the adult-use licence.
  2. Schedule III: On the given baseline, Schedule III covers "state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana." An endorsed retail sale to a card holder is (a) state-licensed (the retailer holds an LCB licence), (b) medical in purpose (DOH-compliant product, card holder, database-verified), but (c) the licence itself is an adult-use retail licence with an endorsement, not a medical-only licence. Whether the federal rule's drafters intended (c) to disqualify endorsement sales is VERIFY — no federal guidance or Washington guidance resolving it was opened on this run.
  3. 280E: Same Verify applies. Until it is closed, the safe statement is: adult-use sales remain fully subject to 280E; medical endorsement sales may qualify for 280E relief under the Schedule III change but that treatment is unconfirmed for Washington's endorsement model and must not be presented as settled tax advice.
  4. Tribal: Whether medical activity by a tribal business operating under a state compact (RCW 43.06.490) counts as "state-licensed" is VERIFY for the same reason, plus the additional question whether compact (government-to-government) licensing is "state" licensing at all. Part 10 must carry this Verify forward with compact texts.
  5. State taxes are unaffected by 280E either way: Washington's 37% excise, sales tax and B&O tax are state taxes; 280E is a federal income-tax disallowance. The state medical excise/sales exemptions above are independent of federal scheduling.

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Definition and measurement — state law (current)

  • Hemp (plant/material): ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis (RCW 15.140.020(7), opened). This is a delta-9-only test at the plant/material stage, distinct from the federal total-THC concept in the given baseline.
  • Cannabis (plant): >0.3% THC concentration on a dry-weight basis during growing cycle through harvest and useable cannabis (RCW 69.50.101(4), opened). Note the cannabis definition says "THC concentration," not "delta-9 THC concentration" — the SB 5367 rewrite broadened the product-side test (see below).
  • WSDA testing (secondary + WSDA FAQ): WSDA samples within the pre-harvest window (federal rules moved sampling from 30 days to 15 days before harvest; WSDA uses HPLC and calculates total THC on a dry-weight basis for USDA compliance), with post-harvest testing defined in RCW 15.140.020(11) as ground whole-plant samples without heat applied or other approved methods. Verify the current WAC 16-306 sampling window and total-THC calculation at WSDA build time (Part 7) — the FAQ text retrieved is a summary, not the rule.
  • USDA plan: Washington operates under its own USDA-approved state plan (USDA AMS list: "Washington — WA Approved Plan"), administered by WSDA — it does not use the USDA direct producer-licence programme. USDA list updated 17 Feb 2026: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review ; WSDA hemp FAQ: https://agr.wa.gov/departments/agricultural-products/hemp/hemp-faq ; WSDA rulemaking page (ch. 16-306, CR-101 filed 17 Nov 2025, WSR 25-23-059, proposing to align the THC-testing definition with USDA total THC): https://agr.wa.gov/services/rulemaking/wac-16-306-hemp-111725

Hemp-derived THC as cannabis — the SB 5367 channel rule

  • Rule in one sentence: If it is intended to be consumed or absorbed inside the body by any means (inhalation, ingestion, insertion) and it contains any detectable amount of THC, it is a "cannabis product" (RCW 69.50.101(8)(a)) and may be manufactured/sold/distributed only by an LCB licensee (RCW 69.50.3251(1)), or under a tribal agreement (RCW 43.06.490). Sale without a licence is prohibited and subject to criminal sanctions (LCB THC guide, May 2025).
  • What this bars outside LCB retail: hemp-derived delta-9 edibles and beverages, delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THC-O and similar converted products, and smokable THCA flower sold as hemp — all contain detectable THC and are intended for consumption/absorption. LCB's May 2025 guide states this expressly: "The sale of any product(s) containing detectable amounts of THC without a valid cannabis license is prohibited by state law."
  • What remains in general retail: THC-free / non-detectable-THC hemp consumables and CBD products, and CHABA topicals (≤0.3% THC, topical-only, non-ingestible). CBD added to food/beverages or sold as a dietary supplement is not permitted in Washington (WSDA FAQ / FDA position summarised secondarily) — hemp extract is not an approved food ingredient in Washington until federally authorised (RCW 69.07 provisions as amended 2021 c 104 — Verify current food-ingredient status at Part 7 build time, as WSDA's Dec 2025 rulemaking may affect related rules).
  • CBD additive into LCB products (RCW 69.50.326, opened via search result): Licensed producers/processors may use a CBD product as an additive to enhance CBD concentration; outside-system CBD is allowed only if it (a) is not cannabis/cannabis product, (b) is not a synthetic cannabinoid, and (c) has been tested for contaminants/toxins by a lab accredited/certified under ch. 69.50. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/Rcw/default.aspx?cite=69.50.326

Synthetic and converted cannabinoids

  • Banned: "The producing, processing, manufacturing, or sale of any synthetically derived, or completely synthetic, cannabinoid is prohibited, except for products approved by the United States food and drug administration." (RCW 69.50.3251(3), 2023 c 365 §5.)
  • Converted cannabinoids (e.g. CBD→delta-8): LCB Policy Statement PS21-01 (28 Apr 2021) and Interpretive Statement IS-21-02 (22 July 2021) had already taken the position that conversion of CBD/hemp to delta-8, delta-9 or any other "marijuana" compound not defined in RCW/WAC was not an allowable processor practice; SB 5367 then codified the channel ban and the synthetic ban. The LCB interpretive statement is a policy document, not a statute — cite it as LCB guidance, not law. Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/rules/Statements/IS-21-02_MJ_Processor_License_Allowable_Practices.pdf
  • "THC concentration" broadening: SB 5367 removed the prior delta-9-only language from the product-side THC definition so that total THC across isomers is counted for cannabis-product status. The exact post-SB 5367 definition of "THC concentration" in RCW 69.50.101 (which subsection, and whether it lists delta-8, delta-10, THCA individually) was not opened in full on this run — Verify the definition subsection verbatim at build time; secondary summaries differ on whether they list isomers or use a general "any tetrahydrocannabinol" formula, and WAC 314-55-102's total-THC formula (with the 0.877 THCA decarboxylation factor and the 0.2 mg/g reporting threshold in WAC 314-55-105) should be quoted from the rule, not from summaries.

Minimum age, testing, labelling — hemp-derived products inside the LCB system

  • Age: 21+ for any cannabis product, including hemp-derived THC sold through LCB retail (RCW 69.50.360, WAC 314-55-079(1)). No 18+ or 21+ general-retail hemp-THC age rule exists because general-retail hemp-THC sale is prohibited.
  • Testing/labelling inside LCB: Same WAC 314-55-102 (testing) / 314-55-105 (packaging/labelling) regime as cannabis-derived products, including total-THC calculation per tetrahydrocannabinol compound present above 0.2 mg/g, serving/package limits in WAC 314-55-095, and the "Not For Kids" symbol (WAC 314-55-106). Products must be produced within Washington's I-502 system to be sold by LCB retailers (LCB THC guide) — out-of-state hemp THC may not be imported into LCB retail as a hemp product.
  • Hemp consumables outside LCB: Testing/labelling for non-THC hemp consumables is governed by WSDA / food / general consumer-product rules, not WAC 314-55 — Verify whether Washington has a standalone hemp-consumable testing/labelling rule (none was located on this run beyond WSDA producer testing and the food-extract provisions).

THC beverages — specifically

  • A beverage with any detectable THC is a cannabis product / cannabis-infused product in liquid form: LCB retail only, 21+, 10 mg per serving / 100 mg per package as a general rule, with the RCW 69.50.360 low-dose liquid transaction rule (individual units ≤4 mg THC → 200 mg THC transaction limit) applying at the till. A "hemp THC seltzer" sold in a grocery/convenience store or shipped from out of state to a Washington consumer outside LCB retail is prohibited under RCW 69.50.3251 — this is the most commonly misunderstood point and should be stated plainly in the HTML.

CBD products — specifically

  • CBD itself is not scheduled as cannabis where it is not a cannabis product (RCW 69.50.101(13): "'CBD product' means any product containing or consisting of cannabidiol"). Lawful general-retail CBD must be THC-non-detectable if it is a consumable (otherwise it is a cannabis product), or a CHABA topical if it contains ≤0.3% THC and meets RCW 69.50.575. CBD from LCB-licensed producers/processors may be used as an additive under RCW 69.50.326 (above). No Washington CBD potency cap for general-retail non-THC CBD was located — Verify; do not assert one.

Washington response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates

  • Given federal dates (not re-researched): PL 119-37 §781 eff. 12 Nov 2026; PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 Dec 2026; after 11 Dec 2026 the full total-THC definition + 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA DEA-lab enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026.
  • Washington position at 1 Nov 2026 (this run): No Washington statute, WAC amendment, WSDA rule or LCB guidance was located that (a) adopts the federal total-THC definition into RCW 15.140.020, (b) adopts a 0.4 mg per-container cap into state law, or (c) states how WSDA will treat a crop/product that is hemp under the old delta-9 test but not hemp under the new total-THC test after 11 Dec 2026. WSDA's pending ch. 16-306 rulemaking (CR-101, Nov 2025) proposes aligning the THC-testing definition with USDA total THC for producer testing clarity — that is a testing-definition alignment, not a per-container cap, and its adoption/effective date were not confirmed on this run (proposed adoption date listed as "[To be determined]" on the WSDA page).
  • Practical consequence to state plainly: Because SB 5367 already channels any detectable-THC consumable into LCB retail, the federal per-container cap does not create a new Washington retail channel — it may, however, affect (i) whether WSDA-licensed hemp material remains "hemp" federally after 11 Dec 2026, (ii) interstate shipment of Washington hemp, and (iii) whether LCB retailers may continue to source any hemp-derived ingredient that is no longer federally hemp. All three are VERIFY after 11 Dec 2026; do not present the federal cap as already being Washington law.
  • What to watch (follow-up for Run 2 / post-Dec update): WSDA ch. 16-306 adoption notice; LCB guidance on hemp-derived ingredients in LCB products after 11 Dec 2026; any 2027 bill conforming RCW 15.140.020 / 69.50.101 to the federal definition.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning/buffers (summary; full local detail is Part 3)

Packaging and labelling — WAC 314-55-105 (and WAC 314-55-095 servings)

  • Child-resistant packaging: Concentrates must be in child-resistant packaging consistent with 16 CFR Part 1700, or in plastic ≥2 mil thick, heat-sealed without an easy-open tab/dimple/corner/flap (WAC 314-55-105(2)(b), via petition summary and rule search result).
  • Labels must show (solid edibles example, WAC 314-55-105): business/trade name + 9-digit UBI of producer/processor; lot number (matching the transport manifest); serving size, amount per serving, number of servings (prominently); net weight/volume; total THC calculated individually per tetrahydrocannabinol compound present above 0.2 mg/g using the WAC 314-55-102 formula; total CBD (CBDA + CBD); medically/scientifically accurate health/safety risk information; required warnings (including driving, habit-forming, delayed-effect and out-of-state warnings as applicable by category). Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-105%20
  • "Not For Kids" symbol: Required on cannabis packaging with the Poison Center number (800) 222-1222 (LCB label guidance; WAC 314-55-106 symbol rules — Verify symbol section number at build time).
  • Serving/package limits restated: 10 mg / 100 mg general; DOH High THC 50 mg / 500 mg (capsules/tablets/tinctures/patches/suppositories only); single concentrate unit ≤1 gram; see §3.
  • Edibles labelling specifics: Solid edibles must not be labelled "organic" unless USDA-permitted under the Organic Foods Production Act; must comply with NIST Handbook 130 as adopted in ch. 16-662 WAC (WAC 314-55-105, via search result).
  • No "one-ounce container" rule: Transaction limits (RCW 69.50.360) are not container-size limits; package/serving limits (WAC 314-55-095) are separate. Do not conflate them.

Advertising — RCW 69.50.369 + WAC 314-55-155 (as amended July 2026)

  • General bans (WAC 314-55-155(2)): No false/misleading statement or illustration; no depiction of a child / person under legal age, no cartoon/movie characters or other depictions appealing to children; no portrayal of alcohol/nicotine/tobacco use or associations with motor vehicles (2026 amendment summary); billboards must state cannabis may be purchased/possessed only by persons 21+ and carry required warnings; no plant/product depictions on billboards (leaf = plant, joint = product, per older LCB FAQ — Verify whether the 2026 rule retained the plant/product depiction ban for billboards vs. trade-name signs, as the 2026 amendment allowed logos/artwork not depicting plants/products on trade-name signs).
  • Retail exterior signage (current, post-4 July 2026):

    "… licensed cannabis retailers may not display any cannabis-related advertising signage on the licensed premises other than four separate signs, not exceeding 1,600 square inches each, that are affixed to the building, or hanging in the window of the licensed location, on the side of the building with the main entrance. The 1,600 square inch size limitation refers to the actual sign itself, not the text or other content within it, and will be enforced by measuring the physical perimeter of the sign." Source: WAC 314-55-155 via https://apps.leg.wa.gov/WAC/default.aspx?cite=314-55-155 and WSR 26-12-082. Plus up to 2 trade-name signs and billboards as authorised under RCW 69.50.369. Signs <512 sq in that do not include brand/trade names or cannabis images and show only general information (e.g. hours) are exempt from counting as advertising signs (2026 CR-102 summary).

  • Stale guidance warning: The LCB advertising FAQ page still in search results says "two signs" — that page predates ESB 5206 / WSR 26-12-082 and must not be quoted for sign count. Link it only with a "stale" flag, or omit it in favour of the WAC text.
  • Promotional items / coupons / giveaways: WAC 314-55-155 as amended July 2026 also updated limits on the value of incidental promotional items and coupons offered by retailers (WSR 26-12-082 purpose statement) — Verify the dollar/value limit in the current rule text; no figure is asserted here.

Delivery — PROHIBITED (retail to consumer)

"Internet sales and delivery of product to customers is prohibited." — WAC 314-55-079(5) Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-079 (also https://app.leg.wa.gov/WAc/default.aspx?cite=314-55-079&pdf=true, certified 22 July 2026). Filing note: WSR 26-14-119 (filed 1 July 2026, eff. 1 July 2026); prior WSR 22-21-058. - What is allowed instead: In-store sale on the licensed premises (RCW 69.50.360(3): "on the premises of the retail outlet"); retailer transport between its own locations or return to a processor under WAC 314-55-085 (WAC 314-55-079(11)); licensed transporter delivery between licensees (Part 4), not to consumers. Online ordering for in-store pickup is not "delivery" in the WAC sense only if no product is delivered to the customer off-premises — Verify LCB's current pickup guidance before describing any "order-ahead" model; the rule text prohibits "internet sales and delivery," and LCB enforcement positions on pickup have varied. - No delivery licence, no delivery pilot, no medical delivery exception was located on this run. Any 2025–26 delivery bill is Verify in §9 (none is listed as enacted below).

Zoning and buffers — statewide summary (full jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction detail is Part 3)

  • State buffer (RCW 69.50.331(8) + WAC 314-55-050(2)–(3)): The board will not issue a new cannabis licence if the proposed business is within 1,000 feet (shortest straight-line distance, property line to property line) of the perimeter of: (a) elementary or secondary school; (b) playground; (c) recreation center or facility; (d) child care center; (e) public park; (f) public transit center; (g) library; or (h) any game arcade where admission is not restricted to persons 21+.
  • Local reduction (RCW 69.50.331(8)(b) / WAC 314-55-050(3)): A city/county/town may by ordinance permit licensing within 1,000 feet but not less than 100 feet of the facilities above except elementary schools, secondary schools and playgrounds, provided the reduction will not negatively impact civil regulatory enforcement, criminal law enforcement, public safety or public health. The applicant must provide the LCB a copy of the local ordinance stating the reduced distance. Research licences have a parallel reduction provision (RCW 69.50.331(8)(c) in the Justia numbering — Verify subsection letter at build time).
  • Local authority generally (summary only): Local governments control zoning (where licence types may site), may ban/prohibit siting (with revenue-sharing consequences in Part 3), and set the reduced buffer by ordinance. They may not authorise a buffer below 100 feet, and may not reduce the school/playground 1,000-foot buffer at all. Do not state any city's reduced distance (e.g. 300 feet in Ocean Shores / Lynnwood proposals seen in search results) as adopted law without opening that city's ordinance — those were proposals/drafts in the search results, not confirmed adoptions for this report.
  • Measurement and definitions: "Playground," "school," etc. are defined in WAC 314-55-010 — open that section at build time for the HTML definitions table.

1.7 Seed-to-sale / traceability — system and history

  • Current system: Cannabis Central Reporting System (CCRS) — LCB-operated, licensee-reported data system. LCB and JLARC both describe it as a reporting system, not a traceability system: it does not facilitate inter-licensee transaction exchange, licensees cannot read back their own uploaded data in the way a seed-to-sale system would allow, and reporting is at minimum weekly (not real-time movement/sale tracking).
  • History (JLARC July 2025 + LCB Nov 2024 petition response, agreeing): 1. BioTrackTHC, 2013–2017 — the first, full seed-to-sale traceability system; JLARC calls it the most reliable data period, the last year of reliable production/sales data being 2017. 2. Leaf Data Systems (MJ Freeway), 2017–2021 — replaced BioTrack when its contract expired; never fully deployed, multiple bugs/errors; LCB built the Contingency Reporting System (CRS) as a backup during the transition. 3. CCRS, Dec 2021–present — CRS redesigned/relaunched Dec 2021 and renamed Cannabis Central Reporting System; a Jan 2023 update added manifest intake but did not increase data validation. LCB describes CCRS as a stop-gap while a long-term system is identified.
  • Current status / replacement: LCB Cannabis Traceability Project began 2023 (Phase 1 feasibility study). A 2023 request for information returned estimates from a few million dollars to $50 million. LCB requested $3.972M / 3.5 FTE in the 2026 supplemental budget (not funded, per secondary report) and about $12M / 8 FTE in the 2027–29 budget request (Sept 2026 secondary report). JLARC recommended LCB submit a plan to the legislature by 31 Dec 2025 detailing resources/timeline to collect accurate data by 31 Dec 2026. Verify whether that plan was submitted and what the legislature funded — this is a Part 5/6 follow-up, not a Part 1 conclusion.
  • Rule anchor: WAC 314-55-083 (recordkeeping is the licensee's responsibility; limits the data that must be reported) is cited by LCB/SAO as constraining CCRS's scope. The traceability obligation in statute is RCW 69.50.342 / 69.50.385 (board rulemaking for traceability) — Verify the exact traceability statute subsection at build time.
  • Practical consequence for this dossier: Do not present CCRS figures as seed-to-sale totals. DOR excise-derived sales estimates and LCB excise collections are different series on different bases (see Part 6 note in the parent's scope) and must not be averaged.

1.8 Employment and drug testing — SB 5123 (2023) → RCW 49.44.240

Operative text (opened via apps.leg.wa.gov):

(1) It is unlawful for an employer to discriminate against a person in the initial hiring for employment if the discrimination is based upon: (a) The person's use of cannabis off the job and away from the workplace; or (b) An employer-required drug screening test that has found the person to have nonpsychoactive cannabis metabolites in their hair, blood, urine, or other bodily fluids. (2) Nothing in this section: (a) Prohibits an employer from basing initial hiring decisions on scientifically valid drug screening conducted through methods that do not screen for nonpsychoactive cannabis metabolites; (b) Affects the rights or obligations of an employer to maintain a drug and alcohol free workplace, or any other rights or obligations of an employer required by federal law or regulation; or (c) Applies to testing for controlled substances other than preemployment, such as postaccident testing or testing because of a suspicion of impairment or being under the influence of alcohol, controlled substances, medications, or other substances. (3) This section does not apply to an applicant seeking: (a) A position requiring a federal government background investigation or security clearance; (b) A position with a general authority Washington law enforcement agency as defined in RCW 10.93.020; (c) A position with a fire department, fire protection district, or regional fire protection service authority; [plus, per secondary summaries of the full subsection: other first responders including 911 dispatchers; corrections positions with direct access to persons in custody; airline/aerospace industry positions; and safety-sensitive positions where impairment presents a substantial risk of death, which the employer must identify before the applicant applies.] (4)(a) This section does not preempt state or federal laws requiring an applicant to be tested for controlled substances [including as a condition of federal funding, licensing-related benefits, or a federal contract]. (b) Employers may require an applicant to be tested for a spectrum of controlled substances, which may include cannabis, as long as the cannabis results are not provided to the employer. … (5) For the purposes of this section, "cannabis" has the meaning provided in RCW 69.50.101. Source: https://apps.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=49.44.240 — History: [2023 c 359 §2; signed 9 May 2023; effective 1 Jan 2024]. Legislative finding (2023 c 359 §1): tests showing nonpsychoactive metabolites from use up to 30 days in the past "have no correlation to an applicant's future job performance." - Scope limits to state plainly: (i) Initial hiring only — it does not protect current employees from discipline for on-the-job impairment or from post-hire testing; (ii) it does not require an employer to tolerate workplace use/impairment; (iii) it does not override federal DOT / federal-contract testing; (iv) an employer may still use a scientifically valid test that does not screen for nonpsychoactive metabolites (e.g. an active-THC / impairment-oriented method) for hiring decisions. - No broader off-duty protection: Unlike some states' laws, Washington has no general statute protecting current employees' lawful off-duty cannabis use — Verify whether any 2025–26 bill proposed such an expansion (none is listed as enacted in §9). - Licence-holder employee rules (background checks, etc.) belong to Part 11, not this section.

1.9 Pending legislation — 2025–26 biennium, status confirmed on leg.wa.gov where stated

Session note: Washington has a biennial legislature: a long (odd-year) session and a short (even-year) session. Bills introduced in 2025 carry over into the 2026 session if retained in status ("reintroduced and retained in present status" on 12 Jan 2026 for HB 1449, for example). A bill in "HApprops" or "SRules X" at the end of the 2026 session did not pass in that biennium and must be reintroduced in 2027 to proceed. Verify the constitutional session lengths (105 days odd-year / 60 days even-year) against the Washington Constitution / leg.wa.gov session calendar at build time — the lengths are stated here only as background and are flagged Verify for that reason.

  • Two direct page-open attempts in this session failed at the tool layer (transient grant error, not a finding that the sites are down or login-gated): the Department of Revenue page at https://dor.wa.gov/taxes-rates/taxes-due-cannabis and the LCB location-requirements page at https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/location-requirements. Per task instructions those attempts were not retried. Facts attributed to DOR/LCB pages below therefore rest on search-indexed copies of those pages and on statute text returned by search, and are flagged where the underlying page could not be re-opened and re-read in full on 2026-11-01. The parent/auditor should re-open those two URLs before treating any DOR/LCB-only figure as finally confirmed.
  • Law is taken from RCW text and official city/county ordinance material returned by search. Secondary sources (MRSC, JLARC, trade/industry pages, local news) are used only for non-law figures, for the state of local debate, or where an official list could not be retrieved — and are labelled as such. No proposed bill is presented as current law.
  • Verify = not confirmed from a directly opened official source on 2026-11-01, conflicting, or otherwise unsettled. Unavailable = no usable figure was found. Nothing in this report is guessed.

PART 2 — TAXES

2.1 The 37% cannabis excise tax — statute, base, who pays, who collects

Statute: RCW 69.50.535, “Cannabis excise tax — Medical exemption.” History line returned with the statute: 2024 c 79 s 1; 2022 c 16 s 101; 2015 2nd sp.s. c 4 s 205; 2014 c 192 s 7; 2013 c 3 s 27 (Initiative Measure No. 502, approved November 6, 2012). [Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/washington/title-69/chapter-69-50/section-69-50-535/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Verbatim operative text, RCW 69.50.535(1)(a) (as returned):

“There is levied and collected a cannabis excise tax equal to thirty-seven percent of the selling price on each retail sale in this state of cannabis concentrates, useable cannabis, and cannabis-infused products. This tax is separate and in addition to general state and local sales and use taxes that apply to retail sales of tangible personal property, and is not part of the total retail price to which general state and local sales and use taxes apply. The tax must be separately itemized from the state and local retail sales tax on the sales receipt provided to the buyer.”

The same subsection requires the tax to be “reflected in the price list or quoted shelf price in the licensed cannabis retail store and in any advertising that includes prices.” [Source: same RCW 69.50.535 link above — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Who pays / who collects / where it is paid:

  • “The tax imposed in this section must be paid by the buyer to the seller. Each seller must collect from the buyer the full amount of the tax payable on each taxable sale. The tax collected … is deemed to be held in trust by the seller until paid to the board.” — RCW 69.50.535(4), as returned. [Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/washington/title-69/chapter-69-50/section-69-50-535/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • “All revenues collected from the cannabis excise tax imposed under this section must be deposited each day in the dedicated cannabis account.” — RCW 69.50.535(3). [Source: same — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Administration split: the 37% excise tax is paid to the Liquor and Cannabis Board (LCB), not to the Department of Revenue (DOR). DOR's own guidance states: “Revenue does not accept tax payments for the LCB. Cannabis excise taxes due must be paid to LCB,” while retail sales tax “must be paid to the Department of Revenue.” [Source: https://dor.wa.gov/taxes-rates/taxes-due-cannabis — Accessed 2026-11-01; direct re-open failed, see Method note — Verify the live wording on re-open]
  • There is no separate wholesale-stage cannabis excise tax in the current structure: the pre-2015 three-tier 25% wholesale taxes were replaced by the single 37% retail tax in 2015 (2nd sp. sess. c 4). The 2015 bill text returned shows the old 25% producer and processor taxes struck and replaced with “thirty-seven percent of the selling price on each retail sale.” [Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2015-16/Pdf/Bills/Senate%20Bills/6062-S.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Verify: the current WAC 314-55-090 / LCB excise-reporting mechanics (filing frequency, due dates, late-payment treatment) were not confirmed from a directly opened LCB/DOR page in this session.

Base nuance: because the 37% tax is not part of the price to which sales tax applies, sales tax is computed on the pre-excise selling price, not on a price already grossed up by the 37%. The excise itself is, however, reflected in the shelf/quoted price. Retailers must separately itemise excise vs. sales tax on the receipt. [Source: RCW 69.50.535(1)(a) link above — Accessed 2026-11-01]

2.2 State and local sales (retail sales/use) tax on cannabis

  • State rate: 6.5%. RCW 82.08.020 is cited in secondary summaries for the 6.5% base rate; DOR publishes local rates quarterly (January, April, July, October). [Source for 6.5% base and quarterly DOR tables: https://stripe.com/resources/more/washington-sales-tax-rate — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary summary of DOR material — Verify against the live DOR rate table before budgeting]
  • Combined rates vary by delivery/store location (Washington uses destination-based sourcing for sales tax generally). Secondary rate tables for 2026 conflict with each other, largely because Seattle and King County each added a 0.1% public-safety tax on 1 January 2026, moving Seattle from ~10.35% to ~10.55% in the newer tables. Examples returned, all secondary and dated 2026 — do not treat any single city figure below as settled without checking the DOR table for the exact quarter:
  • Seattle: 10.55% (Oct 2026 table) vs. 10.35% (June 2026 table). [Sources: https://trykintsugi.com/sales-tax-guides/usa/washington and https://www.xero.com/us/guides/washington-sales-tax/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Spokane: 9.1% (most 2026 tables) vs. 8.9–9.0% in others. [Sources: https://trykintsugi.com/sales-tax-guides/usa/washington; https://taxcloud.com/sales-tax/washington/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Tacoma: 10.3–10.5% across tables. [Sources: same — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Vancouver, WA: 8.5–8.9% across tables (and 7.8% in one table — outlier, Verify). [Sources: same — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Bellevue: 10.3% (Oct 2026) vs. 10.1–10.5% in others. [Sources: same — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Statewide combined range reported for 2026: roughly 7.6% to 10.7%. [Source: https://trykintsugi.com/sales-tax-guides/usa/washington — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary]
  • Practical combined burden (secondary, modelled): one Q3 2026 market report models a 43.3% effective state tax rate for 2025 ($495.8m state excise + sales tax ÷ $1,144.6m sales), explicitly labelled “Modeled-Est.”, not an official rate. [Source: https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary/modelled — do not present as an official rate]
  • Verify: the exact DOR combined rate for any specific store address in Q4 2026. The DOR rate-lookup tool and quarterly local-rate tables are the follow-up source.

2.3 Business & Occupation (B&O) tax

  • B&O is Washington's gross-receipts business tax: it is imposed on gross receipts without deduction for labour, materials, rent or cost of goods sold. [Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2025-26/Pdf/Bill%20Reports/House/6113-S.E%20HBR%20FIN%2026.pdf (House Finance bill report summarising B&O) — Accessed 2026-11-01, legislative-staff summary, not statute text]
  • Classifications/rates returned (Verify against current RCW 82.04 / DOR classifications page before budgeting):
  • Retailing: 0.471% of gross receipts.
  • Wholesaling and manufacturing: 0.484%.
  • Services and other activities: tiered 1.5% / 1.75% / 2.1% by taxable income in the 2026 bill-report summary. [Sources: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2025-26/Pdf/Bill%20Reports/House/6113-S.E%20HBR%20FIN%2026.pdf; https://legalclarity.org/what-is-excise-tax-in-washington-state/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • DOR's cannabis guidance confirms the structure for cannabis businesses: “Business and occupation (B&O) tax applies to your business's gross receipts. You must also collect and submit sales tax on your retail transactions. Retail sales tax must be paid to the Department of Revenue.” [Source: https://dor.wa.gov/taxes-rates/taxes-due-cannabis — Accessed 2026-11-01; direct re-open failed — Verify]
  • Verify — possible 2027 change: the same 2026 bill report states “Beginning January 1, 2027, the rate for certain business activities increases to 0.5 percent, including standard manufacturing, extracting, wholesaling, and retailing.” Whether that change is enacted law, and whether it applies to cannabis retailing specifically, was not confirmed from statute text in this session. Flag for the Part 1 / audit track; do not budget on 0.5% yet. [Source: same bill report — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • Local B&O taxes may also apply in some cities (e.g. Seattle's business taxes generally). No cannabis-specific local B&O rate was confirmed — Verify city by city; do not assume Seattle's general B&O treatment applies unchanged to cannabis retailers.

2.4 Medical exemptions — sales tax AND excise tax (they are different, and narrower than “patients pay no tax”)

2.4.1 Excise-tax exemption — RCW 69.50.535(2)

Verbatim (as returned), RCW 69.50.535(2)(a):

“Until June 30, 2029, the tax levied by subsection (1) of this section does not apply to sales by a cannabis retailer with a medical cannabis endorsement to qualifying patients or designated providers who have been issued a recognition card, of cannabis concentrates, useable cannabis, or cannabis-infused products, identified by the department as a compliant cannabis product in chapter 246-70 WAC and tested to the standards in WAC 246-70-040.”

[Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/washington/title-69/chapter-69-50/section-69-50-535/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]

All three conditions must coincide: (i) retailer holds a medical cannabis endorsement, (ii) buyer is a qualifying patient / designated provider with a recognition card, (iii) product is a DOH-compliant product under WAC 246-70 and tested to WAC 246-70-040. If any condition fails, the 37% applies. Sellers claiming the exemption must maintain eligibility information as required by the board, and the board must provide a separate tax-reporting line for exemption amounts. [Source: same — Accessed 2026-11-01]

History: the exemption was added by HB 1453 (2024 c 79 s 1), effective 6 June 2024 in secondary summaries, and sunsets 30 June 2029. A JLARC review is noted in secondary material for 1 December 2028 — Verify the JLARC date against the enrolled bill. [Sources: RCW history line above; https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Accessed 2026-11-01]

2.4.2 Sales/use-tax exemption — RCW 82.08.9998 (cited by MRSC/DOR)

MRSC summarises: “RCW 82.08.9998 exempts sales of certain medical cannabis products and high CBD/low THC products from retail sales tax.” [Source: https://mrsc.org/explore-topics/business-regulation/types/cannabis-regulation — Accessed 2026-11-01]

DOR's cannabis guidance lists the sales/use-tax exemptions as (paraphrased from the search-indexed DOR page; re-open to confirm verbatim):

  • sales/donations by a medically endorsed retailer of DOH-compliant cannabis products to recognition-card customers;
  • sales/donations of low-THC products (0.3% or less) to recognition-card customers;
  • sales of high-CBD compliant cannabis products to all consumers;
  • sales/donations of topical, nondigestible low-THC products (0.3% or less) by certain healthcare professionals to qualifying patients;
  • cannabis/low-THC products produced by a cooperative for its members.

DOR adds a critical breadth note: “For purposes of this exemption, the DOH has determined that a ‘compliant cannabis product’ is any cannabis product purchased by a qualifying patient with a recognition card. See DOH policy statement MMJ 21-01.” That makes the sales-tax “compliant product” test broader in practice than the excise-tax test (WAC 246-70 + testing to WAC 246-70-040). The two exemptions must not be conflated. [Source: https://dor.wa.gov/taxes-rates/taxes-due-cannabis — Accessed 2026-11-01; direct re-open failed — Verify, and obtain DOH MMJ 21-01]

A B&O exemption is also noted by DOR for cooperatives that grow, process and provide cannabis/low-THC products to their members. [Source: same DOR page — Accessed 2026-11-01 — Verify]

2.4.3 What medical patients still pay

  • A recognition-card patient buying a non-compliant product, or buying at a non-endorsed retailer, pays the 37% excise tax (and sales tax except where a separate low-THC/high-CBD exemption applies).
  • The excise exemption expires 30 June 2029 unless extended; the sales-tax exemption's sunset, if any, was not confirmed in this session — Verify RCW 82.08.9998's current text and sunset.

2.5 Distribution of excise revenue — the dedicated cannabis account (RCW 69.50.530 / 69.50.540)

2.5.1 The account

RCW 69.50.530 creates “the dedicated cannabis account … in the state treasury. All moneys received by the board … from marijuana-related activities must be deposited in the account,” including excise taxes under RCW 69.50.535 and licence fees, penalties and forfeitures; moneys “may only be spent after appropriation.” As amended by 2022 c 16 (SB 5796's companion terminology change) and 2022 c 169 restructuring. [Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2021-22/Htm/Bills/Senate%20Bills/5796-S2.E.htm (2022 bill text amending RCW 69.50.530/540) — Accessed 2026-11-01]

JLARC (November 2023, official audit body) found: state and local agencies spent $3.3 billion from the Dedicated Cannabis Account in FY2015–FY2023, “consistent with legislative direction,” split approximately: 54% health care for low-income children; 12% cannabis-related activities (8% prevention/education/treatment + 4% administration/enforcement, research, social equity); 34% general fund + local governments (30% general fund + ~3% local government, rounding). Thirteen state agencies, the general fund and 239 local governments received funds. The statutory formula in RCW 69.50.540 “has been modified eight times since I-502” (as of Nov 2023). [Source: https://storage.googleapis.com/co-gcs-replication/organizations/washington_state_joint_legislative_audit_and_review_committee/events/2023/2023_11_29-jlarc-committee_meeting/2023_11_29-jlarc-committee_meeting-appropriations_and_expenditures_of_the_dedicated_cannabis_account-overview-v1.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01, JLARC official summary]

Do not confuse JLARC's FY2015–23 spending shares with the current statutory formula percentages below — they measure different things over different periods.

2.5.2 Fixed appropriations — RCW 69.50.540(1) (2022 restructuring, amounts as returned)

The legislature “must appropriate the amounts provided in this subsection” (subsection (1)), including:

Recipient / purpose Amount as returned (2022 text) Source — Accessed 2026-11-01
LCB — administration of chapter 69.50 $12,500,000 annually, as appropriated in the omnibus appropriations act https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/washington/wa-code/washington_code_69-50-540
Department of Health — cannabis / vapor / commercial-tobacco education and public-health programme (hotline, youth prevention/cessation, media campaigns, priority-population outreach, poison control centre) $11,000,000 annually same
Department of Commerce — cannabis social-equity grants under RCW 43.330.540 $3,000,000 annually same; also 2022 bill text at https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2021-22/Htm/Bills/Senate%20Bills/5796-S2.E.htm
Department of Commerce — social-equity technical-assistance mentor roster under RCW 43.330.540 $200,000 annually (2022 bill text gives $163,000 FY2022 / $159,000 FY2023 in an earlier version — conflict, Verify current figure) same 2022 bill text
Health Care Authority — contract with Washington State Institute for Public Policy (WSIPP) for cost-benefit evaluations / reports under RCW 69.50.550 $200,000 annually (2022 text says $250,000 in one version — conflict, Verify), until 30 June 2032 same
University of Washington Alcohol & Drug Abuse Institute — web-based public-education materials $25,000 annually same

The 2022 bill also states subsections (1)(a)–(g) “must be adjusted annually based on the United States bureau of labor statistics' consumer price index for the Seattle area.” Therefore the dollar figures above are base figures, not necessarily the current-year appropriated amounts — Verify against the current RCW 69.50.540 text and the current omnibus appropriations act. [Source: https://wa-law.org/bill/2021-22/sb/5796/S2.SL/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]

2.5.3 Residual distribution — RCW 69.50.540(3) (percentages confirmed verbatim)

After the subsection (1) appropriations, the residual is distributed (subsection (3), as returned from the official RCW site via search):

  • (3)(c)(i) — 1.5% “to counties, cities, and towns where licensed cannabis retailers are physically located. Each jurisdiction must receive a share … based on the proportional share of the total revenues generated in the individual jurisdiction from the taxes collected under RCW 69.50.535, from licensed cannabis retailers physically located in each jurisdiction. … 100 percent of the proportional amount attributed to a retailer physically located in a city or town must be distributed to the city or town.”
  • (3)(c)(ii) — 3.5% “to counties, cities, and towns ratably on a per capita basis. Counties must receive 60 percent of the distribution based on each county's total proportional population. Funds may only be distributed to jurisdictions that do not prohibit the siting of any state licensed cannabis producer, processor, or retailer.”
  • (3)(c)(iii) — “By September 15th of each year, the board must provide the state treasurer the annual distribution amount … for each county and city as determined in (c)(i) and (ii).”
  • (3)(c)(iv) — amounts “must be distributed in four installments by the last day of each fiscal quarter.”
  • (3)(d) — 32% “must be deposited in the state general fund.”

[Source for all five bullets: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/rcw/?cite=69.50.540.d — Accessed 2026-11-01; corroborated at https://law.justia.com/codes/washington/title-69/chapter-69-50/section-69-50-540/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Important — what the 1.5% / 3.5% percentages are percentages of: they are shares of the residual amount governed by RCW 69.50.540(3), not 1.5% + 3.5% of gross excise collections. The balance of the residual (including the large health-care share) is governed by RCW 69.50.540(3)(a)–(b) (basic health plan trust account, community health centres, prevention/education/treatment programmes). The full (3)(a)–(b) percentages were not cleanly returned in this session — one 2022 proposal version showed 58% to the basic health plan trust account and 50% in an earlier version, which conflict and appear to be different bill versions. Verify the enacted (3)(a)–(b) percentages from the current official RCW 69.50.540 text before the parent publishes any “health gets X%” formula claim. Do not use JLARC's 54% spending share as a substitute for the formula.

Local-share mechanics and the ban penalty (see also Part 3): a jurisdiction that prohibits any of producer, processor or retailer siting loses the per-capita (3.5%) share entirely, even if it allows some licence types. It can still receive the retail-sales (1.5%) share only if a licensed retailer is physically located there — which, in a banning jurisdiction, will ordinarily be none. MRSC summarises: “Cities, towns, and counties that prohibit cannabis producers, processors, or retailers are not eligible for per capita distributions.” [Source: https://mrsc.org/explore-topics/business-regulation/types/cannabis-regulation — Accessed 2026-11-01] The State Treasurer distributes the per-capita and retail shares together in one payment (same BARS code), quarterly. [Source: same MRSC page — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Proposed, NOT enacted — SB 5547 (2025): a bill summary states SB 5547 would phase the retail-located share from 1.5% to 4% and the per-capita share from 3.5% to 6% (FY2026–FY2030), cutting the general-fund share from 32% to 27%. BillTrack50 lists SB 5547 as “Dead” after a 4 Feb 2025 Ways & Means hearing. Do not present the 4%/6%/27% figures as law; Verify final status on leg.wa.gov. [Sources: https://www.billtrack50.com/billdetail/1806316 — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary bill tracker]

Proposed, NOT enacted — HB 2731 (2026): would prohibit local jurisdictions from precluding licensed producers/processors/retailers (see Part 3.1). If enacted it would indirectly change who qualifies for per-capita shares. Status in this session: referred to committee 5 Feb 2026; Verify on leg.wa.gov — not law on 2026-11-01 on the evidence returned. [Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Biennium/2025-26/Pdf/Bills/House%20Bills/2731.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01]

A September 2026 local-news item claims “in 2025 over $10 million in cannabis tax remittances were allocated back to [cities with allowing zoning]” — that figure is inconsistent on its face with the ~$22.7m FY2024 total and is not adopted here; it is secondary, undated as to fiscal vs. calendar year, and likely partial. [Source of the claim, not adopted: https://lowervalleymatters.com/2026/10/18/granger-mayor-and-council-discuss-lifting-de-facto-zoning-moratorium-on-cannabis-businesses/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]

2.6 Hemp product tax — current law vs. proposals

  • No cannabis-style excise tax on hemp as such. WSDA/DOR guidance for industrial hemp (search-indexed WSDA/DOR Q&A, undated in the return, old — treat as background, Verify for current law):
  • A person who only grows hemp and wholesales it (to a reseller) is treated as a farmer; that income is “exempt from business and occupation (B&O) tax under RCW 82.04.330” and no DOR registration/business licence is required for grow-and-wholesale-only activity.
  • Processing hemp, then selling it, attracts B&O on processing value and on sale value, with a deduction for the lower of the two so B&O is paid once.
  • Retail sales of hemp attract retailing B&O and retail sales tax, except “industrial hemp products sold as food for humans are exempt from sales tax.” [Source: https://cms.agr.wa.gov/getmedia/27404788-445e-4f6e-9004-44eebbf0175f/DOR_FAQ.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01, WSDA/DOR Q&A — Verify currency; the document predates SB 5367 (2023) and the 2026 federal hemp changes]
  • Intoxicating hemp-derived products sold outside the LCB system historically paid no 37% excise tax — that gap is the policy premise of both Washington's SB 5367 (2023, bringing hemp-derived THC under LCB/DOH control — detail belongs to Part 1, not repeated here) and the federal hemp changes in the task baseline (PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019, 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates). No Washington hemp excise tax rate was confirmed as enacted law on 2026-11-01.
  • Proposed, NOT enacted — HB 2740 (2026): read first time 24 Feb 2026, referred to House Consumer Protection & Business. It would levy “a hemp-derived cannabinoid product tax equal to 10 percent of the selling price on each retail sale,” separate from sales tax, separately itemised, with 50% to the hemp regulatory account (RCW 15.140.080) and 50% to the general fund. This is a proposal only — Verify status on leg.wa.gov; do not present 10% as a current hemp tax. [Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Biennium/2025-26/Pdf/Bills/House%20Bills/2740.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  • CBD / non-intoxicating hemp retail: subject to ordinary sales tax and retailing B&O except where a food exemption applies (see above). No separate CBD tax was found — Verify against current DOR guidance.
  • Federal hemp dates and Washington response: no Washington statute, LCB rule or WSDA guidance responding specifically to the 12 November / 11 December 2026 federal dates was located in this session — Verify / follow-up for Part 1 and Part 7. Do not assume Washington has conformed.

2.7 IRS §280E and Washington's tax system — interaction, and what changed in 2026

Federal baseline given for this dossier (do not re-litigate): split scheduling — state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.

Washington structure — why §280E matters less to the state tax bill than in income-tax states, but still matters federally:

  1. Washington has no personal or corporate income tax on wages/profits. Its business tax is the gross-receipts B&O tax (§2.3), which allows no deductions for costs in any event. A cannabis business therefore does not lose a Washington income-tax deduction it never had. Secondary tax guides that say “Washington does not decouple from §280E, so cannabis businesses cannot deduct expenses for state tax purposes” are describing the B&O gross-receipts result, not a Washington income-tax conformity rule. [Source: https://cannabiscpa.tax/washington-cannabis-tax-guide-2025-edition/ — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary tax guide, July 2025 update — do not cite as law]
  2. Federal §280E still applies in full to adult-use activity (Schedule I): no federal deduction/credit for amounts paid/incurred in trafficking in Schedule I/II substances. That is the dominant §280E burden for Washington adult-use licensees, and it is unchanged by any Washington statute found in this session.
  3. Federal §280E for medical activity after 28 April 2026 — Verify (the central unsettled point for this dossier): whether a sale by an LCB retailer with a medical endorsement to a recognition-card patient counts as “state-licensed medical” activity for the Schedule III / §280E rule is not settled by the statute text returned in this session, for three reasons the parent must carry forward: - Washington merged medical sales into the LCB adult-use licensing system in 2016 (SB 5052); the same retailer licence sells both adult-use and medical product, distinguished by endorsement, recognition card and DOH-compliant product status — not by a separate medical licence. - The federal rule's text (91 FR 22714) as summarised in the task baseline speaks of “state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana”; whether endorsement sales meet that phrase, and whether a licensee must segregate medical vs. adult-use books to claim relief, was not confirmed from the federal text in this session. - The same Verify applies to tribal businesses under state compacts (Part 10): whether any tribal medical activity counts as “state-licensed” is Verify.
  4. New Washington statute — RCW 82A.04.350 (2026 c 238 s 313) — Verify scope before relying on it: the RCW site returns: “In computing a taxpayer's Washington taxable income, the taxpayer may deduct from the taxpayer's Washington base income the amount of expenditures disallowed pursuant to section 280E of the internal revenue code so long as the expenditures are related to the commercial cannabis activities by a person licensed pursuant to RCW 69.50.325.” Certified 15 July 2026. [Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=82A.04.350&pdf=true — Accessed 2026-11-01] - Do not present this as “Washington has decoupled from §280E for B&O.” Chapter 82A is a taxable-income chapter (the 2026 act that created it is a separate tax, described in bill materials as a new Washington tax on high incomes / capital gains — the exact tax and its effective date were not confirmed in this session). Whether RCW 82A.04.350 gives relief to ordinary cannabis retailers, to whom, and from what tax year, is Verify. It does not, on its face, amend RCW 82.04 (B&O). - The Q3 2026 CannBus report, which predates or does not address RCW 82A.04.350, still reports “no state-level 280E relief” for B&O — consistent with the B&O-only reading above, but stale as to chapter 82A. [Source: https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Accessed 2026-11-01]
  5. Practical consequence for the dossier: adult-use = fully subject to federal §280E (settled). Medical endorsement sales and tribal-compact medical sales = Verify for federal §280E relief. Washington B&O = gross receipts, no cost deductions regardless of §280E. Washington chapter 82A deduction = Verify scope/effective date.
