Trend that can be stated safely: excise collections peaked around FY2021 (~$555m) and have declined each year since to ~$438m in FY2025, on the secondary figures above. The FY2024/FY2025 $0.1–0.8m discrepancies are immaterial to the trend but must not be silently rounded away in the audit — the parent should pick the LCB FY2025 Annual Report figure as the authoritative FY2025 number once it is directly opened (Part 5/6 track), and list the other here as the conflict.

Unavailable: monthly excise collections for 2026, FY2026 total, and a clean excise-only vs. excise+sales split for every year — not retrieved in this session. DOR Tax Statistics and LCB annual reports are the follow-up sources (Part 5).

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

3.1 What authority counties and cities have

3.1.1 The state framework — RCW 69.50.331

Buffer (subsection (8), as summarised from the official RCW site and LCB guidance): the board may not issue a licence for premises within 1,000 feet of the perimeter of the grounds of: an elementary or secondary school, playground, recreation centre/facility, childcare centre, public park, public transit centre, library, or game arcade to which admission is not restricted to persons 21+. Distance is measured as the shortest straight line from the property line of the proposed premises to the property line of the restricted entity (WAC 314-55-050(2), as described in a June 2025 LCB board memo; the LCB was in June 2025 considering amending how that measurement is defined — Verify whether that amendment was adopted). [Sources: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/location-requirements (search-indexed LCB page — direct re-open failed, Verify); https://storage.googleapis.com/co-gcs-replication/organizations/washington_state_liquor_and_cannabis_board/events/2025/2025_06_04-wslcb-board_meeting/2025_06_04-wslcb-board_meeting-meeting_materials-buffer_zone_measurement-cr_101-v1.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Local reduction (subsection (8)(b)): “A city, county, or town may reduce the buffer distance to 100 feet, except for schools and playgrounds, by enacting an ordinance authorizing a reduction.” In other words: schools and playgrounds can never go below 1,000 feet by local action; every other listed entity can go as low as 100 feet if the local jurisdiction so ordains. Many cities chose an intermediate figure (500 feet is common — see §3.4). [Source: LCB board memo above — Accessed 2026-11-01; corroborated at https://mrsc.org/explore-topics/business-regulation/types/cannabis-regulation — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Residential/rural producer-processor ban (subsection (9)): “A city, town, or county may adopt an ordinance prohibiting a cannabis producer or cannabis processor from operating or locating a business within areas zoned primarily for residential use or rural use with a minimum lot size of five acres or smaller.” Note the limit: this express authority covers producers/processors, not retailers — retailer bans rest on general land-use/zoning authority (see §3.1.2), not on (9). [Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/washington/2022/title-69/chapter-69-50/section-69-50-331/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Local objection (subsection (10), per MRSC): cities, towns and counties “may also file objections to the granting of a state license at a particular location and the [LCB] must ‘give substantial weight to objections,’ but it is still up to the LCB to make the state license decision.” [Source: https://mrsc.org/explore-topics/business-regulation/types/cannabis-regulation — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Indian country: RCW 69.50.331(8)(f) (as returned) — the board “may not issue a license for any premises within Indian country … without the consent of the federally recognized tribe associated with the reservation or Indian country.” (Tribal detail belongs to Part 10.) [Source: same Justia RCW 69.50.331 page — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Other state constraints on local action: - LCB will not issue producer/processor/retailer licences on property used as a residence, because law-enforcement inspection access is required (WAC 314-55-015(5), per MRSC). [Source: MRSC page above — Accessed 2026-11-01] - A local jurisdiction cannot reduce the 37% state excise tax, create its own cannabis excise tax in conflict with state law, or authorise a use state law forbids (e.g. delivery — see Part 1; home grow for adults is prohibited statewide). Local business-licence taxes/fees are a separate matter and were not inventoried here — Verify city by city. - Proposed change — HB 2731 (2026) would prohibit any local ordinance/regulation/land-use plan “that has the effect of precluding the siting or operation of licensed cannabis producers, processors, or retailers” within a jurisdiction. Not law on the evidence returned (referred to committee 5 Feb 2026) — Verify on leg.wa.gov. If enacted, it would end local bans outright. [Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Biennium/2025-26/Pdf/Bills/House%20Bills/2731.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01]

3.1.2 Bans and moratoriums — legal basis

Washington local governments have general land-use authority, and the Attorney General (13 March 2018 statement, as reproduced in a state health-equity briefing) confirmed: “Jurisdictions can ban marijuana sales under Washington law.” MRSC's formulation: “Cities, towns, and counties in Washington State can choose to prohibit or to designate appropriate zones for state-licensed cannabis businesses because Washington local governments have authority to enact legislation regulating land uses within their jurisdictions. However, LCB has final authority over whether to grant or deny a state license.” [Sources: https://healthequity.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2022-01/Licensing%208_25%20Bans%20%26%20Moratoriums%20Briefing%20.pdf (briefing reproducing AG statement, LCB and MRSC material); https://mrsc.org/explore-topics/business-regulation/types/cannabis-regulation — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Practical forms a “ban” takes, which the parent must not conflate: 1. Express prohibition ordinance (no producer/processor/retailer in any zone). 2. Zoning omission — cannabis uses are simply not listed as permitted in any zone (functionally a ban without the word “ban”). Bellevue's early position was of this type (see §3.4). 3. Moratorium — time-limited hold on accepting/processing applications while permanent rules are drafted (commonly 6–12 months in 2013–14; some, e.g. Granger's 2015 moratorium, are reported as still operating de facto in 2026 — see §3.2). 4. Numerical cap below the state allocation (e.g. Everett 5, Renton 5, Vancouver 6 historically) — not a ban, but a local limit. 5. Restrictive zoning + buffers that leave no viable parcel — functionally exclusionary without a ban ordinance (the Spokane Valley 2026 debate is about this effect — see §3.4).

3.1.3 How local bans affect revenue sharing

See §2.5.3–2.5.4: a jurisdiction that prohibits any of producer, processor or retailer siting forfeits the 3.5% per-capita share. It does not forfeit the 1.5% retail-sales share in theory, but in practice a banning jurisdiction has no retailer physically located there to generate that share. The forfeiture is all-or-nothing on the per-capita share: allowing retailers but banning producers still forfeits it, on the statute's “any … producer, processor, or retailer” wording. [Source: RCW 69.50.540(3)(c)(ii) link in §2.5.3 — Accessed 2026-11-01]

This creates the incentive structure visible in local debates: Granger's September 2026 council discussion explicitly frames lifting its moratorium as a way to “generate tax revenues” and notes LCB outreach to banning cities; Lynnwood (2025) and Poulsbo are cited in the same report as recent ban-lifters. [Source: https://lowervalleymatters.com/2026/10/18/granger-mayor-and-council-discuss-lifting-de-facto-zoning-moratorium-on-cannabis-businesses/ — Accessed 2026-11-01, local news, Sept 2026 — secondary, for debate context only]

3.2 Which jurisdictions ban or allow — what can and cannot be stated on 2026-11-01

There is no current, authoritative, jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction ban list that could be verified in this session (see §3.3). The figures below are therefore presented as dated snapshots with their dates attached, not as a current list. The parent must not publish them as “current bans” without re-checking each jurisdiction's code.

Snapshot A — LCB work session, February 2025 (secondary reporting of LCB staff): LCB Deputy Director Nicola Reid told the Senate Labor & Commerce Committee that “approximately 80 total bans or moratoriums were active” statewide, and that LCB had surveyed local officials, stressing local governments “didn't have to cap the number of retailers in their jurisdiction anymore.” [Source: https://cannabis.observer/observations/97455-wa-senate-lc-committee-meeting-february-10-2025-wslcb-work-sessions/ — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary reporting of a 10 Feb 2025 hearing]

Snapshot B — 2026 secondary reporting: “Roughly 70 jurisdictions across the state maintain outright bans or moratoriums” (June 2026 TVW/Inside Olympia discussion reporting); “About 70 cities and 6 counties … don't have any cannabis operations and aren't eligible for cannabis tax remittances” (September 2026 local news). The drop from ~80 (Feb 2025) to ~70 (2026) is not confirmed as a real net change — the two counts may use different definitions (bans + moratoriums vs. outright bans; cities vs. cities + counties). Do not present a trend. [Sources: https://idahonews.co/washingtons-legal-cannabis-industry-squeezed-by-surplus-black-market-and-federal-limbo/ (June 2026); https://lowervalleymatters.com/2026/10/18/granger-mayor-and-council-discuss-lifting-de-facto-zoning-moratorium-on-cannabis-businesses/ (Sept 2026) — Accessed 2026-11-01, both secondary]

Snapshot C — LCB Social Equity report, December 2025 (official, but narrow): LCB's December 2025 Social Equity in Cannabis report (submitted under ESSB 5167 §147(7)) contains a table of social-equity applicants by county/city with a “City Zoning Type” column. The excerpts returned include: Kennewick — Prohibited; Kent — Prohibited; Lynden — Prohibited; Monroe — Prohibited; Walla Walla (city) — Permanent [in the report's zoning-type column, i.e. permanent zoning in place, not a ban label]; Seattle, Renton, Everett, Spokane, Olympia — Permanent; Bellingham — Interim; Lacey — Allowed; Ritzville, Republic, Prosser — Allowed Under Existing Zoning; Pacific — Prohibited (but with a successful location, 1 of 1 — showing the column does not mechanically determine outcomes). [Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-02/LCB%20Social%20Equity%20In%20Cannabis%20Review%20SB%205167%2C%20Section%20147%287%29_12172025_Final%20Revised.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01, official LCB report] - Caution: this table classifies zoning for social-equity applicants, as returned in garbled tabular form. “Permanent” is not defined in the excerpt as “allows all licence types,” and “Prohibited” in this table should be read as “the report codes that city as prohibiting (for the relevant licence type)” — the parent/auditor must read the full table and its definitions before converting any row into a ban claim. It is included here as the most recent official zoning snapshot found, not as a ban list.

Snapshot D — Historical (2013–14) implementation survey, for context only: a December 2013 / March 2014 survey of the 75 most populous Washington cities found, in Dec 2013: 22 zoned (29%), 36 moratorium (48%), remainder banned/no action; by March 2014, 33 zoned (44%). Cities coded MORATORIUM in Dec 2013 included Kent, Renton, Federal Way and Yakima; ZONED included Seattle, Everett, Bellingham and Olympia; Bellevue was coded ZONED by March 2014. These 2013–14 codes are historical only and must not be used for current status — most moratoriums have since been replaced by permanent zoning or by bans, in either direction. [Sources: https://cannabisandsocialpolicy.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/11/Local-I-502-Implementation-Press-Release4.pdf; https://cannabisandsocialpolicy.org/local-implementation-update-and-trend-report/ — Accessed 2026-11-01]

3.4 Local buffer and zoning rules — jurisdiction by jurisdiction

State default (applies wherever the locality has not reduced buffers): 1,000 feet from schools, playgrounds, recreation centres/facilities, childcare centres, public parks, public transit centres, libraries and 21+-restricted-exempt game arcades; reducible by local ordinance to 100 feet for all except schools/playgrounds (which stay at 1,000 feet). See §3.1.1. Rows below give the local variation only where a source was returned; “Verify” means the current code section was not confirmed in this session and must be checked in the locality's published code (Qcode / CivicWeb / Granicus) before publication.

Jurisdiction Zoning / buffer position returned Code / ordinance citation returned Status
Seattle (King County) Allows. “Major cannabis activity” regulated under SMC 23.42.058. Retail: 1,000 ft from elementary/secondary schools and playgrounds; 500 ft from childcare centres, game arcades, libraries, public parks, public transit centres, recreation centres/facilities (with exceptions); in Downtown Mixed Commercial / Downtown Mixed Residential zones north of Yesler Way, 250 ft from those non-school/playground uses; non-retail major activity 250 ft from all listed uses. Dispersal: no more than two retail major uses within 1,000 ft of each other (property-line measurement). SMC 23.42.058; Ord. 124969 (2016) Confirmed from City of Seattle SDCI summary. [Source: https://www.seattle.gov/construction-and-inspections/codes/codes-we-enforce-(a-z)/cannabis-code — Accessed 2026-11-01]
King County (unincorporated) Verify — no King County code section returned in this session. Do not infer Seattle's buffers apply countywide. — Verify

3.5 Retail licence allocation under the state cap — history, and why no current per-city table is given here

How allocation worked (WAC 314-55-081, as returned): “Using estimated consumption data and population data obtained from the office of financial management (OFM) population data, the [board] will determine the maximum number of marijuana retail locations per county. The number of retail locations will be determined using a method that distributes the number of locations proportionate to the most populous cities within each county. Locations not assigned to a specific city will be at large. At large locations can be used for unincorporated areas in the county or in cities within the county that have no retail licenses designated.” [Source: https://apps.yakimawa.gov/WebLink/DocView.aspx?id=212224&dbid=0&repo=CityOfYakima (Yakima memo quoting WAC 314-55-081) — Accessed 2026-11-01; corroborated at https://www.aclu-wa.org/app/uploads/2016/01/2015-04-13-Intervenor-Appellants-Reply-Br.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01]

Cap history (all dates matter; do not present any as current): - Initial LCB cap: 334 retail stores statewide, distributed by population. - December 2015 / January 2016: LCB raised the cap from 334 to 556 to accommodate the medical merger (SB 5052 / HB 2136), via emergency rules announced 6 Jan 2016; allocations to be published on lcb.wa.gov. [Sources: https://cms.tacoma.gov/Planning/Planning%20Commission/PC%20Agendas%202016/Agenda%20Packet%20(2-3-16).pdf; https://www.lakestevenswa.gov/ArchiveCenter/ViewFile/Item/2159 — Accessed 2026-11-01] - A Lake Stevens planning document states: “Subsequent to the City Council providing direction, the [LCB] removed its cap on local retail locations” — conflicts with the 556-cap account and with the Dec 2025 LCB report's continued use of allocations. The reconciliation (whether “removed its cap” meant removed the local per-city cap while keeping a statewide figure, or something else) was not resolved in this session. Verify the current WAC 314-55-081 text and the current LCB allocation publication before stating any current cap. [Source: Lake Stevens document above — Accessed 2026-11-01] - A secondary 2016 summary claimed “there is currently no limit on the number of retail stores that will be licensed” after the Oct 2015 application window (962 applications) — historical claim, not adopted as current law. [Source: https://app.brightfieldgroup.com/pages/washington-full-report — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary]

Historical per-jurisdiction allocation examples returned (historical only — Verify before reuse): - Bellevue: 4 retail licences initially allocated (2013–16 materials). - Yakima (city): 5 (May 2016 memo). The same memo's “Yakima County ‘At Large’ licenses: 61” alongside “Grandview 1, Selah 1, Sunnyside 1, Yakima 5, TOTAL: 14” is internally inconsistent (61 + 14 ≠ any stated county total) and is not adopted — it is flagged here so the auditor does not copy it. - Clark County (all cities + unincorporated): 15 total (2015 article). - Walla Walla (city): 3 (2017 agenda). - Pasco: allocation described in 2023 draft-ordinance reporting as supporting 3 stores (4 with a social-equity licensee). - Tukwila: 3 retail licences issued in the city (2025 council memo: “the LCB has issued three licenses for cannabis retailers within the City of Tukwila”). [Source: https://records.tukwilawa.gov/WebLink/1/edoc/426531/COW%202025-08-25%20Item%204A%20%205A%20-%20Public%20Hearing%20-%20Ordinance%20Updating%20Cannabis%20Use%20Regulations.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01] - Vancouver (city): 6 (2013 cap) — see §3.4 conflict with a later “12” figure. - Everett: 5 (local cap, not state allocation — see §3.4). Renton: 5 (local cap). Pullman: 3 originally, later “state limit currently five” (secondary, chronology Verify).

No current per-city allocation table is published in this report because (a) the live LCB allocation publication could not be opened and verified in this session, (b) the historical figures above conflict with each other and with the “cap removed” account, and (c) local caps (Everett, Renton, Vancouver) are distinct from state allocations and are easily conflated. Follow-up: obtain the current LCB retail-allocation list (lcb.wa.gov, per WAC 314-55-081's publication requirement) and the Dec 2025 Social Equity report's full allocation appendix, and reconcile them before Part 4 is finalised.

At-large licences — why they matter locally: an at-large licence “can be used for unincorporated areas in the county or in cities within the county that have no retail licenses designated” (WAC 314-55-081, as quoted above). A city that bans retail therefore does not necessarily strand an at-large licence — the licence can locate in unincorporated county or in a non-designated city — but it also cannot be forced into the banning city. The Fife/Pierce County litigation excerpt returned (ACLU-WA brief) illustrates the tension: LCB issued an at-large Pierce County licence usable in Fife, and Fife's ban was litigated as prohibiting what state law allows. The outcome of that litigation was not returned — Verify before citing Fife as precedent either way. [Source: https://www.aclu-wa.org/app/uploads/2016/01/2015-04-13-Intervenor-Appellants-Reply-Br.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01]

3.6 Business concentration — only what official sources say

No official concentration ranking, cluster map or “most stores per capita” table was located in this session, and none is offered here. In particular, this report does not rank Seattle, Spokane, Tacoma, Vancouver or any other city by store count or density, and does not estimate clusters — per the task instruction.

What official sources do say, in general terms: - JLARC (Nov 2023): Dedicated Cannabis Account funds reached 239 local governments in FY2015–23 — i.e. distribution was geographically broad, not confined to a handful of cities. [Source: JLARC summary link in §2.5.1 — Accessed 2026-11-01] - LCB (Feb 2025 work session, via secondary reporting): licensees operate “in over 200 cities and 33 counties” (per a Sept 2026 local-news summary of LCB material: “over 2,300 cannabis licensees … in over 200 cities and 33 counties”). [Source: https://lowervalleymatters.com/2026/10/18/granger-mayor-and-council-discuss-lifting-de-facto-zoning-moratorium-on-cannabis-businesses/ — Accessed 2026-11-01, secondary] - LCB Dec 2025 Social Equity report: social-equity applicants' ability to secure a location varied sharply by city (e.g. Seattle 1 of 7 successful in the excerpt; Prosser, Republic, Arlington 1 of 1) — but that table measures social-equity applicants' location success, not overall business concentration, and must not be repurposed as a concentration ranking. [Source: LCB Dec 2025 report link in §3.3 — Accessed 2026-11-01] - Follow-up for Part 5/6: the LCB licensee list by county/city (Part 5) is the correct source for any future concentration statement, and any such statement must be computed from that list with its date attached, not estimated here.

PART 4 — LICENCE TYPES

4.0 Regulator, application route, and the general-market closure — the headline that governs everything else

  • The Washington State Liquor and Cannabis Board (LCB) is the cannabis licensing regulator. Its licence-types page states, verbatim in substance: "LCB is currently not accepting new license applications for cannabis retailers, producers or processors except under the Social Equity Program." — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/license-types — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Applications and forms for cannabis licences are found through the Department of Revenue's Business Licensing Services (BLS) website and on the LCB portal. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/license-types — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • The same closure is repeated on the LCB "Apply for a Cannabis License" page. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/apply — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Consequence for a new entrant on 2026-11-01: there is no general public application window for a new producer, processor, or retailer licence. The only new-licence route is the Social Equity Program (Section 4.9), plus the always-open routes that are NOT producer/processor/retailer licences: transporter, research, and cooperative registration (see below, with their own caveats). The secondary-market route — buying / assuming an existing licence, subject to LCB approval — is the other route, but no official LCB price or marketplace is published; any price is a private negotiation (see Section 4.11).
  • A separate licence is required for each physical location. This is stated in the EHB 2681 bill report for producer, processor, and retailer licences. — Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2025-26/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/2681.E%20HBR%20PL%2026.htm — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • No delivery licence, no microbusiness licence, no standalone wholesale licence, no consumption-lounge licence: Washington has no separate wholesale licence (producers and processors sell wholesale), no delivery licence for delivery to consumers, and no microbusiness class. This is stated in secondary market intelligence summarising official sources, and is consistent with the five-licence list in RCW 69.50 (producer, processor, retailer, researcher, transporter) plus endorsements/registrations below. — Source: https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Snippet only — Verify against RCW 69.50.325 text before final publication, Accessed 2026-11-01. Do not present delivery, lounge, or microbusiness as a Washington licence type.
  • Bundling: Washington does not issue a bundled producer+processor+retailer licence. A business may hold both a producer and a processor licence (the common "producer-processor" combination in counts), but retail is separated from production/processing by the tied-house rule (Section 4.8). — Source: https://washingtonstatecannabis.org/licensing/producer — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.

Social Equity licence RCW 69.50.335; WAC 314-55-570; E2SHB 2870 (2020); E2SSB 5080 (2023) A retailer, producer, or processor licence issued through the Social Equity Program allocation, not a separate licence class $250 application (same as base type) — plus scoring/registration step Same $1,781 base fee, BUT annual fee waiver for social equity retail licences through 1 July 2032 per secondary summary — Verify current waiver text in RCW 69.50.335 Registration window CLOSED on 2026-11-01 (last window 2 June–1 July 2025)

4.2 Producer — tiers, privileges, fees, cap

  • Privileges: "A cannabis producer license allows the licensee to produce, harvest, trim, dry, cure, and package cannabis into lots for sale at wholesale to cannabis processor licensees and to other cannabis producer licensees" and to produce/sell plants, seed, and plant tissue culture to other producer licensees. — Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wAC/default.aspx?cite=314-55-075&pdf=true — Snippet only (full PDF fetch failed, see header note) — Verify verbatim text on re-open, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Canopy tiers (plant canopy, not total premises):
  • Tier 1 — less than 4,000 sq ft.
  • Tier 2 — 4,000 sq ft up to 10,000 sq ft.
  • Tier 3 — 10,000 sq ft up to 30,000 sq ft.
  • Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wAC/default.aspx?cite=314-55-075&pdf=true — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. The same tiers are repeated in multiple secondary summaries.
  • A licensee may not grow beyond the designated square footage approved by the LCB. — Source: https://washingtonstatecannabis.org/licensing/producer — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Fees: Application $250 (applicant also pays approved-vendor fingerprint evaluation fees). Annual issuance/renewal $1,781. — Source for $1,781: LCB EHB 2681 CR-103 memo, https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-07/EHB2681_CR103_Memo.pdf — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01; and WAC 314-55-075 snippet showing "$1,781". Prior fee was $1,381. The increase was made by EHB 2681 (2026), amending RCW 69.50.325, effective 11 June 2026 by statute, with WAC amendments filed 1 July 2026 effective immediately on filing. — Source: same memo. Do not use $1,381 as the current fee. Older secondary pages still showing $1,381 are stale.
  • CPI adjustment from 2027: Beginning 1 July 2027, the LCB must add the change in the Consumer Price Index (Seattle area, all urban consumers) since 1 July 2026 to the producer/processor/retailer annual fee. — Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2025-26/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/2681%20HBA%20APP%2026.htm — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Ownership cap: Any entity and/or principals within any entity are limited to no more than three cannabis producer licences. — Source: WAC 314-55-075 snippet via search result 2 in first batch — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. The same "three" limit is repeated for processors (see 4.3) and in the JLARC summary: "A single business may hold up to five retail licenses or three producer and processor licenses." — Source: https://leg.wa.gov/jlarc/reports/2025/CannabisMarket/pf_ii/default.html — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Producer cap (statewide number): There is NO published statewide numerical cap on producer licences comparable to the retail cap. The constraint is the closed application window (general market) plus the Social Equity allocation (10 producer licences, each in conjunction with a processor licence — see 4.9). Any statement of a "producer cap" as a fixed number would be a guess — do not state one.

4.3 Processor — privileges, fees, edibles endorsement

  • Privileges: "A cannabis processor license allows the licensee to process, dry, cure, package, and label useable cannabis, cannabis concentrates, and cannabis-infused products for sale at wholesale to cannabis processors and cannabis retailers." — Source: WAC 314-55-077 via https://www.casemine.com/regulation/us/64674b3c1d923c422d6ac2f3 — Snippet only (secondary republication of WAC text) — Verify against app.leg.wa.gov WAC 314-55-077, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Processors may not sell directly to the general public. Single class; no sub-tiers by extraction method. — Source: https://www.flourishsoftware.com/states/washington — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Fees: Application $250 + fingerprint vendor fees. Annual issuance/renewal $1,781 from 1 July 2026 (was $1,381). — Source: LCB EHB 2681 memo, https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-07/EHB2681_CR103_Memo.pdf — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. Note: one WAC republication still shows $1,381 — that is the pre-July-2026 text and is stale.
  • Application window: "The application window for cannabis processor licenses is closed. The board may reopen the cannabis processor application window at subsequent times when the board deems necessary." — Source: WAC 314-55-077 snippet — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. Consistent with the LCB licence-types page closure notice.
  • Ownership cap: No more than three cannabis processor licences per entity/principals. — Source: WAC 314-55-077 snippet — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Cannabis-infused edibles endorsement (WSDA, not LCB alone): A processor that makes orally ingested solid or liquid product (edibles) must obtain a cannabis-infused edible endorsement from the Department of Agriculture under RCW ch. 15.125 and WAC ch. 16-131. — Source: WAC 314-55-077 snippet — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. The $895 figure for this endorsement appears ONLY in a secondary guide (CannaCon) and is Verify — do not present $895 as an official fee without checking the WSDA/DOR fee schedule. — Source for the $895 claim: https://cannacon.org/cannabis-license-washington/ — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Inventory limit (context, not a licence count): Processors are limited to six months of average usable cannabis and six months of average total production on premises — secondary summary only. — Source: https://www.flourishsoftware.com/states/washington — Snippet only — Verify in WAC 314-55-077, Accessed 2026-11-01.

4.4 Retailer — privileges, fees, cap and allocation, medical endorsement

  • Privileges: "A cannabis retailer license allows the licensee to sell only useable cannabis, cannabis concentrates, cannabis-infused products, cannabis paraphernalia, and lockable boxes to store cannabis at retail in licensed retail outlets to persons 21 years of age and older" (plus, at medically endorsed stores, to registered patients/designated providers). — Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/WAc/default.aspx?cite=314-55-079&pdf=true — Snippet only — Verify verbatim on re-open, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Fees: Application $250 + fingerprint vendor fees. Annual issuance/renewal $1,781 from 1 July 2026 (was $1,381). — Source: WAC 314-55-079 snippet and LCB EHB 2681 memo https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-07/EHB2681_CR103_Memo.pdf — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • No consumer delivery, no internet sales: "Internet sales and delivery of product to customers is prohibited." WAC 314-55-079(5). A retailer may transport product to other locations operated by the same licensee or return product to a processor, as outlined in WAC 314-55-085. — Source: WAC 314-55-079 snippet — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Retail cap — history and current position (read carefully; "556" alone is now incomplete):
  • Original LCB allocation: 334 retail outlets statewide, set taking into account population, security/safety, and discouraging illegal markets. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/mj2015/fact-sheet — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • In 2015 the Board increased the cap from 334 to 556 (an additional 222) to ensure access for medical patients after the SB 5052 merger. — Source: Court of Appeals brief summarising the record, https://www.courts.wa.gov/content/Briefs/a02/555760-Appellant's Brief.pdf — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • The 556 figure was described by the Board itself as a maximum it was able to safely license, NOT a minimum, and no law required all 556 to be issued at any given time. — Source: same brief — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Social Equity additions on top of 556: Under E2SSB 5080 (2023), in addition to forfeited/revoked/cancelled/not-previously-issued licences that could have been issued within the pre-2020 limit, the LCB may issue up to 52 additional retail licences for the Social Equity Program between 1 January 2024 and 1 July 2032, AND, beginning 1 January 2023 and every three years until 1 July 2032, may — with legislative approval by bill — increase the number of retail (and producer) licences for the programme based on census / Office of Financial Management population estimates. — Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2023-24/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/5080-S2.E%20HBA%20RSG%2023.htm — Snippet only — Verify in RCW 69.50.335, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Allocation method: Retail licences were originally allocated by county and by most-populous city within each county, to accommodate medical needs — WAC 314-55-079 snippet references allocation "to the most populous cities within each county and to accommodate the medical needs of qualifying patients". The county-by-county allocation table itself is a Part 3 matter and was not re-verified in this Part 4–5 task; do not reproduce a county allocation table from memory. The current Social Equity allocation is NOT tied to the old county allocation in the same way (see 4.9 mobility rule).
  • Operating count vs cap: DOH reported 459 licensed retail stores in July 2026 (see Part 5 reconciliation). That is below 556, but the gap does NOT mean 97 licences are "available" to apply for — the general window is closed and unissued/forfeited licences are channelled to Social Equity. Verify the exact number of unissued/forfeited licences held in reserve on 2026-11-01; LCB's 2023 statement that "approximately 46 cannabis retail licenses" were held in reserve is dated May 2023 and is stale for 2026. — Source for 46 figure: https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/WALCB/bulletins/3583085 — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Ownership cap — retailer (5) and the 2026 clarification:
  • No individual/entity (with all persons/entities holding a financial or ownership interest counted together) may hold an interest in more than five retail licences. — Source: LCB bulletin on ESSB 5403, https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-02/5403-Education-1-22-26.pdf — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01; also EHB 2681 bill report summary.
  • ESSB 5403, effective 1 January 2026, clarifies what counts as a "financial or other ownership interest" (RCW 69.50.325) that triggers the five-licence limit, including: sharing of profits/revenue; assistance/coordination/recommendation for purchase where pricing is coordinated or discounted; common use of intellectual property (branding, trade names, logos, social media, websites); operational control or day-to-day operational support; coordinated sharing of employment/hiring decisions including shared employment. Existing agreements that did not meet the clarified requirements were not allowed to continue past 1 January 2026. LCB rulemaking to align rules was in progress at the time of the bulletin. — Source: same ESSB 5403 bulletin — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Tied-house / cross-ownership: A person/entity may not be a true party of interest in both a retail licence and a producer or processor licence. This is the rule that separates retail from production/processing. — Source: https://cannacon.org/cannabis-license-washington/ — Snippet only — Verify in RCW 69.50.325 / WAC 314-55-035, Accessed 2026-11-01. Note: the same person may hold producer + processor licences (subject to the three-each caps).
  • Medical endorsement (retailer):
  • What it is: an endorsement on a retailer licence (not a separate licence) that allows the store to sell DOH-compliant product to patients/designated providers holding recognition cards and to employ a certified medical cannabis consultant.
  • Counts (July 2026, DOH): 321 medically endorsed stores out of 459 licensed; 258 of those had issued a card in the last quarter; 699 certified consultants with an active credential. — Source: https://doh.wa.gov/you-and-your-family/cannabis/medical-cannabis — Snippet only — Verify (DOH page updated July 2026), Accessed 2026-11-01. See Part 5 for the conflicting January 2026 snapshot (302/460) and reconciliation.
  • Fee: secondary source lists "Medical Cannabis: $0" for the endorsement application. — Source: https://cannacon.org/cannabis-license-washington/ — Snippet only — Verify on the LCB fee schedule before publishing as $0, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • LCB medically endorsed stores list is updated every Tuesday but "may not be completely up to date" — LCB's own warning. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/records/frequently-requested-lists — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.

4.5 Transporter

  • Privileges: "A cannabis transporter license allows the licensee to transport or deliver cannabis products between licensed cannabis businesses within Washington State." — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/license-types — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • WAC 314-55-310 formulation: "A transportation license allows the licensee to physically transport or deliver cannabis, cannabis concentrates, and cannabis-infused products between licensed cannabis businesses within Washington state." — Source: https://apps.leg.wa.gov/Wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-310 — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Fees: Application $250. — Source: LCB licence-types page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. Annual fee $1,300 per WAC 314-55-310 snippet ("The application fee for the transportation license is $250 and the annual fee is $1,300"). — Source: same WAC snippet — Snippet only — Verify current figure: EHB 2681 raised only producer/processor/retailer fees, so $1,300 may still be current, but it was not confirmed on an opened LCB fee page on 2026-11-01. Do not assume it was raised to $1,781.
  • Requirements (LCB licence-types page, opened): licensed facilities must be 1,000 feet from restricted entities; business may not be located inside another business (including cannabis) or a residence; applicant must submit a copy of an approved Common Carrier permit from the Utilities and Transportation Commission (UTC); all vehicles/trailers must also be UTC-permitted as common carriers; proof of commercial general liability and product liability insurance; proof of right to the vehicle (title, registration, lease); final inspection and briefing by LCB; vehicle additions via Cannabis Alteration Request on the LCB portal; vehicles must travel directly from shipping to receiving licensee, no unnecessary stops except to other receiving facilities; pickup and delivery within 48 hours. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/license-types — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • No consumer delivery: transporter authority is business-to-business only. Consumer delivery remains prohibited (see 4.4).

4.6 Research licence

  • Privileges: "A cannabis research endorsement allows the licensee to produce, process, and possess cannabis for the limited research purposes provided in RCW 69.50.372." — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/license-types — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. The LCB page uses both "research license" and "research endorsement" wording; RCW 69.50.372 is the controlling citation. Related statutes cited on the same page: RCW 28B.20.502, RCW 69.50.369, RCW 69.50.372; rule WAC 314-55-073. — Source: same page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Application: $250 fee; submitted through BLS; applicant then receives an LCB letter directing them to submit additional materials directly to the designated scientific reviewer; if the reviewer does not approve, the application is withdrawn with no refund. Labs certified by LCB for quality-assurance testing may apply for a research licence. — Source: same page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Facility separation: Research facilities must be completely separate and distinct from other cannabis businesses, except actively licensed producer/processor licensees, where a shared entrance/exit is allowed but all cannabis grown/possessed must be for research purposes. — Source: same page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Annual fee: Verify / Unavailable — not stated on the opened LCB licence-types page excerpt. A secondary guide's former figures are removed; current RCW 69.50.372(7) is $250 application / $1,300 annual, and the $250 application fee on the official page is consistent with itks stale/pre-2026; do not use it. Mark research annual fee as Verify.
  • University link: RCW 28B.20.502 (University of Washington) is cited on the LCB page, but the page excerpt does not explain its scope. University research licences are a Part 11 matter; do not expand here beyond noting the citation.

4.7 Laboratory certification

  • Not a sales licence. A laboratory does not hold a producer/processor/retailer licence; it is certified by the LCB to conduct quality-assurance testing, after WSDA accreditation.
  • Rule: "To become certified, a third-party laboratory must meet the board's certification requirements as described in WAC 314-55-0995 and this chapter before conducting quality control tests required under this section. Cannabis licensees must use a laboratory certified by the board to conduct quality control testing required under this chapter. Prior to becoming certified, laboratories must be accredited by the WSDA as specified in chapter 16-310 WAC." — Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-102-html&pdf=true — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Required fields: water activity; cannabinoid concentration analysis; foreign matter inspection; microbiological testing; mycotoxin testing; pesticide testing; residual solvent testing. Heavy-metal testing and terpene analysis are additional certifications a lab may hold. — Source: same WAC snippet — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Count: Secondary summary citing the LCB FY2025 Annual Report says 6 labs certified in FY2025. — Source: https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Snippet only — Verify against the LCB Approved Testing Labs list dated 29 Oct 2026 (see Part 5), Accessed 2026-11-01. The LCB list itself is the authoritative count; the "6" was not verified by counting that list on 2026-11-01.
  • Fees: Verify / Unavailable — no lab certification fee was found on an opened official page.

4.8 Residency, true parties of interest, transfers, and other general requirements — with explicit Verify flags

  • Historical residency rule (stated in older statute summaries, now Verify for current text): Older RCW 69.50.331 summaries state: sole proprietor must have lawfully resided in Washington for at least six months prior to applying; a business/nonprofit entity must be formed under Washington law or hold a certificate of registration under RCW ch. 23.95, with 75% of interest holders meeting the residency requirement and all non-resident interest holders being US citizens. — Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/committeeschedules/Home/Document/138515 (2018 amendment summary) — Snippet only — Verify current RCW 69.50.331 text: secondary summaries conflict (some say "all principals must be Washington residents for six months", others say the 75% rule). Do NOT publish a single residency sentence as settled without re-opening current RCW 69.50.331.
  • Age: No licence of any kind may be issued to a person under 21. — Source: same 2018 summary — Snippet only — Verify in current RCW 69.50.331, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • True party of interest / financial interest: The five-retailer and three-producer/processor limits count all persons/entities holding a financial or ownership interest together (see 4.4 ESSB 5403 clarification, eff. 1 Jan 2026). The detailed "true party of interest" definition is in WAC 314-55-035 — Verify current text; it was not opened on 2026-11-01.
  • Transfers / changes (general, official route): LCB forms page lists Change Applications via the LCB portal, including: Cannabis Alteration Request (including expanding plant canopy), Reinstate Retail Cannabis License from Title Certificate, Change of Location, Change in Governing People or Percentage, and Splitting a Producer and Processor License. BLS applications (with cannabis addendum) cover retailer, producer, processor, transportation, research, change of location, change in governing people/percentage. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-forms-and-applications — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. A secondary guide lists a $75 fee for Change in Owners/Officers/Location — Verify; do not present as official without the LCB fee schedule. — Source: https://cannacon.org/cannabis-license-washington/ — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Title Certificates: A retailer licence that is not operating may be held as a Title Certificate and later reinstated (including through the Social Equity Program for eligible holders — see 4.9 mobility exclusion for Title Certificate Holders). The reinstatement route exists on the LCB forms page; its deadlines and fees are Verify.
  • Insurance: Transporters must show commercial general liability + product liability insurance (4.5). A secondary guide states retailers must show at least $1 million commercial general liability insurance. — Source: https://washingtonstatecannabis.org/business — Snippet only — Verify in WAC 314-55-079 / LCB application checklist; do not present $1m as confirmed for retailers without that check, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Entity registration prerequisite: An LLC/corporation must be registered with the Secretary of State and include its Unified Business Identifier (UBI) in the application; LCB will not issue to an unregistered entity. — Source: https://washingtonstatecannabis.org/business — Snippet only — Verify on LCB Apply page, Accessed 2026-11-01. See Part 5 for SOS/DOR search routes.

4.9 Social Equity in Cannabis Program — eligibility, windows, scoring, numbers, mobility, grants, Task Force

Legal lineage (LCB history page, opened): - 2020 — E2SHB 2870 (cited on LCB history page as "Senate Bill 2870"; the bill is House Bill 2870): authorised new retail applications with available retail licences going to Social Equity applicants; any additional retail licence returned until July 2028 to go to a social equity applicant; formed the Social Equity Task Force (work/recommendations completed December 2022); defined some social equity requirements; established a grant fund of $1,100,000 per year to the Department of Commerce for the social equity grant programme. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/history — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. - 2023 — E2SSB 5080: amended 2870; in addition to more social equity retail licences, producer and processor licences made available to Social Equity applicants. — Source: same page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Programme expiry: extended from 1 July 2029 to 1 July 2032 by E2SSB 5080. — Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2023-24/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/5080-S2.E%20HBA%20RSG%2023.htm — Snippet only — Verify in RCW 69.50.335, Accessed 2026-11-01. A 2025 bill (ESHB 1551) that would have extended expiry to 2034 and required an evaluation/report by 1 Dec 2025 was NOT treated as enacted in this report — its status on leg.wa.gov is Verify before any Part 1/Part 9 use; do not cite 2034 as current law. - Final SB 5080 rules (WAC 314-55-570, implementing RCW 69.50.335) went into effect 18 January 2025. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/history — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.

Eligibility — the "51% + 2 of 4" test (current SB 5080 definition): A "social equity applicant" is an applicant with at least 51% ownership AND control by one or more individuals who meet at least TWO of: 1. Lived in a disproportionately impacted area (DIA) in Washington for a minimum of five years between 1980 and 2010; 2. Has been arrested or convicted of a cannabis offence, or has a family member who has been arrested or convicted of a cannabis offence; 3. Had household income in the year prior to submitting an application that was less than the median household income within Washington as calculated by the US Census Bureau; 4. Is both a socially and economically disadvantaged individual as defined by the Office of Minority and Women's Business Enterprises (OMWBE) under RCW ch. 39.19. — Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2023-24/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/5080-S2.E%20HBA%20RSG%2023.htm — Snippet only — Verify in RCW 69.50.335 / WAC 314-55-570, Accessed 2026-11-01. - DIA definition (SB 5080 summary): a census tract or comparable geographic area with high rates of poverty, participation in income-based federal/state programmes, unemployment, and arrest/conviction/incarceration related to cannabis sale/possession/use/cultivation/manufacture/transport. LCB may further define DIA in rule after consultation with the Commission on African American Affairs and others. — Source: same bill report — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. LCB provides a Disproportionately Impacted Area Lookup Tool (existence reported in secondary summary; tool URL Verify — not opened 2026-11-01). - Older 2870 definition differs (5 of preceding 10 years in a DIA, OR misdemeanour cannabis conviction / family member). Do not mix the 2870 and 5080 definitions. The 5080 definition above is the one to use for the 2025 round.

Allocations authorised under SB 5080: - Up to 100 processor licences may be issued immediately (from forfeited/revoked/cancelled licences). - Beginning 1 January 2025, up to 10 producer licences may be issued, which must be issued in conjunction with a processor licence (i.e. producer-processor pairs, not standalone producer licences in this track). - Beginning 1 January 2024 until 1 July 2032, up to 52 retail licences in addition to forfeited/revoked/cancelled/not-previously-issued licences. - Triennial increase mechanism (see 4.4) subject to legislative approval by bill. — Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2023-24/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/5080-S2.E%20HBA%20RSG%2023.htm — Snippet only — Verify in RCW 69.50.335, Accessed 2026-11-01. - LCB May 2023 statement: "approximately 46 cannabis retail licenses" held in reserve to be awarded that year to eligible applicants (pre-5080-expansion figure). — Source: https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/WALCB/bulletins/3583085 — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01. Stale for 2026 — do not use as the 2026 reserve.

Application windows and scoring: - 2023 round (HB 2870): applications opened 1 March 2023 for 30 days; emergency rule extended the window to 27 April 2023. — Source: https://cannaspire.com/how-to-open-a-dispensary-in-washington/ — Snippet only — Verify on LCB SE pages, Accessed 2026-11-01. - 2025 round (SB 5080): retail registration window opened 2 June 2025 and closed 1 July 2025 (30 days, closing at midnight 1 July per secondary summary). — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/history — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01; closing-time detail from https://sativauniversity.com/cannabis-laws/washington/ — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Status on 2026-11-01: CLOSED. No next window had been announced as of the September/October 2026 secondary checks. — Source: https://sativauniversity.com/cannabis-laws/washington/ and https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Snippet only — Verify on https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/license-types and LCB SE pages before final publication, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Scoring, not lottery: Applicants in the 2025 round were scored against a rubric, not drawn in a lottery. LCB must select a third-party contractor to identify and score social equity applicants using a scoring rubric developed by LCB, and must rely on the contractor's score in issuing licences. LCB may deny if, on the contractor's advice, the application does not meet social equity licensing requirements or general licensing requirements. — Source for third-party scoring rule: SB 5080 bill report (above) — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. Scorers named on the LCB Frequently Requested Lists page: Ponder Diversity Group (lowest awarded score by county; score by UBI) and Alta Point Consulting (score by case number). — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/records/frequently-requested-lists — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Detailed rubric points: Verify. An older Task Force recommended rubric (650 total points, with race-weighted eligibility points) circulates as a PDF, but it is a 2022 recommendation, NOT the 2025 SB 5080 rubric. — Source for old rubric: https://healthequity.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2022-01/Scoring Rubric modified - community.pdf — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01. Do not reproduce the old rubric points as the current scoring system. The current rubric's point weights were not confirmed from an opened LCB page on 2026-11-01. - Social Equity Plan: Applicants submit a Social Equity Plan with application materials (qualifications, how issuance advances social equity goals, personal/family criminal-justice history, workforce composition, neighbourhood characteristics, business plans/partnerships). LCB may give priority based on the extent the application addresses plan components. — Source: 2021 bill report summary of the programme, https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2021-22/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/1443-S.E%20HBR%20PL%2021.htm — Snippet only — Verify current plan requirements in WAC 314-55-570, Accessed 2026-11-01.

How many issued, how many operating — reconciled, with conflicts stated plainly: - 69 registrants received notification they were "selected" for the Social Equity Program as of 20 October 2025 (2025 SB 5080 retail round). "Selected" ≠ licensed ≠ open. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/history — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Older round: 40 applicants received a preliminary letter of approval under E2SHB 2870, plus 5 retail licence holders with Title Certificates eligible to pursue licensing under the programme. — Source: LCB December 2025 Social Equity review report, https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-02/LCB%20Social%20Equity%20In%20Cannabis%20Review%20SB%205167%2C%20Section%20147%287%29_12172025_Final%20Revised.pdf — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Same December 2025 report (data as of September 2025): about 25% (11) of all eligible social equity licences had secured a location and 16% (7) had reported sales data to LCB in Q1 2025. — Source: same report — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. - A secondary summary citing February 2025 says "9 retail licenses issued" under SB 5080. — Source: https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Snippet only — Conflict/Unreconciled: 9 issued (Feb 2025) vs 69 selected (Oct 2025) vs 11 secured location / 7 reporting sales (Sept 2025) measure different stages (issued vs selected vs located vs trading) and different rounds. Do not collapse them into a single "X social equity stores open" figure. The number of SB 5080 social equity stores actually open and trading on 2026-11-01 is Unavailable from the sources opened; it requires counting the LCB License Applicants list filtered to social equity licences with sales activity, or an LCB statement dated after October 2025. - Fee waiver: Secondary summary states the annual issuance/re-issuance/renewal fee for a social equity retail licence is waived through 1 July 2032. — Source: https://washingtonstatecannabis.org/business — Snippet only — Verify in RCW 69.50.335 before publishing, Accessed 2026-11-01. Separately, non-social-equity licensees who submit a social equity plan may receive a one-time reimbursement of one annual renewal fee (SB 5080 summary / SB 6195 text) — Verify current status and whether this reimbursement is still funded. - Relocation / mobility (two different rules — do not conflate): - SB 5080 licences: "SB 5080 allots to move anywhere in WA as long as approved by County, City, or town (RCW 69.50.335(1)(e)(i))." — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/se-cannabis-faq — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. Local zoning and outlet-density control is retained; "anywhere" still requires local approval and buffer compliance. - HB 2870 licences: Effective 1 January 2026, a 2870 social equity applicant may change the initial business location from the allocated local jurisdiction IF (i) the licensee has not secured a location in the initial county, AND (ii) relocation into a different county requires a score exceeding the lowest score awarded for successful applicants in the desired county (lowest scores posted online by county). Title Certificate Holders are NOT eligible for this mobility option (they qualified without scoring). — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/se-cannabis-faq and https://lcb.wa.gov/content/2870-applicant-resources — Snippet only — Verify in WAC 314-55-570(8)(d), Accessed 2026-11-01. - General (non-social-equity) relocation is by Change of Location application via BLS (see 4.8), subject to buffer, zoning, and local objection rules — detail is Part 11. - Technical assistance grants (Department of Commerce, NOT LCB): - Programme: Cannabis Social Equity Technical Assistance Grant Program (CSEP), administered by the Department of Commerce under RCW 43.330.540. Grants are reimbursement-based, plus technical assistance and mentorship. From FY2027 the programme uses a five-phase milestone approach; Phase 1 is technical assistance only (no grant reimbursement); Phases 2–5 (location identification to letter of intent; through operating/close-out) may apply for reimbursement when costs are tied to the approved SEA licence, approved retail location, milestone phase, and eligible business purpose. Commerce retains final authority over eligibility, award amounts, reimbursement approvals, and payment decisions. — Source: Commerce CSEP page via https://mystartup365.com/cannabis-social-equity-program/ — Snippet only (mirror of Commerce content) — Verify on the official Commerce / Startup Washington CSEP page before publishing, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Funding figures (conflicting scopes — state the scope, do not add them): (a) LCB history page: $1,100,000 per year to Commerce (2020 law). (b) Task Force initial plan FY2024–25: $6m state + $3m reinvestment = $9.0m (secondary summary). (c) Commerce RFP CSETAGP-2-2026: administration contract period 13 May 2026–30 June 2027, budgeted not to exceed $3,200,000 total, of which $2,880,000 for grant distribution and $320,000 maximum for administrative overhead; proposals due 10 April 2026. — Sources: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/history (opened); https://deptofcommerce.box.com/shared/static/joun73woe6fdra350occ7iwiep5azhu8.pdf (RFP) — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01. These are not three totals for the same pot. Individual award range "up to $320,000" in one secondary summary appears to confuse the $320,000 admin cap with individual awards — Verify; do not state an individual grant cap. - Eligible grantees (SB 5080 text): social equity applicants submitting social equity plans, and licensees holding a licence issued after 1 April 2023 and before 1 July 2024 who meet social equity criteria. Grants awarded primarily on strength of social equity plans. — Source: SB 5080 bill report — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01. - Social Equity in Cannabis Task Force role: Created by E2SHB 2870 (2020) to make recommendations to LCB on establishing a programme for issuance/reissuance of retail licences and related matters; completed its work and recommendations in December 2022. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/history — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01; and https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2021-22/Htm/Bill%20Reports/House/1443-S.E%20HBR%20PL%2021.htm — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01. The Task Force is NOT the licensing decision-maker and is NOT the scorer; LCB issues licences on the third-party contractor's score. Its December 2025 successor evaluation is the LCB report under E2SSB 5167 §147(7) (see above), not a Task Force report — do not misattribute it. - Office of Equity: Listed in the parent task as a regulator to cover in Part 9; it has no licence-issuing role in Part 4. No Office of Equity licence function was found. Verify if Part 9 claims otherwise.

4.10 Cooperative registration (not a commercial licence)

  • Up to 4 qualifying patients or designated providers may form a cooperative and share responsibility for acquiring/supplying resources to produce/process cannabis ONLY for members' medical use. All members must hold valid recognition cards and be at least 21 (a designated provider for a patient under 21 may be a member on that patient's behalf). All plants must come from immature plants/clones purchased from a licensed producer; seeds may also be purchased from a licensed producer. — Source: RCW 69.51A.250 summary via https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/washington/wa-code/washington_code_69-51a-250 — Snippet only — Verify in current RCW text, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Registration: location must be registered with LCB; that is the only location where members may grow/process. Registration includes names of all members and copies of each recognition card. Only members registered with LCB for that location may participate or receive product. — Source: same — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Location bans: no cooperative within one mile of a cannabis retailer, and other location restrictions in RCW 69.51A.250(3) / WAC 314-55-410 (including 1,000-metre / school-type buffers as summarised in secondary sources — Verify exact distances; secondary summaries give "1 mile from retailer" and "1000 meters" from schools etc. in different units and may conflate provisions). One cooperative per property tax parcel; must be at a member's domicile. — Sources: same RCW summary + https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Law/WAC/WAC%20314%20%20TITLE/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20%20CHAPTER/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20-410.htm — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Plant/possession cap: up to the total authorised per member, maximum 60 plants at the location; possess up to 72 ounces of usable cannabis produced there. Members may belong to only one cooperative and may grow only at the cooperative (not also at home). No sale/donation to non-members. — Source: same — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • DOH patient page confirms: cooperatives allow up to 4 patients/designated providers, every member must be in the Medical Cannabis Registry, RCW 69.51A.250; members must be 21+, hold a valid recognition card, grow up to authorised total max 60, belong to one cooperative. — Source: https://doh.wa.gov/you-and-your-family/cannabis/medical-cannabis/patient-information — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Fee: Verify. A secondary guide states "There is no fee prescribed for registering a cooperative" — Source: https://washingtonstatecannabis.org/licensing/cooperative — Snippet only — Verify on the LCB cooperative registration form, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Cooperative count: Unavailable — no published count of active cooperatives was found on opened pages. LCB "will contact the primary contact for each registered cooperative on an annual basis" (WAC 314-55-410 summary), but no public cooperative list was located in Part 5. Do not estimate.

4.11 What does NOT exist, transfers on the secondary market, and estimated startup costs

  • Does not exist as a Washington licence type (2026-11-01): standalone delivery licence; consumption lounge licence; microbusiness licence; standalone wholesale/distribution licence; separate medical dispensary licence (medical access is via retailer endorsement + cooperative registration, not a separate store licence); separate hemp-THC licence under LCB (hemp is WSDA, see Part 7 in Run 2 — do not invent an LCB hemp licence here).
  • Secondary-market transfers: The only way for a non-social-equity entrant to obtain a producer/processor/retailer licence on 2026-11-01 is to assume/buy an existing licence, subject to LCB approval via BLS + cannabis addendum, change-in-governing-persons / assumption application, and LCB vetting (fingerprinting, true-party, residency, buffer, local notice). — Sources: LCB forms page (above) + secondary guides — Snippet only — Verify procedural steps on https://lcb.wa.gov/cannabis-license/apply, Accessed 2026-11-01. One live broker listing (Oct 2026) asked $350,000 for a non-operational, relocatable social equity retail licence (50% down, NDA required). — Source: https://www.biztrader.com/listing/rare-washington-social-equity-retail-license-relocatable-cannabis-retail-opportunity-washington-usa-2051/ — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01. This is a single asking price, NOT a market price and NOT an official valuation. Do not present $350,000 as "the cost of a licence." Social equity licence transferability itself is contested (see the Sept 2025 rulemaking petition asking for an unrestricted right to sell/transfer social equity licences — Source: https://storage.googleapis.com/co-gcs-replication/organizations/washington_state_liquor_and_cannabis_board/regulations/rulemaking/2025/2025_09_29-wslcb-social_equity_program-rulemaking_petition/2025_09_29-wslcb-social_equity_program-rulemaking_petition-v1.pdf — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01); Verify current transfer restrictions in WAC 314-55-570 before advising any buyer.
  • Estimated startup costs — NON-OFFICIAL ESTIMATES ONLY, clearly marked:
  • State licence fees themselves are small ($250 application + $1,781 annual for producer/processor/retailer; $250 + $1,300 reported for transporter). The dominant costs are premises, build-out, security, inventory, and — on the secondary market — the licence purchase price.
  • National (not Washington-specific) dispensary startup range cited by Upmetrics: $187,000–$1,174,600 total, with one-time expenses $70,600–$281,000 (renovation $20,000–$50,000; initial inventory $50,000–$200,000). — Source: https://upmetrics.co/startup-costs/dispensary — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01. Non-official, national, not Washington-validated — use only as an order-of-magnitude illustration, labelled as such.
  • National cultivation build-out range cited by a design firm: $500,000 to over $10 million depending on size/type; indoor $400–$500/sq ft, greenhouse $200–$350/sq ft, outdoor ~$50–$200/sq ft (their figures). — Source: https://studioblissla.com/how-much-does-it-cost-to-design-and-build-a-cannabis-cultivation-facility/ — Snippet only, Accessed 2026-11-01. Non-official, national, not Washington-validated.
  • No official Washington startup-cost estimate was found. Do not present any Washington-specific startup total as official. A Washington-specific estimate would need premises rent in a buffer-compliant zone, security/CCRS costs, local permit fees, and (if applicable) the secondary-market licence price — none of which LCB publishes as a bundle.

PART 5 — LICENCE DATA SOURCES

How to use this section: Do NOT list every licence holder here (per task). This is an inventory of WHERE to get holder data, in what format, how current it is, and what it cannot tell you. Where the LCB page uses click-to-download buttons, the text fetch on 2026-11-01 did NOT expose the underlying file URLs or filenames. Those are therefore recorded as "Click-to-download on [page URL]; direct file URL/filename NOT exposed in text fetch — capture in live browser" rather than invented. That is a deliberate gap, not an omission to fill by guessing.

LCB data-quality warning (applies to all LCB lists below): The Frequently Requested Lists page currently carries the notice: "These list reports contain possible errors due to a known data transfer issue. Our team is working to fix this as soon as possible. This notice will be removed when the issues are resolved." — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/records/frequently-requested-lists — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. Any count derived from those lists on 2026-11-01 must carry this warning. Also: "Per RCW 42.56.070(8), records received through the Public Records Act may not be used for commercial purposes." — Same page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.

5.2 Enforcement, recalls, and health advisories — what exists and where

  • Recalls page: LCB "posts all notices of voluntary and Board-directed cannabis product recalls per WAC 314-55-225(5)." — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/examiners/recalls — Snippet only — Verify, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Latest entries seen in snippet: 4 Feb 2026 — Hitterz pre-rolls (producer/processor HANNAH INDUSTRIES 424223), reason QC failure at Alis Group for heavy metals; and Okanogan Gold products (423400), reason above pesticide action levels. An older entry (16 Jan 2023, Pioneer Production and Processing, cannabis materials) also appears in the snippet ordering — snippet ordering is not chronological proof; open the page for the full dated list.
  • Fields on the page: date, products (+ traceability identifiers), producer/processor (name + licence number), reason. No bulk download is stated.
  • Enforcement datasets (Frequently Requested Lists): Enforcement Visits (with date and type), Violations, Compliance Checks. Update lag ~2 weeks after month-end. — Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/records/frequently-requested-lists — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • FY2025 enforcement context (secondary summary of LCB Annual Report): 516 cannabis compliance checks at 94% compliance rate; 860 complaint investigations, down from 1,132 in FY2024. — Source: https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Snippet only — Verify in the FY2025 Annual Report PDF before reuse, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Youth-access dashboard: 2025 secondary reporting of dashboard data: 528 cannabis compliance checks, 492 compliant (93.2%); since 2015, cannabis youth compliance 94.3% vs tobacco 86%, alcohol 80%. — Source: http://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/07/washington-cannabis-stores-continue-to-have-far-higher-youth-compliance-rate-than-alcohol-and-tobacco-retailers/ — Snippet only (secondary) — Verify on the LCB dashboard itself, Accessed 2026-11-01. Do not cite dashboard figures from news coverage as primary data in the final dossier without that check.
  • Health advisories: No separate DOH "health advisory" list for cannabis was located distinct from LCB recalls. DOH medical cannabis pages and LCB recalls are the two official channels found. If Part 11 claims a DOH advisory list, its URL is Verify / Unavailable from this task.

5.3 Total licence counts by type and status — with explicit reconciliation

Rule used: Where official sources give different figures for the same thing, the most authoritative and most recent official figure is preferred and the others are listed (per parent sourcing rules). Counts below are licence counts, NOT company counts — one business may hold multiple licences (notably producer + processor).

Count Figure Source + date Status / caveat

5.5 Limitations that apply across Part 5 (carry into research notes)

  1. LCB data-transfer-error notice is live on the master lists page (see Part 5 header). All LCB-list-derived counts on 2026-11-01 are provisional until that notice is lifted.
  2. Status churn: "Active" vs "Pending (Issued)" (renewal/change) vs "Pending (Non-Issued)" (liquor analogue; cannabis page defines only the Issued variant in the excerpt) — counts that ignore this will be wrong.
  3. Licences ≠ businesses ≠ premises ≠ brands: one business, multiple licences; one licence, one premises; producer-processor pairs double-count if producer and processor totals are added naively.
  4. Tribal licensees are not in the state list in the same way; compact data is by compact PDF, not by a unified holder file.
  5. Hemp processor registrants ≠ hemp processors: WSDA processor registration is voluntary (ESB 5372, 2021; WAC 16-306-055 eff. 15 Jan 2022). The registrant list undercounts processors. — Source: https://agr.wa.gov/Departments/Agricultural-Products/Hemp — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01.
  6. Local permission is invisible in state lists. A state licence on the LCB list does not prove city/county permission, and a local permit does not prove state licensure. Both must be checked for any Part 8 property or Part 3 jurisdiction claim.
  7. No personal data: DOH publishes counts only. LCB lists publish business/licence data. No patient, owner home address, personal phone, or personal email is reproduced in this report, per task rule. Business public contact details on LCB lists are official public data, but none are reproduced here because this task does not list holders.
  8. Commercial-use restriction: RCW 42.56.070(8) notice on LCB lists (see Part 5 header) — records obtained via Public Records Act may not be used for commercial purposes. The final dossier should carry this note if it republishes LCB list data.

Part 6 — Market Data

Washington Cannabis & Hemp — Run 1, Part 6: Market Data

Scope note for the parent assembler: This is Run 1, Part 6 ONLY. All facts are for Washington State (WA), not Washington, D.C., and WA places are kept distinct from same-name places elsewhere (e.g. Vancouver, WA vs Vancouver, British Columbia; Bellevue, WA vs Bellevue, NE; Pullman, WA vs any other Pullman). Cross-border neighbours named here are Idaho, Oregon and British Columbia, Canada, only where the source names them.

Date and sourcing convention: Every fact below carries a source link and "Accessed 2026-11-01" (the research date). Official government sources are used first. Secondary sources are used ONLY for non-law market figures, are dated, and are marked "Secondary". Where a page could not be opened, that is stated and the fact is marked Verify. Where a figure could not be located in an official source, it is marked Unavailable — it is not estimated or filled in. No per-capita ranking has been computed by the researcher (see §6.5).

A note on the three different sales bases — read before comparing any two sales figures in this Part: 1. LCB fiscal-year sales and excise tax by county — LCB-administered, state fiscal year (1 July–30 June), retail sales only for FY2018–FY2024 files, FY2025 file separate. Source: LCB Frequently Requested Lists, opened 2026-11-01 (see §6.1). 2. DOR quarterly "Cannabis sales tax table" (XLSX) — DOR's own label is "estimated retail value of cannabis and cannabis products sold at a licensed cannabis retailer (before tax)", estimated from excise tax returns; it is an estimate, not a till total, and its sales-tax column is NOT the 37% excise tax. Source: DOR, "Recreational and medical cannabis taxes", opened 2026-11-01 (see §6.1). 3. Headset point-of-sale (POS) data — sales recorded through participating retailers' POS systems linked to Headset software. It is a commercial sample, not a census of all WA retailers, and its "average item price" covers all product types, not flower per gram. Headset's own methodology note (Q4 2025 report) warns of possible misreporting, duplicates and misclassification. Treat Headset totals as Secondary and do not average them with LCB/DOR totals. 4. JLARC / RAND (July 2025) — JLARC staff analysis of LCB retail sales and excise data, with RAND estimates for production/consumption in 2023 where LCB data were incomplete. JLARC itself states LCB's Central Cannabis Reporting System (CCRS) "currently lacks the basic information LCB needs to reliably estimate production and sales" and that production was estimated in broad ranges in 2023.

6.1 Sales by fiscal year and by month; excise tax revenue

6.1.1 What the official series actually publish

  • The LCB publishes, on its Frequently Requested Lists page, downloadable "Fiscal Year 2015 Sales and Excise Tax by County", "Fiscal Year 2016 …", "Fiscal Year 2017 …", "Fiscal Year 2018 – Fiscal Year 2024 Sales and Excise Tax by County (retail sales only)", "Fiscal Year 2025 – Sales and Excise Tax by County", plus "Cannabis Sales Activity by License Number", "Cannabis Local Government Distributions" and a "Dedicated Cannabis Account Dashboard". The page warns: "These list reports contain possible errors due to a known data transfer issue" and that enforcement datasets are updated about two weeks after the end of the previous month. The underlying XLSX/CSV files themselves were NOT downloaded and read in this Part 6 pass (live-browser/download step for the parent to verify if a cell-level county table is required) — Verify the cell values before republishing any county table from those files.
  • Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/records/frequently-requested-lists — Accessed 2026-11-01. Page opened directly.
  • DOR publishes a "Cannabis sales tax table (XLSX)" under "Recreational and medical cannabis taxes". DOR states the report includes (a) estimated sales tax collections on cannabis and cannabis products for businesses with a cannabis retailer licence, and (b) estimated sales of cannabis and cannabis products exempt from sales tax for medical purposes. DOR states the LCB "approves, regulates, and enforces cannabis licenses" and "also administers and collects the 37 percent cannabis excise tax" — i.e. the excise tax is an LCB collection, not a DOR collection, and DOR's table must not be cited as the excise series.
  • Source: https://dor.wa.gov/about/statistics-reports/other-taxes/recreational-and-medical-cannabis-taxes — Accessed 2026-11-01. Page opened directly.
  • The Senate Ways & Means staff "Legislative Guide to Washington's Tax Structure" (2026) states cannabis excise taxes "are estimated to generate $858.2 million in the 2025-27 biennium with $255 million directed into the state General Fund" and that the rate is 37% on the selling price on each retail sale of cannabis concentrates, useable cannabis and cannabis-infused products. That biennium figure is a forecast, not collections.
  • Source: https://fiscal.wa.gov/Revenue/2026GuideToWashingtonStateTaxStructure.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01 (via search result; PDF text not separately opened — Verify before quoting the biennium split).

6.1.2 Annual retail sales — calendar-year series (official-derived, with conflicts flagged)

Washington's licensed retail sales peaked in 2021 and have fallen every year since on a calendar-year basis. The two compilations below do NOT agree to the dollar and must not be averaged; the difference is small (under ~2%) but real, reflecting the LCB vs DOR bases in §6.1.1.

2022 About $1.277 billion (secondary compilation) Secondary. See conflict note below.
2023 "Just under $1.25 billion" (JLARC); $1.226 billion (DOR-derived compilation) JLARC vs DOR-derived compilation — conflict, see note.
2024 $1.18 billion (MJBizDaily, citing DOR, 8 July 2026); $1.197 billion (DOR-derived compilation) Secondary reporting of DOR data.

6.1.3 Quarterly / monthly sales — most recent official-derived points

No official WA monthly retail-sales series was located in this pass. The DOR table is quarterly; the LCB county files are fiscal-year. A secondary data project that republishes the DOR quarterly table states the regulator's former monthly open-data portal "no longer exists" and that spreading a quarter across three months "would publish figures nobody reported" — that claim itself is Verify against LCB/DOR, but the practical consequence stands: do not present a monthly official series for WA in this dossier unless the parent obtains the LCB "Sales Activity by License Number" file and aggregates it, with method stated.
