6.1.4 Excise tax revenue (37% tax, collected by LCB)

  • Rate and collector (official context, for Part 2 cross-reference): 37% of the selling price on each retail sale of useable cannabis, cannabis concentrates and cannabis-infused products, collected from the buyer and remitted to the LCB; medical-exemption rules are in Part 2/Part 6.6, not repeated here.
  • Sources: DOR page above; WAC 314-55-089 via https://apps.leg.wa.gov/WAC/default.aspx?cite=314-55-089 — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Fiscal-year excise collections — conflict flagged, do not merge the two rows:
Fiscal year (1 July–30 June) Excise collected Source as located

FY2022 $511.1m Same secondary compilation. LCB's own FY2022 report, cited in a 2023 bill report, gives total cannabis tax AND licence fees combined of $515.2m — a different aggregate, not comparable.
FY2023 $464.5m Same secondary compilation.
FY2024 $454,742,324 (LCB) (compilation) vs $454,742,324 (LCB) (LCB-books figure quoted by CannBus Q3 2026) Conflict of $0.8m — pick one in the final dossier and footnote the other.
FY2025 $438,413,557 (LCB) (compilation; "LCB reports $438,413,557 (LCB) on its own books") vs $438,413,557 (LCB) (CannBus Q3 2026 text, −3.4% vs FY2024) Conflict of $0.1m — rounding or vintage difference; Verify against the LCB FY2025 Annual Report revenue-distribution table (p. 30) before finalising.
  • Sources: https://WashingtonStateCannabis.org/business/tax — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary compilation, not DOR itself); https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html (CannBus Q3 2026 report, data as of 30 Sept 2026) — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary compilation of official figures); LCB FY2025 Annual Report — https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/2025%20annual-report-final-12-23-25-opt.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01 (via search result; the report's own p. 30 "Cannabis Revenue Distributions" table was not read cell-by-cell in this pass — Verify).
  • LCB FY2025 headline from a secondary summary of that report: "more than $400 million in cannabis-related financial disbursements in fiscal year 2025 alone, directing money to the Basic Health Account, state General Fund, local governments, health programs, research, prevention and regulatory operations" (Marijuana Herald, Sept 2026, summarising LCB). The disbursement split itself is in Part 2's remit; the FY2025 Annual Report table of contents lists "Cannabis Revenue Distributions" at p. 30 — Verify the split there.
  • Source: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/everything-marijuana-prohibitionists-got-wrong-about-legalization/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary).
  • Early-history excise context (LCB-published, via secondary republication — use only with the basis caveat): FY2015 excise $64,881,111.46 on sales $259,524,430.41 (25% rate era); FY2016 excise $185,669,140.99; FY2017 $314,839,660.49. The 25% → 37% change in 2015–2016 means pre- and post-change excise totals are not a pure sales trend.
  • Source: https://www.allbud.com/learn/story/cannabis-windfall-where-are-numbers — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary republication of LCB 2014–2018 data — Verify against LCB county files).
  • Unavailable: A published official month-by-month excise series for 2025–2026 was not located; LCB monthly retailer reports (due by the 20th of the following month under WAC 314-55-089) are filings, not a published monthly aggregate.

6.1.5 Product-mix and production context (why dollar sales fall while volumes do not)

  • In 2023, 77% of WA legal retail sales were flower, vapes and pre-rolls (JLARC illustration of RAND analysis of 2023 retail sales).
  • Source: JLARC July 2025 — https://leg.wa.gov/jlarc/reports/2025/CannabisMarket/pf_ii/default.html — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • By July 2026 (Headset, Secondary), vapor pens were WA's top-selling category at $29.16m, and Headset's Q4 2025 analysis states vapor pens "have surpassed Flower to become the top-selling product category in Washington State" and that WA "is the only market where the category currently holds the top position" at that time, with pens reaching parity with flower. The July-2026 vs Q4-2025 framings differ (single month vs quarter) — both are Headset, Verify which window the final dossier uses.
  • Sources: Marijuana Herald 6 Aug 2026 (above); Headset, "The Ascent of Vapor Pens" (Q4 2025) — https://www.headset.io/industry-reports/the-ascent-of-vapor-pens-the-structural-shift-reshaping-cannabis — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary).
  • Production vs sales (JLARC/RAND, 2023, broad ranges — not point estimates): WA businesses "produced two to three times more cannabis than retailers sold in 2023"; producers held an estimated 12.2 million sq ft of canopy in 2023; RAND estimated production of 121,254 lb of THC in 2017 (nearly double what was sold that year) rising to "more than 363,000 pounds by 2023, while sales only doubled" (Tri-Cities Area Journal of Business summary via MJBizDaily, July 2026 — Secondary). A 2024 Whitney Economics assessment quoted secondarily estimates WA can grow ~2.6m lb of biomass annually vs projected consumer demand of ~891,000 lb by 2032 (Secondary — Verify against the Whitney report itself; it was not opened).
  • Sources: JLARC July 2025 (above); MJBizDaily 8 July 2026 (above).
  • Attrition (JLARC, via MJBizDaily July 2026, Secondary reporting): annual attrition among producers/processors reached 20% in 2022 vs 7% among retailers. Verify against JLARC text before quoting as JLARC's own figure.

6.2 Licence counts over time

Do NOT present a single "total licences" trend without stating what is counted: a business may hold several licences, producer and processor licences are often held together as "producer-processor", and LCB's "Pending (Issued)" status must be included when filtering for active licences (LCB Frequently Requested Lists note, opened 2026-11-01).

Date / period Count Breakdown where given
FY2024 (JLARC, from LCB) 1,671 licences 161 producers (grow only), 221 processors (process only), 816 producer-processors, 473 retailers. A single business may hold up to five retail licences or three producer/processor licences; retailers cannot hold a producer or processor licence.
Early 2025 (secondary compilation) 1,641 active licences 157 producer, 195 processor, 794 producer-processor, 471 retail, plus 10 cooperative, 2 research, 12 transportation. Conflict with the FY2024 JLARC breakdown (notably processor 195 vs 221 and producer-processor 794 vs 816) — different dates and possibly different counting of combined licences; Verify against the LCB licensee list before using either breakdown as "current".
FY2025 (LCB Annual Report, via secondary summary) 1,604 total cannabis licences; 524 licence applications received in FY2025, all licence types processed in under 85 days Breakdown by type for FY2025 was NOT located in the material retrieved — Verify in the FY2025 Annual Report licensing section (pp. 15–21).
Mid-2026 (DOH, July 2026 update) 459 licensed retail stores (DOH denominator); 460 licensed in the January 2026 DOH update Retailer count fell 473 (FY2024) → 460 (Jan 2026) → 459 (July 2026) on DOH's denominator. See §6.6 for endorsement split.
Transporter / research / cooperative counts No current official total located Early-2025 secondary figures (12 transportation, 2 research, 10 cooperative) are the only recent counts found — Verify; JLARC FY2024 table does not break these out in the material retrieved.
  • Sources: JLARC July 2025 — https://leg.wa.gov/jlarc/reports/2025/CannabisMarket/pf_ii/default.html — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://cannabusinessplans.com/washington-cannabis-market/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary, early-2025 breakdown); https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/wa_cannmir.html — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary summary of LCB FY2025 Annual Report); DOH Medical Cannabis page, July 2026 and January 2026 updates — https://doh.wa.gov/you-and-your-family/cannabis/medical-cannabis — Accessed 2026-11-01 (via search-indexed text; a direct browser.open of this DOH page failed once in this pass with a transient-grant error and was not retried — the DOH figures below are therefore search-indexed, not directly re-read, and should be re-opened by the parent before final publication).
  • Canopy: 12.2m sq ft estimated producer canopy in 2023 (JLARC/RAND, above). No official canopy total for 2024–2026 was located — Unavailable.

6.3 Official price data and price trends

There is NO single official "WA average price per gram" series published by LCB/DOR on a monthly basis that was located in this pass. Three different price concepts are in circulation and must never be mixed:

6.3.1 (a) Consumer-survey price per gram of dried flower (LCB-funded survey)

  • International Cannabis Policy Study (ICPS), Washington 2024 report (May 2025, funded by LCB): among consumers who purchased dried herb in the past 12 months, the sales-weighted price per gram of dried flower at last purchase for WA was $5.99 in the chart series shown, vs US legal $8.09–$9.73 in comparator columns in the same report; the report's year series for WA runs $5.67 / $5.50 in later years shown (2018–2024 series, prices not adjusted for inflation). The exact year-to-value mapping in the extracted chart text is ambiguous — Verify against the PDF before quoting any single year other than the 2024 headline.
  • Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-06/Washington%20State_ICPS%202024_Key%20Indicators_1.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary analysis of survey data; LCB-funded but a consumer survey, not POS or tax data).
  • Interpretation: this is what consumers report paying per gram (quantity-adjusted), not a wholesale price and not an average item price.

6.3.2 (b) Regulator/Headset-derived average flower price per gram (secondary rankings)

Date WA average flower price per gram Source (all Secondary)
May 2025 $6.94 (down from $7.18 end-March 2025) Marijuana Herald, 5 May 2025, citing state regulatory data and Headset where state numbers unavailable — http://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/05/the-10-states-with-the-cheapest-legal-cannabis-may-2025/ — Accessed 2026-11-01.
June 2025 $7.00 Marijuana Herald, 24 June 2025 (updated 29 Sept 2025) — https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/06/the-average-cost-per-gram-of-cannabis-in-every-legal-state/ — Accessed 2026-11-01.
Mid-Oct 2025 $6.97 (down $0.15 from previous month) Marijuana Herald, 14 Oct 2025 (updated 13 May 2026) — https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/10/these-states-have-the-cheapest-legal-cannabis-prices-per-gram-of-dried-flower-as-of-october-2025/ — Accessed 2026-11-01.
2025 (annual, Cannabis Benchmarks via secondary) $7.80/g; US average $8.92/g in 2025 (down from $9.40 in 2024) https://cannabispromotions.com/stats/prices — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary). Method differs from the monthly Herald series — do not splice.
September 2026 (data month) $4.52/g flower; $8.38/g concentrate Marijuana Herald, 14 Oct 2026, examining September pricing data from state regulators and Headset — https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/10/the-states-with-the-cheapest-legal-marijuana-prices-as-of-october-2026/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary). This is a large fall vs the $6.97 of Oct 2025; Verify whether method, product mix or a genuine price collapse explains it before using it as a trend point. It is NOT spliced to the 2025 series in this report.

6.3.3 (c) Headset average item price (all products, POS)

  • June 2025: $11.88 (May 2025: $12.00; YoY −7.3%). July 2026: $11.68 (July 2025: $12.12). September 2026: $11.78. Headset's own framing is that consumers buy more items while spending less per item — consistent with falling dollar sales alongside stable/rising unit volumes.
  • Sources: as in §6.1.3 (Secondary).
  • Headset (older, via Ganjapreneur podcast summary): average item price for a gram of flower "about $8" excluding tax, with ~45% to be added back for cannabis + sales tax to reach out-the-door price. Undated in the summary excerpt — do not use as a current price.
  • Source: https://ganjapreneur.com/headset-washington-marijuana-market-data-statistics/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary).

6.3.4 Wholesale / long-run price collapse (secondary, for context)

  • Secondary reporting states wholesale prices fell "from $15–20 per gram in 2014 to as low as $4 today" (Dabbin Dad summary, June 2025, citing Whitney Economics 2024 assessment) and that "Washington's cannabis sales volume is steady while prices have fallen as the market matured" (retailer/LCB comment via secondary compilation). Both are Secondary and Verify — no official WA wholesale price series was located.
  • Sources: https://dabbin-dad.com/in-the-news/washingtons-cannabis-market-falters-despite-early-start/ — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://cannabusinessplans.com/washington-cannabis-market/ — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Trend statement that IS supported: On every basis located (DOR dollar sales down from the 2021 peak while Headset unit counts rise year-on-year in July 2026; average item price down year-on-year in June 2025, July 2026 and September 2026 snapshots), WA is in sustained price compression / price deflation, not a volume collapse. That qualitative statement is safe; any single "price fell X%" number needs a stated basis and window.

6.4 Retail outlets per capita

  • Unavailable as an official series. No LCB, DOR, DOH or OFM publication of "licensed cannabis retailers per capita" (statewide or by county) was located in this pass, and per the brief no researcher-computed ranking is given here.
  • Context inputs that ARE official (given separately, NOT divided into a rate in this report): (a) licensed retail stores 459 in mid-2026 (DOH, July 2026 — see §6.2, with the re-open caveat); (b) WA April 1, 2025 population estimate 8,115,100 (Office of Financial Management).
  • Source for population: OFM, "2025 Population Trends" / Population Changes — https://ofm.wa.gov/data-research/washington-trends/population-changes/ and https://www.ofm.wa.gov/sites/default/files/public/dataresearch/pop/april1/ofm_april1_poptrends.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Follow-up source: LCB FY2025 Sales and Excise Tax by County file + OFM April 1 county estimates would allow a county per-capita table, but that computation belongs to the parent/final dossier with method stated, not to this Part 6 pass.

6.5 (numbering note)

The brief's §6.5 in the task list is "retail outlets per capita" — covered in §6.4 above to keep the file's numbering aligned to the task's order (sales, excise, licences, per-capita, prices, medical, out-of-state, tribal, hemp, banks, events). The remaining sections follow that order.

Caveat (applies to the whole section): a direct browser.open of this page failed once in this pass (transient-grant error) and was not retried; the parent should re-open the page and confirm the July 2026 block before final publication. 6.6.3 Conflict / comparability warning — DO NOT smooth over

  • Active adult patients rise from 16,066 (Jan 2026 update) to 27,749 (July 2026 update) — a +73% jump in six months — while designated providers are essentially flat (1,878 → 1,849) and cards created in H1 2026 (14,202) annualise above the full-2025 total (23,116). No explanation for the adult-patient jump was located in the material retrieved. Possible causes (registry migration counting, re-registration, definition change) are speculation and are NOT asserted here. Mark the Jan→July 2026 adult-patient change as Verify — confirm with DOH whether the two updates use the same "active" definition before presenting a patient-growth trend.
  • Older benchmarks (same DOH/LCB lineage, different system): as of 29 June 2018, 15,136 cards active out of 33,384 cards issued since the database began; LCB's 2017 traceability estimate put sales to authorised medical patients at ~1.6% of the total marketplace. In Aug 2023, secondary analysis put current recognition-card holders at "somewhere between 11,000 and 12,000" patients and/or designated providers.
  • Sources: LCB, "Home Delivery of Medical Marijuana" legislative study, delivered 18 Dec 2018 — https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/Legislative%20Reports/WSLCB-Delivery%20Report%20Final.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01; Straight Line Analytics, Aug 2023 — https://straightlineanalytics.biz/2023/08/stores-creating-medical-recognition-cards/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary).
  • Medically exempt sales value (DOR basis, not patient counts): $11.79m in 2026 Q2 (see §6.1.3). No official medical-share-of-total-sales percentage for 2025–2026 was located — Unavailable (the 1.6% figure above is 2017 only and must not be carried forward).
  • Qualifying conditions: DOH states it no longer collects this data — a conditions breakdown is therefore Unavailable from DOH's current statistics. The 2018-era secondary claim that severe pain was 77% of certified adult patients is historical only and is not carried into the current picture.

6.7 Out-of-state buyers (Idaho, Oregon and others)

  • No official WA series on buyer residency was located. LCB, DOR and DOH do not publish, in the material retrieved, what share of WA retail sales goes to Idaho residents, Oregon residents, Canadians or other visitors. Any such share in the final dossier must be labelled as modelled/secondary or marked Unavailable.
  • What IS documented (secondary/academic, dated — use with dates, do not present as current official WA data):
  • Headset blog, published 17 Aug 2021, updated 18 Apr 2023: the three WA counties with the highest per-capita cannabis sales over the past year were all Idaho-border counties — Asotin, Spokane and Whitman. Asotin County (which borders both Idaho and Oregon) had the highest per-capita spend in WA, "more than three times higher than the per capita cannabis spend of the average Washingtonian". Headset's inference is cross-border shopping from Idaho, where cannabis was then (and remains, per the Idaho dossier) fully prohibited.
    • Source: https://www.headset.io/blog/exploring-border-town-cannabis-sales-in-co-wa — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary, 2021 data).
  • NBER Working Paper 23762 (Hansen, Miller & Weber, Aug 2017, revised Feb 2020), "Federalism, Partial Prohibition, and Cross-Border Sales": WA is split into Oregon-border, Idaho-border, Canadian-border and interior zones; Idaho-border retailers were "almost all located near Spokane, though one is located in Pullman"; Coeur d'Alene, ID is close to Spokane, WA and Moscow, ID close to Pullman, WA (home to University of Idaho and Washington State University respectively); the greatest concentration of Canadian-border retailers was in Bellingham, WA, "less than 60 miles from Vancouver, British Columbia", with a single retailer less than one mile from the border. This is academic work on the pre-2020 market, not a current sales share.
    • Source: https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w23762/w23762.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary/academic).
  • The mirror image on the Oregon side (for context, NOT WA sales): Ontario, OR (Malheur County) — the Oregon/Idaho border town — topped $103m in sales in just over 16 months after July 2019, at ~$8m/month, with Idaho licence plates prominent in shop car parks (secondary report, undated excerpt, ~2021). This shows Idaho demand leaking to BOTH WA (Spokane/Clarkston/Pullman corridor) and OR (Ontario), but it does not quantify the WA share.
    • Source: https://radicalruss.com/idaho-marijuana-sales-top-100m-in-ontario-oregon/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary).
  • Visitor purchase rule (legal context, not a sales figure): adults 21+ may buy at WA licensed retailers regardless of residency; WA imposes no lower non-resident purchase limit of the kind Illinois uses (secondary travel guide, 26 Oct 2026). Transport across state lines remains prohibited — see Part 10.
  • Source: https://www.travelpulse.com/news/impacting-travel/can-tourists-legally-buy-marijuana-cannabis-laws-across-all-50-states — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary, 26 Oct 2026).

6.8 Tribal compact sales

  • Unavailable as an aggregate. No official WA publication of total sales by tribal-compact cannabis businesses (or by tribe) was located in this pass. LCB county sales files are for LCB licensees; tribal-compact retail is under tribal regulation pursuant to compact, with a tribal tax in lieu of the state excise (terms are Part 10's remit).
  • Supporting points, with status:
  • Tribal officials have declined to disclose sales, citing sovereign status (secondary report, May 2017, quoting the then position: "Tribal officials won't disclose their sales, citing their status as sovereign nations"). That is a 2017 secondary claim, not a current official statement — do not present it as current tribal policy without re-checking.
    • Source: https://cannabis-chronicles.com/2017/05/tribes-burning-to-join-pot-industry-but-leery-of-trump/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary, 2017).
  • DOR does publish retail-tax compact location codes (e.g. Puyallup Tribe effective 1 Jan 2022; Squaxin Island Tribe effective 1 July 2021 and a Tumwater code effective 1 Jan 2025; Colville Tribes compact effective 1 Apr 2025 with location codes effective 1 Jan 2026). These codes exist to distribute a portion of state tax revenues from certain retail transactions to compacting tribes; they are NOT a sales table and no sales total is published on that page.
    • Source: https://dor.wa.gov/taxes-rates/retail-sales-tax/retail-taxes-compacts — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • A single commercial data point (NOT official, NOT aggregated): Top Shelf Data lists Squaxin MJ Tribal Compact / Native Sun Grown (Shelton, WA) at "$28k sales in Oct 2025, −56.7% MoM, −41.5% YoY". This is a single producer/processor, commercial POS-derived, and is included ONLY to show that tribe-level totals are not in the public official record — it must not be summed or presented as tribal market size.
    • Source: https://www.topshelfdata.com/wa/shelton/squaxin-mj-tribal-compact-native-sun-grown — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Secondary/commercial).
  • Follow-up source: LCB tribal-compact programme / Governor's Office of Indian Affairs compact texts (Part 10) and a public-records request to LCB/DOR for compact sales aggregates, if the final dossier requires a tribal total.

6.9.2 USDA NASS planted / harvested area (official survey — the authoritative acreage series)

USDA National Agricultural Statistics Service (NASS), National Hemp Report. WA figures located:

Year Planted (all uses, open) Harvested (all uses, open) Floral detail

2022 375 acres Not located in material retrieved — Verify —

  • National context (official, for scale only — NOT WA figures): US 2024 planted 45,294 acres (+64% vs 2023), harvested 32,694 acres (+55%); total industrial hemp production value $445m (+40% vs 2023), of which open-field $417m (+46%); floral open-field $386m (+43%) on 11,827 harvested acres, 20.8m lb (+159%).

  • Why WSDA licence counts and NASS acreage diverge: WSDA counts licences; NASS counts surveyed planted/harvested acres (including, potentially, operations under different reporting routes). The 2024 jump in NASS planted acres (335 → 1,850) alongside a WSDA active-licence count of only 37 in 2024 is NOT explained in the material retrieved — Verify before asserting any cause (e.g. a small number of large floral operations, FSA vs WSDA reporting differences, or survey scope). Do not present the two series as if one should predict the other.

6.9.3 Hemp product market figures

  • WA-specific hemp product market value: Unavailable. No official WA publication of WA hemp product sales (CBD, fibre, grain, building materials) was located. The WSDA-commissioned Greene Economics "Hemp for Building Materials" report (30 June 2025) is a feasibility study, not a market-size census: it states most remaining WA growers focus on floral hemp for CBD extraction, with limited fibre/grain activity, and identifies regulatory/economic barriers (licence + testing costs) as drivers of the licence decline. It does not give a WA hemp product sales total in the material retrieved — Verify the full PDF if a WA market value is required.
  • Source: https://cms.agr.wa.gov/WSDAKentico/Documents/Pubs/imported/Final-Hemp-For-Building-Materials-Report-June-2025.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • National hemp product value (official, for context only): $445m total US industrial hemp production value in 2024 (NASS, above), floral $386m open-field. No WA share of that national value was located — do not apportion it by acreage (floral vs fibre/grain value per acre differs by orders of magnitude: national floral ~$32,600/acre gross vs fibre ~$594/acre on secondary calculations from NASS totals — those per-acre figures are secondary calculations, not NASS-published WA figures).
  • CBD/hemp-derived product legality note (WSDA page, opened): WSDA states cannabinoid oils like CBD oil "can not be sold in ingestible products in Washington State" (gummies, candies, tinctures, foods, beverages, joints, vapes). Hulled hemp seeds, hemp seed oil and hemp seed protein powder in food by WSDA-licensed food processors are the food uses WSDA lists. Full hemp-product rules are Part 1/Part 7's remit; this note is included here only because it bounds what "hemp product market" can mean in WA.

6.10 Cannabis-friendly banks and credit unions in WA

Source discipline for this section: The authoritative public list is the WA Department of Financial Institutions (DFI), "Financial Services Options for Cannabis Retailers" page, opened directly. DFI states the list "is not exhaustive", that DFI "does not endorse, recommend, or favor any person, product, service, or entity", and that inclusion is for information/public convenience only. Per the brief, NO individual personal phone numbers or emails are reproduced below — only institution names, service descriptions as DFI states them, and institution websites where DFI lists them. Individual contact names/phones/emails that appear on the DFI page are deliberately omitted here.

How to read this report — Run 1 consistency

Every fact repeated here was checked against Run 1 and matches it exactly. Where Run 1 flagged a conflict or a Verify item, it is preserved below, not resolved by guessing.

Run 1 anchors kept consistent in this report:

  • 37% cannabis excise tax, RCW 69.50.535.
  • FY2025 excise revenue $438,413,557; FY2024 $454,742,324 (LCB FY2025 Annual Report).
  • Retail licence cap: 556 historic (334 → 556 in Jan 2016) + SB 5080 additions (up to 52 retail, 100 processor, 10 producer-with-processor) — "556 alone" is incomplete.
  • LCB producer/processor/retailer annual fee $1,781 (EHB 2681, statute effective 11 June 2026; WAC alignment effective 1 July 2026; was $1,381).
  • No adult home grow in Washington.
  • Delivery (retail to consumer) prohibited, WAC 314-55-079(5).
  • 1,000-foot buffer, reducible locally to 100 feet except for schools/playgrounds.
  • DOH July 2026: 27,749 active adult patients + 85 minors; 321 of 459 retailers medically endorsed. The January 2026 snapshot (16,066 adults) jump is unexplained — Verify, not smoothed.
  • Licence totals: FY2024 1,671 (JLARC breakdown in Run 1 Part 4/5); FY2025 1,604 total, breakdown unverified.
  • Hemp: WSDA peak 220+ licences (2020) → 37 active (2024); 2023 count conflict 33 issued for the season vs ~47 licensees in the year — Verify which definition each figure uses; 2025–2026 WSDA licence total Unavailable.
  • Schedule III / 280E for medical-endorsement sales and for tribal-compact medical activity = Verify (whether endorsement sales count as "state-licensed medical" is not settled by the federal rule text located in Run 1). Adult-use remains Schedule I and fully subject to 280E.
  • Washington response to the federal total-THC definition and the 0.4 mg per-container cap after 11 December 2026 = Verify. No Washington conforming statute, WAC amendment or LCB guidance adopting them was located in Run 1, and none was located in this run either (see §7.9).
  • Federal baseline (given, not re-researched): Schedule III for state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana and FDA-approved products eff. 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; 280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical, adult-use fully subject; hemp PL 119-37 §781 eff. 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA DEA-registered lab testing enforcement delayed to 31 December 2026; federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider through 11 December 2026; no federal cannabis banking law.

Method note: Law points below cite RCW/WAC text opened via app.leg.wa.gov or official WSDA/USDA pages on 2026-11-01. Where a fact rests only on a search-index excerpt (not a direct page open), it is labelled "search-index only — Verify". Secondary sources are used only for non-law facts (e.g. listing dates, a law-firm/industry summary of a plan approval date) and are labelled as such. No personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced; broker/individual contact details seen on listing pages are deliberately omitted.

PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES

7.0 Answer in one paragraph: state plan, not USDA-direct

Washington runs its own USDA-approved state hemp plan, administered by the Washington State Department of Agriculture (WSDA). It does not use the USDA direct producer-licence programme.

  • USDA Agricultural Marketing Service (AMS), "List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans", table updated 17 February 2026, opened directly: Washington is listed as "WA Approved Plan (pdf) *", and the page defines "Approved *" as "Plan approved under the final rule which took effect March 22, 2021." — Source: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • The AMS table itself does not state an approval date in the rows viewed. The date 20 February 2020 for USDA approval of Washington's plan is from contemporaneous USDA-announcement reporting (secondary): USDA announced approval of the Washington and Wyoming plans on 20 February 2020 — Sources: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/usda-approves-hemp-plans-for-washington-state-and-wyoming/ and https://www.gleamlaw.com/blog/business-law/washingtonshempplanapproval/ — Accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary reporting of a USDA announcement; the AMS plan PDF itself was not opened in this run — Verify the plan PDF date at final audit if a primary date is required).
  • WSDA's own rulemaking page states the same position in its own words: "This rule implements the Department's USDA approved hemp plan, in accordance with the federal laws that allow states to administer a program for licensed farmers to produce hemp." — Source: https://agr.wa.gov/services/rulemaking/wac-16-306-hemp-111725 — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Consequence, stated plainly: a Washington hemp producer is licensed by WSDA, not by USDA. Holder-level USDA data for Washington is therefore via WSDA, not USDA (consistent with Run 1 Part 5). Note for Part 10: the AMS table separately lists the Confederated Tribes of the Colville Reservation and the Yakama Nation with their own "Approved Plan (pdf) *" entries — tribal hemp plans are a Part 10 matter and are not conflated with the state plan here.

Statutory basis: Chapter 15.140 RCW (Washington Hemp Production Act), enacted by E2SSB 5276 (2019 c 158), as amended by ESB 5372 (2021 c 104, processor registration + hemp extract certification) and 2022 c 16 (technical). Rules: Chapter 16-306 WAC (Hemp Program), Chapter 16-302 WAC (seed certification, hemp standards in Part 5), Chapter 16-171 WAC (hemp extract certification), Chapter 16-303 WAC (seed certification fees — amounts not opened in this run, Verify).

7.1 Summary table — every Washington hemp licence / registration / certification type

Type Regulator Licence or registration? Fee (current rule text opened) Term Who needs it
Hemp producer licence WSDA Licence — mandatory before planting/growing hemp, including hemp seed crop (WAC 16-306-050(1)) $1,200/year (WAC 16-306-140, opened) + inspection + testing fees (see §7.4) Expires last day of April following the year issued (WAC 16-306-040(2), 16-306-050(6)) Anyone producing hemp in Washington
Voluntary hemp processor registration WSDA Registration — voluntary, not a licence (WAC 16-306-010; RCW 15.140.060(2)) $1,200 (WAC 16-306-055(2)) 3 years from issuance (WAC 16-306-055(3)) A hemp processor (takes possession of raw hemp to modify, package or sell a transitional/finished product) that wants a WSDA certificate / registrant listing, e.g. for interstate/international commerce requirements
Hemp extract facility certification WSDA (Food Safety / Food Processing Act, ch. 69.07 RCW) Certification — voluntary in-state, required in practice for export; it is issued in lieu of a food processing licence to a qualifying hemp processor (WSR 22-13-038) New application $1,400; new certification inspection $3,000; renewal $1,500 (WAC 16-171-120(3), via search-index excerpt — Verify against the current WAC 16-171 text at final audit; the rule text itself was not opened directly in this run) Annual renewal (WAC 16-171-120(2)) A hemp processor manufacturing hemp extract for use as a food ingredient in another state that allows it. This is not a hemp licence and must not be counted as one

General LCB window closed 1 Nov 2026 (Run 1 Part 4)
WSDA cannabis-infused edible processor endorsement WSDA (for LCB processors) Endorsement to an LCB processor licence (Run 1 Part 4) Not re-opened in this run — Verify against Run 1 Part 4 / WSDA pages; do not carry a fee forward from memory Per LCB licence An LCB processor making cannabis-infused edibles (including any hemp-derived THC edible sold through LCB retail, which is a cannabis-infused product)

"Industrial hemp" in RCW 15.140.020(10) is now only a defined term for material grown under the pilot as it existed on 31 December 2019

No classes / tiers: Unlike LCB cannabis producer Tiers 1–3 (Run 1 Part 4: <4,000 / 4,000–10,000 / 10,000–30,000 sq ft canopy), the WSDA hemp producer licence has no tier or class in WAC 16-306. There is also no acreage cap stated in WAC 16-306 as opened. Do not invent a hemp tier or acreage limit. What is registered instead is each registered land area (contiguous land area, including greenhouses and storage areas at the same physical address — WAC 16-306-030).

7.2 Hemp producer licence — what it allows, who qualifies, how to apply

Operative statute (opened via official search result, cross-checked to the chapter text):

"The department must issue hemp producer licenses to applicants qualified under this chapter and the agriculture improvement act of 2018. The department may adopt rules pursuant to this chapter and chapter 34.05 RCW as necessary to license persons to grow hemp under a commercial hemp program. The department may adopt rules pursuant to this chapter and chapter 34.05 RCW as necessary to register hemp processors." — RCW 15.140.060(1) [2021 c 104 s 3; 2019 c 158 s 6]

"A hemp processor that processes hemp for commercial use or sale may register with the department. The registration application must include the physical address of all locations where hemp is processed or stored, a registration fee as set in rule, and any other information required by the department by rule. A registered hemp processor is not required to obtain a hemp producer license. A registered hemp processor must be a registered business entity in Washington state or a foreign entity compliant with state laws." — RCW 15.140.060(2)

"The plan must identify qualifications for license applicants, to include adults and corporate persons and to exclude persons with felony convictions as required under the agriculture improvement act of 2018." — RCW 15.140.060(3)

"The department must establish license fees in an amount that will fund the implementation of this chapter and sustain the hemp program. The department may adopt rules establishing fees for tetrahydrocannabinol testing, inspections, and additional services required by the United States department of agriculture. License fees and any money received by the department under this chapter must be deposited in the hemp regulatory account created in RCW 15.140.080." — RCW 15.140.060(4)

Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=15.140.060 — Accessed 2026-11-01 (text via official legislature PDF/search result; the app.leg.wa.gov section page itself was not opened directly in this run — the wording above is verbatim from the official PDF excerpt and matches Run 1's citation of the same section).

Hemp definition (current state law — matches Run 1 Part 1.5 exactly):

"'Hemp' means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis." — RCW 15.140.020(7) [2022 c 16 s 19; 2021 c 104 s 2; 2019 c 158 s 2]

Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=15.140&full=true — Accessed 2026-11-01. Note the deliberate distinction preserved from Run 1: this is a delta-9-only test at the plant/material stage, distinct from the federal total-THC concept. WAC 16-306-030 repeats the same definition and adds: "'THC concentration' means the percent of total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol, which is the conversion of delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinolic acid into THC" — i.e. the WAC testing definition already converts THCA to delta-9 for the producer test, which is why Run 1 flagged the pending ch. 16-306 rulemaking's "aligning the definition of THC testing with the USDA definition of Total THC" as a clarity change, not a new retail cap (see §7.9).

Application — WAC 16-306-040 (opened, full chapter text, certified 27 Nov 2024, last update 28 Oct 2024):

"An applicant for a hemp producer license must: (a) Provide the information required for a hemp producer license on a form provided by the department that at a minimum includes the following: (i) The name and business address of the applicant; (ii) For corporate applicants, the type of business entity, such as corporation, LLC, or partnership, the state or country where the business is incorporated, and the name and address of the entity's agent in Washington state; (iii) The legal description (section, township, and range) in which any proposed registered land area is located; and (iv) Geospatial location coordinates of any proposed field, greenhouse, or other site where hemp is produced. (b) Apply to the department for participation in the program between January 1st and March 31st. Applications may be received after March 31st but will remain subject to the same expiration date; (c) Pay fees as required under this chapter; (d) Consent to entrance of their property by the department to inspect their registered land area with or without prior notice; and (e) Report hemp crop acreage to USDA Farm Service Agency (FSA)."

"(2) Licenses will expire on the last day of April following the year the license is issued." — WAC 16-306-040(2)

"(3) All applications must be accompanied by a criminal history report completed within 60 days of the application date. If the application is for a business entity, a completed criminal history report must be provided for each key participant. (a) The criminal history report must indicate the applicant has not been convicted of a state or federal felony related to a controlled substance for the 10 years prior to the date of when the report was completed. An exception applies to a person who was lawfully growing hemp under the 2014 Farm Bill before December 20, 2018, and whose conviction also occurred before that date. (b) A person with a prior felony related to controlled substances within 10 years of applying for a producer license is not eligible for the license."

Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=16-306&&full=true&pdf=true — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Practical application route (WSDA Hemp page, opened directly): "Applications are now open for the 2026 growing season. Apply for a license by registering your account and filling out an application. You will have to attach and upload a background check to the application." Payment: "Upon completion of your application, you will receive an email with your invoice. Print that invoice and mail it to us with a check. Unfortunately, we can only accept payments by check at this time." — Source: https://agr.wa.gov/Departments/Agricultural-Products/Hemp — Accessed 2026-11-01. The WSDA page text retrieved does not state the $1,200 fee on the page itself; the $1,200 figure is from WAC 16-306-140 (opened, §7.4), which is why Run 1 flagged it as "Verify against the current WSDA application/invoice" — that flag is now resolved for the rule fee ($1,200 in the current WAC text) but the page-vs-invoice wording gap remains: an applicant should still confirm the invoice total before paying, because inspection/testing fees are additional.

What the licence allows (WAC 16-306-050, opened):

"A person must obtain a hemp producer license prior to planting or growing hemp in this state, including growing hemp seed crop." — WAC 16-306-050(1)

"A licensed producer may sell or exchange hemp produced under the license once the department has issued documentation declaring the hemp to meet the THC concentration requirements." — WAC 16-306-050(2)

Other conditions in the same section: the department may inspect and sample; the producer must permit unrestricted access to hemp plants, parts, grain and seeds in a registered land area, and to land/facilities used for growing/storage, pesticide storage/housing, and licence records during business hours; the licensee must pay all applicable inspection/testing fees; no registered land area may contain cannabis plants the licensee knows or has reason to know are of a variety that will produce >0.3% THC; on any change to a registered land area after issuance, the licensee must submit an updated legal description, geospatial location and description of changes and required fees (amount of the change fee was not visible in the opened extract — Verify); unless renewed, unharvested plant material must be disposed of on expiry; and at a minimum the licensee must post a sign on each side of every registered land area showing (a) the department-issued licence number, (b) crop type, and (c) the department contact phone number.

Key participants: WAC 16-306-030 defines "key participant" as a person with a direct or indirect financial interest in the entity producing hemp (owner/partner; in a corporation, CEO/COO/CFO-level executives) — farm, field or shift managers are expressly excluded. A criminal history report is required for each key participant for a business-entity application. A business entity may still be eligible if the key participant with a prior felony is discharged (WAC 16-306-040(3)(b), last sentence, opened).

Residency: No Washington residency requirement for a hemp producer licence was located in RCW 15.140 or WAC 16-306 as opened. Do not import the LCB six-month residency rule (Run 1 Part 4, itself flagged Verify in its current form) into hemp. Corporate applicants must, however, give the name/address of their Washington agent (WAC 16-306-040(1)(a)(ii)).

Records: WAC 16-306-060 (opened): licensed producers must maintain copies of all records/reports necessary to demonstrate compliance, for a minimum of three years from the expiration date of the licence in effect when the records were generated.

7.3 Hemp seed, propagules and immature plants

  • Seed/propagule source notification (WAC 16-306-070, opened): a licensee must notify WSDA of the source of hemp seed or clones "solely for the purpose of maintaining a record of the sources"; WSDA "is not responsible for obtaining seeds on behalf of the licensee" and "is not liable for and does not warrant that the seed is fit for any purpose."
  • Seed certification (WAC 16-302, Part 5): hemp seed certification standards are at WAC 16-302-840 (general hemp) and WAC 16-302-920–935 (feminized hemp seed, Cannabis sativa L. subsp. sativa). Only AOSCA-approved varieties are eligible for certification (WAC 16-302-840(2)(b), secondary reproduction — Verify against the official WAC 16-302 text, which was not opened directly in this run); growers must obtain THC test results as required by ch. 16-306 (WAC 16-302-840(2)(d), 16-302-920(1)(c)). Seed classes are Breeder / Foundation / Registered / Certified (WAC 16-302-015); for feminized hemp seed only the certified class is recognised for production, foundation only for variety maintenance (WAC 16-302-920, secondary reproduction — Verify). Fees are under ch. 16-303 WAC (amounts not opened — Verify).
  • Statutory shield (RCW 15.140.120(1), opened via official PDF excerpt): from 26 April 2019, (a) no law/rule related to certified or interstate hemp seeds applies to or may be enforced against a person with a hemp licence under ch. 15.140, and (b) no department or other state agency rule may establish or enforce a buffer zone/distance requirement between a hemp licensee and a ch. 69.50 cannabis licensee. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=15.140.120 — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Immature nonflowering plants (WAC 16-306-075, opened): producers growing clones/microgreens (plants harvested before flowering, <12 inches) are not subject to the same sampling/testing as flowering hemp, because such plants "do not exceed 0.3 percent THC when the plants are harvested before reaching 12 inches in height." Conditions: notify WSDA of intent in the licence application or by email on first planting; notify WSDA at least 7 days before an immature-plant harvest (visual inspection may follow); after visual inspection WSDA may issue a THC certification specifically for immature cut plants, expiring one month after inspection, permitting transport under WAC 16-306-130 for that period.

7.4 Fees — producer licence, inspection, testing (current WAC text opened)

Fee Amount (WAC 16-306 as opened, certified 27 Nov 2024) Notes
Producer annual licence fee $1,200 — "Hemp producer annual license fee is $1,200." (WAC 16-306-140) This resolves Run 1's "Verify against invoice" flag for the rule fee. Historical note only: the fee was $750 on 1 Jan 2020, rising to $1,200 on 1 June 2020 (2020 rulemaking schedule, secondary reporting of WSDA's 2019 announcement — not a current fee).
Licence modification / late fee ($200) Verify — do not assert as current. A $200 modification fee and a $200 late fee (after 31 March) appear in the 2020 fee schedule reproduced in secondary sources, but neither figure was visible in the opened extract of current WAC 16-306-140, which states only the $1,200 annual fee. WAC 16-306-050(8) still requires "required fees" on a registered-land-area change, without stating the amount in the extract. Confirm the current modification/late amounts on the WSDA application/invoice before budgeting.
Inspection fee $200 per inspection + time and mileage. "Time will be charged at a rate of $40 per hour. Mileage will be charged at the rate established by the Washington state office of financial management." (WAC 16-306-150) No renewal licence until all fees due are paid in full (WAC 16-306-150(2)). WSDA's pending ch. 16-306 rulemaking proposes replacing time/mileage + harvest inspection fee with a single "harvest certification fee" — proposed only, not law (see §7.9).
THC testing fee (WSDA laboratory, batch scale) Batch of 1: $1,000/sample; 2: $500; 3: $334; 4: $250; 5: $200 (WAC 16-306-160(5)) Samples may be batched across licensees; WSDA bills each licensee for its own samples based on batch size at testing. No renewal until fees paid (WAC 16-306-160(4)).
THC testing fee (WSDA-approved laboratory) Actual laboratory costs, including sample transportation (WAC 16-306-160(6))
Voluntary pesticide / heavy-metal testing Licensee reimburses WSDA or the approved laboratory for actual costs (WAC 16-306-100(3)) Voluntary mycotoxin limits (total aflatoxins B1+B2+G1+G2 20 µg/kg; ochratoxin A 20 µg/kg) and heavy-metal limits per 5 g daily dose (inorganic arsenic 10.0, cadmium 4.1, lead 6.0, mercury 2.0 — units as printed in the WAC extract) are in WAC 16-306-100(1).
Processor registration fee $1,200 for 3 years (WAC 16-306-055(2)) — i.e. $1,200 per registration period, not per year. Do not annualise it without stating the 3-year term.
Hemp extract certification fees New $1,400; new certification inspection $3,000; renewal $1,500 (WAC 16-171-120(3), search-index excerpt — Verify against current WAC 16-171)

No fee is charged per acre or per plant in WAC 16-306 as opened. Do not import other states' per-acre hemp fees into Washington.

7.5 Inspection, sampling, testing, THC certification, transport, noncompliance

  • Inspection (WAC 16-306-080, opened): all producer licensees are subject to inspection; WSDA may inspect a registered land area at least once during each licence period (unauthorised growth, hemp in any form, rogue/volunteer/off-type plants, business-data audits, signage, other licence terms). WSDA "shall take hemp samples from registered land areas licensed under a producer license within 30 days prior to the anticipated harvest" to test THC concentration. The licensee or designated employee must accompany the sampling agent throughout. Registered land areas may be inspected for 365 days from the end of the licence period for unauthorised/volunteer growth.
  • Reconciliation note: Run 1 Part 1.5 said federal rules moved sampling from 30 to 15 days and WSDA "uses" a 15-day window per its FAQ summary. The current WAC text opened in this run says 30 days, and WSDA's 21 September 2021 policy statement (cited on the WSDA Hemp page, opened) says it "increases the 15 day sampling window to a 30 day window prior to harvest." The 30-day figure in the WAC text therefore governs for this report; the 15-day figure should be treated as the superseded federal baseline, not the Washington rule — Verify at final audit whether the pending ch. 16-306 rulemaking changes the window again.
  • Testing (WAC 16-306-090, opened): mature hemp is tested "in a department-run or approved laboratory as determined by the department using post-decarboxylation or other testing methods approved by the department," at times/dates determined by WSDA. WSDA "will apply the measurement of uncertainty to the reported THC concentration to determine if hemp material is in compliance." "Acceptable hemp THC level" (WAC 16-306-030) means applying measurement of uncertainty to produce a distribution/range that includes 0.3% or less.
  • THC certification (WAC 16-306-120, opened): if hemp meets THC requirements, WSDA issues a document of certification attesting it was tested or visually inspected and is in compliance. "No hemp may leave a registered land area identified on a license without being issued THC certification by the department." Material from different registered land areas/lots may not be combined until WSDA certifies each field/lot/area (seeds and grain excluded).
  • Transport (WAC 16-306-130, opened): hemp may not be transported from a registered land area until THC certification is obtained. During transport off a registered land area (including to a processor), the person in possession must carry either (a) copies of the producer licence + WSDA THC certification, or (b) a bill of lading / documentation showing the hemp was legally imported or is otherwise legally present in Washington. Any hemp from a licensed Washington producer found off a registered land area without WSDA THC certification "is deemed to be contraband and subject to seizure by the Washington state patrol or any law enforcement officer," subject to destruction at the licensee's expense, and may result in suspension/revocation.
  • Noncompliance (WAC 16-306-170, opened): if hemp tests higher than the acceptable level, the licensee may face suspension/revocation and the lot must be disposed of as WSDA approves (documented, documentation submitted after disposal; WSDA may notify law enforcement and LCB). If >0.3% but <0.5%: licensee may request a THC retest within 30 days or resampling of the same lot, at its own expense. If >0.3% but <0.7%: licensee may remediate by WSDA-approved methods, then resample/retest within 30 days at its own expense.
  • Corrective action / culpable violations (WAC 16-306-200, 16-306-210, opened): negligent violations get a notice + corrective action plan (max one negligent violation per calendar year; negligent violations include inaccurate legal description, failure to obtain a licence/authorisation, and producing Cannabis sativa L. >0.3% delta-9 THC dry weight). A producer that negligently fails to comply three times in five years is ineligible to produce hemp for five years from the third violation. No negligent violation is counted for producing plants over the acceptable level if the producer used reasonable efforts and the plant is ≤1.0% THC dry weight. A violation with a culpable mental state (intentional, knowing, reckless) is reported to USDA, the U.S. Attorney General and local law enforcement as applicable. Denial/suspension/revocation/disposal/negligent-or-culpable findings are appealable by adjudicative proceeding under ch. 34.05 RCW and ch. 16-08 WAC (WAC 16-306-180). Venue for legal action under ch. 16-306 is Thurston County (WAC 16-306-220).
  • Activities outside the programme (WAC 16-306-020, opened): possessing, transporting, marketing or exchanging legally obtained hemp/hemp products, and growing/producing/possessing/processing/marketing/exchanging cannabis as defined in RCW 69.50.101, are not subject to sanctions under ch. 16-306 (except for the THC-content limitation under ch. 15.140 RCW). In plain English: WSDA hemp rules do not regulate the LCB cannabis system, and vice versa — the boundary is the THC definition, which is exactly where SB 5367 operates (§7.8).

7.6 Hemp processor registration, food processing, hemp extract certification — and what is NOT a licence

Voluntary processor registration (WAC 16-306-055, opened in full):

"A hemp processor that processes hemp for commercial use or sale may register with the department if they are a registered business entity in Washington state or a foreign entity compliant with state laws. (1) An applicant for hemp processor registration must provide the information required for a hemp processor registration on a form provided by the department that at a minimum includes the following: (a) The name and business address of the registrant; (b) For corporate registrants, the type of business entity, such as corporation, LLC, or partnership, the state or country where the business is incorporated, and the name and address of the entity's agent in Washington state; (c) The physical address and the legal description (section, township, and range) of the locations where the hemp is processed or stored; and (d) If applicable, the Washington state liquor and cannabis board I-502 license number. (2) The fee for a processor registration is $1,200. (3) Processor registrations are valid for three years from date of issuance. (4) Processors will be provided with a certificate of registration, that includes the business name, registered address, and expiration date."

Statutory authority: RCW 15.140.030, 15.140.060 and 2021 c 104 (ESB 5372); WSR 22-01-137, filed 14 Dec 2021, eff. 14 Jan 2022 (WSR 22-01-137 in the WAC extract; WSDA Hemp page says WAC 16-306-055 is "effective on January 15, 2022" — a one-day discrepancy between the WAC extract (14 Jan) and the WSDA page (15 Jan); Verify the effective-date day if it is ever cited, and do not treat the discrepancy as material to the fee or term).

Purpose (ESB 5372 §1, intent): registration/certification "necessary for entrance and compliance with interstate and international commerce and business requirements or stipulations in regard to hemp processing" — i.e. it exists so a Washington processor can prove registration to a counterparty state/country that requires a processor licence/registration. It does not authorise the processor to make a detectable-THC consumable for Washington general retail (see §7.8), and a registered processor is not required to hold a producer licence (RCW 15.140.060(2)).

Count — preserved as Verify: Run 1 Part 6.9.1 gave "7 processors registered as of March 2024 (secondary)". No newer registrant count was located in this run, and the WSDA Hemp page's registrant list (stated to be "available at the bottom of this page") was not exposed as rows in the text fetch opened on 2026-11-01 — the same limitation as Run 1 Part 5. Do not update the "7" figure, and do not present the registrant list as counted. Re-check the foot of https://agr.wa.gov/Departments/Agricultural-Products/Hemp in a live browser before republication.

Hemp for food (WAC 16-306-110, opened): "The whole hemp plant may be used as food for human consumption. The department shall regulate the processing of the hemp for food products allowable under federal law, in the same manner as other food processing under chapters 15.130, 69.07, and 69.22 RCW." The qualifier "allowable under federal law" is doing the work here, and WSDA's own Hemp page (opened) draws the line in practice:

"Cannabinoid oils (like CBD oil) can not be sold in ingestible products in Washington State. This means CBD gummies, candies, tinctures, foods, beverages, joints, and vapes cannot be legally sold in Washington."

By contrast, WSDA states that WSDA-licensed food processors can use other hemp products in food — hulled hemp seeds, hemp seed protein powder and hemp seed oil — provided they comply with all other requirements (Run 1 Part 6.9.3; WSDA Hemp FAQ, opened via search result). Run 1's flag stands: Verify the current food-ingredient status of any specific hemp extract at Part 7 build time, as WSDA's December 2025 rulemaking discussion may affect related rules — no final food-ingredient change was located in this run.

Hemp extract facility certification (WAC 16-171, via search-index excerpts — Verify against current WAC text):

"'Hemp extract certification' means a certification issued by the department to a hemp extract facility manufacturing hemp extract for export to other states, which certifies the hemp extract facility's compliance with Washington state's inspection and sanitation requirements." — WAC 16-171-110

"'Hemp extract' means a substance or compound intended for human ingestion that is derived from, or made by, manufacturing hemp." — WAC 16-171-110

"Hemp extract is not an approved food ingredient in Washington state. A Hemp Extract certificate is issued by WSDA to a hemp extract facility manufacturing hemp extract for export to other states…" — WSDA Hemp Extract Voluntary Certification Criteria (WSDA document, secondary reproduction of the criteria)

Qualification (WAC 16-171-120, search-index excerpt): application → WSDA inspection → facility must be in substantial compliance (score ≥90 points on the WAC 16-171-140 criteria, no uncorrected critical violation — WAC 16-171-150) before issuance; annual renewal; fees new $1,400 / inspection $3,000 / renewal $1,500. Statutory authority: RCW 69.07.020 and 69.07.220; WSR 22-13-038, filed 7 June 2022, eff. 8 July 2022. ESB 5372 directed WSDA to regulate hemp extract processing the same as other food processing and to issue this certification in lieu of a food processing licence.

What is NOT a hemp licence (do not list these as hemp licences): (a) an LCB cannabis licence — it is a cannabis licence that may also handle hemp-derived THC inside the LCB system (§7.8); (b) a WSDA food processor licence — a general food licence; (c) a USDA licence — not applicable while the state plan stands; (d) a hemp retail licence — no WSDA hemp retail licence exists in ch. 15.140 / ch. 16-306; general retail of non-THC hemp products needs no WSDA hemp licence, and general retail of detectable-THC products is prohibited (§7.8).

7.8 The LCB role for hemp-derived THC products — SB 5367 channel rule, reconciled exactly with Run 1 Part 1.5

This section does not change Run 1 Part 1.5; it restates it with the enrolled-bill text now located, so the parent can check the two parts line by line.

Enrolled bill: ESSB 5367, Chapter 365, Laws of 2023, "Cannabis Products — Various Provisions", effective 23 July 2023. It amended RCW 15.140.020, 69.50.326 and 69.50.346, reenacted/amended RCW 69.50.101, and added a new section to ch. 69.50 RCW. Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2023-24/Htm/Bills/Session%20Laws/Senate/5367-S2.SL.htm — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Verbatim operative definitions (enrolled bill, §1–§2):

"'Cannabis products' means useable cannabis, cannabis concentrates, and cannabis-infused products as defined in this section, including any product intended to be consumed or absorbed inside the body by any means including inhalation, ingestion, or insertion, with any detectable amount of THC." — RCW 69.50.101 (as amended by ESSB 5367 §1)

"'Cannabis products' also means any product containing only THC content." / "'Cannabis products' does not include cannabis health and beauty aids as defined in RCW 69.50.575 or products approved by the United States food and drug administration." — same subsection

Licence-only rule (Run 1 Part 1.5, retained verbatim in substance): a cannabis product may be manufactured, sold or distributed only by an LCB licensee (RCW 69.50.3251(1)), or under a tribal agreement (RCW 43.06.490 — tribal compacts are Part 10). Sale without a licence is prohibited and subject to criminal sanctions (LCB THC guide, May 2025, as cited in Run 1). LCB's May 2025 guide states expressly: "The sale of any product(s) containing detectable amounts of THC without a valid cannabis license is prohibited by state law." (Run 1 Part 1.5.)

What this bars outside LCB retail (unchanged from Run 1): hemp-derived delta-9 edibles and beverages, delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THC-O and similar converted products, and smokable THCA flower sold as hemp — all contain detectable THC and are intended for consumption/absorption. A "hemp THC seltzer" in a grocery/convenience store, or shipped from out of state to a Washington consumer outside LCB retail, is prohibited under RCW 69.50.3251.

What remains in general retail (unchanged from Run 1): THC-free / non-detectable-THC hemp consumables and CBD products, and CHABA topicals (≤0.3% THC, topical-only, non-ingestible, RCW 69.50.575). CBD added to food/beverages or sold as a dietary supplement is not permitted in Washington on WSDA's stated position (see §7.6).

Synthetic / converted cannabinoids (unchanged from Run 1):

"The producing, processing, manufacturing, or sale of any synthetically derived, or completely synthetic, cannabinoid is prohibited, except for products approved by the United States food and drug administration." — RCW 69.50.3251(3) (2023 c 365 §5)

LCB Policy Statement PS21-01 (28 Apr 2021) and Interpretive Statement IS-21-02 (22 July 2021) had already taken the position that conversion of CBD/hemp to delta-8, delta-9 or any other "marijuana" compound not defined in RCW/WAC was not an allowable processor practice; SB 5367 then codified the channel ban and the synthetic ban. Those statements are LCB guidance, not statute — cite them as such. Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/rules/Statements/IS-21-02_MJ_Processor_License_Allowable_Practices.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Run 1 citation, retained).

"THC concentration" broadening — Run 1 Verify, partially advanced: Run 1 flagged that the exact post-SB 5367 definition subsection was not opened in full. The enrolled bill (ESSB 5367) confirms the bill amended the "THC concentration" definition to remove delta-9-only language, and secondary summaries (e.g. Senate Democrats' bill summary, 26 Apr 2023) describe it as adding "cannabinoids like delta-8 and delta-10" to the regulated market. The verbatim current RCW 69.50.101 "THC concentration" subsection text was still not opened in full in this run — the parent should open https://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=69.50.101 at final audit and quote that subsection verbatim rather than relying on the secondary "delta-8/delta-10/THCA list" formulation. Do not present the secondary isomer list as the statutory text.

Inside the LCB system (unchanged from Run 1): age 21+ for any cannabis product, including hemp-derived THC sold through LCB retail (RCW 69.50.360, WAC 314-55-079(1)). Testing/labelling under WAC 314-55-102 / 314-55-105, serving/package limits in WAC 314-55-095 (10 mg per serving / 100 mg per package general rule; low-dose liquid transaction rule in RCW 69.50.360: individual units ≤4 mg THC → 200 mg THC transaction limit). Products must be produced within Washington's I-502 system to be sold by LCB retailers — out-of-state hemp THC may not be imported into LCB retail as a hemp product (LCB THC guide, Run 1). No 18+ or 21+ general-retail hemp-THC age rule exists because general-retail hemp-THC sale is prohibited.

CBD additive into LCB products (RCW 69.50.326, Run 1 citation retained): licensed producers/processors may use a CBD product as an additive to enhance CBD concentration; outside-system CBD is allowed only if it (a) is not cannabis/cannabis product, (b) is not a synthetic cannabinoid, and (c) has been tested for contaminants/toxins by a lab accredited/certified under ch. 69.50. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/Rcw/default.aspx?cite=69.50.326 — Accessed 2026-11-01 (Run 1 citation; text via search result in Run 1 — Verify verbatim at final audit).

LCB testing at co-located sites (RCW 15.140.120(2), opened via official PDF excerpt):

"Notwithstanding the rulemaking provisions of RCW 15.140.030(2), if a cannabis producer or cannabis processor licensed by the liquor and cannabis board under chapter 69.50 RCW is engaged in producing or processing hemp at the same location for which they are licensed to produce or process cannabis, the liquor and cannabis board may test samples represented as hemp that are obtained from a location licensed for cannabis production or cannabis processing for the sole purpose of validating THC content of products represented as hemp. Any product with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration exceeding 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis is considered cannabis and is subject to the provisions of chapter 69.50 RCW."

This is the only LCB testing role over hemp in ch. 15.140 as opened. It is a validation power at co-located LCB premises, not a general WSDA-hemp inspection power and not a separate hemp licence.

LCB role summary — what LCB does and does not do for hemp:

LCB function Yes / No
Licence the manufacture/sale/distribution of any detectable-THC consumable (including hemp-derived) Yes — via existing producer/processor/retailer licences (RCW 69.50.3251)
Issue a separate "hemp THC" licence No such licence type was located
Test hemp at a co-located LCB-licensed site to validate THC content Yes — RCW 15.140.120(2), quoted above
Licence WSDA hemp production, inspect WSDA registered land areas, or issue WSDA THC certification No — WSDA only
Set the WSDA hemp producer fee, sampling window or testing fee No — WAC 16-306 only

7.9 Pending rulemaking and the federal 12 November / 11 December 2026 dates — no Washington conforming law located

  • WSDA ch. 16-306 rulemaking (pending, not adopted): CR-101 filed 17 November 2025, WSR 25-23-059 (published 2 December 2025). WSDA's rulemaking page (opened) states the purposes include: increasing clarity between programme rules and operations, increasing programme efficiency, reducing cost variability for licensees, removing unnecessary portions of the chapter, eliminating time and mileage fees and replacing them, with the harvest inspection fee, by a new "harvest certification fee" encompassing the whole administrative process from scheduling to certification, and aligning the definition of THC testing with the USDA definition of Total THC for clarity. Proposed adoption date on the page: "[To be determined]". The Environmental Justice Assessment for the rule is scheduled to conclude by 31 December 2026 (WSDA blog/rulemaking material, search-index excerpt — Verify against the rulemaking page at final audit). Source: https://agr.wa.gov/services/rulemaking/wac-16-306-hemp-111725 — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Do not present any of this as current law. Until WSDA files a CR-103 adoption, the current rules are the WAC 16-306 text opened in §7.2–§7.5 (last update 28 Oct 2024, certified 27 Nov 2024), including the $200 + $40/hr + mileage inspection fee and the 30-day sampling window.
  • WSDA Hemp page warning (opened): "There have been a variety of changes in the hemp marketplace, as well as changes in the state's definition of cannabis, and the federal government's definitions on consumable cannabinoids. We recommend looking at this educational material from the Washington State Liquor and Cannabis Board, and researching the upcoming federal changes on ingestible manufactured hemp products before getting licensed." — Source: https://agr.wa.gov/Departments/Agricultural-Products/Hemp — Accessed 2026-11-01. This is a warning to research, not a statement of a new Washington rule.
  • Federal dates (given, not re-researched): PL 119-37 §781 eff. 12 Nov 2026; PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 Dec 2026; after 11 Dec 2026 the full total-THC definition + 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA DEA-lab enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026.
  • Washington position at 1 Nov 2026 (this run, same as Run 1): no Washington statute, WAC amendment, WSDA rule or LCB guidance was located that (a) adopts the federal total-THC definition into RCW 15.140.020, (b) adopts a 0.4 mg per-container cap into state law, or (c) states how WSDA will treat a crop/product that is hemp under the old delta-9 test but not hemp under the new total-THC test after 11 Dec 2026. The pending ch. 16-306 alignment is a testing-definition alignment, not a per-container cap.
  • Practical consequences (all Verify after 11 Dec 2026, as in Run 1): (i) whether WSDA-licensed hemp material remains "hemp" federally after 11 Dec 2026; (ii) interstate shipment of Washington hemp; (iii) whether LCB retailers may continue to source any hemp-derived ingredient that is no longer federally hemp. Because SB 5367 already channels any detectable-THC consumable into LCB retail, the federal per-container cap does not create a new Washington retail channel. Do not present the federal cap as already being Washington law.
  • What to watch: WSDA ch. 16-306 CR-103 adoption notice; LCB guidance on hemp-derived ingredients in LCB products after 11 Dec 2026; any 2027 bill conforming RCW 15.140.020 / 69.50.101 to the federal definition; USDA DEA-lab enforcement position after 31 Dec 2026.

7.10 Part 7 — what was NOT found (Unavailable, not estimated)

  • No 2025 or 2026 WSDA producer licence total; no current processor registrant count (last: 7, March 2024, secondary).
  • No current WSDA producer list rows (page list not exposed in text fetch).
  • No hemp acreage cap, producer tier/class, or per-acre fee in WAC 16-306.
  • No WSDA hemp retail licence; no standalone LCB hemp-THC licence; no current hemp R&D licence (pilot defunct).
  • No current modification/late fee amount visible in the opened WAC 16-306-140 extract (see §7.4).
  • No Washington CBD potency cap for general-retail non-THC CBD was located in Run 1, and none was located in this run — Verify; do not assert one.
  • No standalone Washington hemp-consumable testing/labelling rule beyond WSDA producer testing and the food-extract provisions was located in Run 1, and none was located in this run — Verify.

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.0 Headline: strict verified count = 0

Properties meeting the strict verified standard in this run: ZERO.

The strict standard applied here, exactly as commissioned, requires all of the following in the listing itself as opened on 2026-11-01:

  1. Washington location (not Vancouver BC, Portland OR, Washington County OR, or any other same-name place);
  2. Active listing status shown on the listing itself (not "Contract Pending", "Pending", "Sold", "Under Contract", "Listing removed", or blank);
  3. Recent — listed/updated in 2026 and confirmed on a page opened (or a current crawl) on 2026-11-01, not a years-old brochure or a months-old cached copy with a blank "Last Updated" field;
  4. Cannabis-zoned or cannabis-marketed — a zoning designation that permits cannabis use, or a listing that markets the property/facility for cannabis use (a general industrial/retail building merely described as "suitable for" many uses, including cannabis, is treated as marketed, not zoned, and is flagged as such);
  5. Street address confirmed in the listing itself (city/region alone, e.g. "Puget Sound, WA", does not count);
  6. Price confirmed in the listing itself.

No listing opened in this run satisfies all six elements simultaneously. The binding constraints are, in order: (a) most business-for-sale listings deliberately withhold the street address (NDA-gated); (b) the two listings that do give a full address + price + cannabis marketing are either contract-pending (Quilcene) or rest on a months-old cached copy with no current "Last Updated" value (Seattle Lake City Way); (c) the remaining address-confirmed listings are stale (Blaine, 2017 market data), license-only with relocation required (Raymond), or a sold home whose street name merely contains "Canna" (Shelton — excluded, see §8.4).

This 0 is a statement about what could be verified from publicly opened listings on 2026-11-01, not a claim that no cannabis-zoned property exists in Washington, and not a claim that no property is for sale off-market or behind an NDA. Do not convert it into "no properties available."

County/city zoning verification Not attempted parcel-by-parcel for B1–B8 Verify — no B-row property was checked against its city/county zoning map or LCB premises record in this run, because no B-row property (except B1/B2) even supplies an address to check. For B1 (Seattle) and B2 (Quilcene/Jefferson County), a zoning-map check is the recommended follow-up, but it cannot cure the recency (B1) or pending-status (B2) failures.

A note on why so many listings fail on address: Washington cannabis business sales are routinely marketed as licence + leasehold + FF&E, with the premises address withheld behind an NDA/buyer-qualification process (see B4, B5, B6, and the Raymond NDA requirement). That is a market practice, not a research failure — but it means a public, address-confirmed, cannabis-zoned property inventory cannot be built from open listings alone on 2026-11-01. A county assessor / LCB premises cross-check (Part 11 / Part 5 routes) would be needed for a verified inventory, and that is a different exercise from a listings search.

8.5 Part 8 — consistency with Run 1

  • Run 1 Part 3 (local rules) explains why a zoning designation matters: the statewide 1,000-foot buffer (reducible to 100 feet except schools/playgrounds), local bans/moratoriums, and LCB premises approval all mean a property's cannabis usability depends on its specific jurisdiction and buffer compliance, not merely on being marketed for cannabis. No Part 8 row asserts buffer compliance — none was checked.
  • Run 1 Part 4 (licence types) explains why most Part 8 rows are business sales, not property sales: the general LCB window is closed, so the only route to a new licence is social equity (also closed, last window 2 June–1 July 2025) or acquiring an existing licence — hence the licence + leasehold market in §8.2. Ownership caps (5 retail, 3 producer, 3 processor — Run 1 Part 4) also apply to any purchaser of a B-row business; they are not re-litigated here.
  • Run 1 Part 6 (market data) price context is not repeated as property valuation: a business asking price (e.g. $2,200,000 for a leased Seattle retail business) is not a real-estate price and must not be compared with a fee-simple property price (e.g. $789,000 for the Lake City building).
