Parts 9–10 — Regulators & History, Tribal & Interstate

Washington Cannabis & Hemp — Run 2, Parts 9–10 ONLY

Research date / cut-off: 1 November 2026. All facts: Accessed 2026-11-01 unless a different publication date is stated in the line.

How to read this report — Run 1 consistency, and a correction to the brief

  • Run 1 anchors carried forward exactly (no change): 37% excise tax (RCW 69.50.535); I-692 (1998), I-502 (6 November 2012), first licensed retail sales 8 July 2014; SB 5052 (2015) / medical merger effective 1 July 2016; SB 5123 (2023) employment law, effective 1 January 2024; SB 5367 intoxicating-hemp / detectable-THC rule; EHB 2681 fees $1,781 from 1 July 2026; no adult home grow; delivery prohibited (WAC 314-55-079(5)); Schedule III / 280E for medical-endorsement sales and for tribal-compact medical activity = Verify; federal hemp dates 12 November / 11 December 2026.
  • Run 1 conflicts preserved, not resolved here: FY2025 excise $438,413,557 (LCB) vs $438,413,557 (LCB); FY2024 $454,742,324 (LCB) vs $454,742,324 (LCB); social-equity issued counts 16 (LCB table) vs 9 retail (JLARC, Feb 2025) vs 17 (secondary, Sept 2026); DOH patient jump 16,066 (Jan 2026) → 27,749 (July 2026), unexplained; buyer-residency (Idaho/Oregon) share and aggregate tribal-compact sales = Unavailable in Run 1 Part 6 — neither is invented here.
  • Correction to the brief's citation (important for the combined file): the task brief names "RCW 43.06.455 / 69.50 provisions" for tribal cannabis. RCW 43.06.455 is the cigarette-tax contract statute, not cannabis. The cannabis compact statute, opened directly in this session, is RCW 43.06.490 ("Cannabis agreements—Federally recognized Indian tribes—Tribal cannabis tax—Tax exemption"), enacted by Chapter 207, Laws of 2015 (HB 2000). Run 1 Part 1 cited RCW 43.06.490 correctly; this report follows Run 1, not the brief's shorthand. RCW 43.06.455 is mentioned only to prevent confusion.
  • Source method, stated plainly: Pages marked Opened were opened with browser.open in this session and read directly. Pages marked Search-indexed were returned as official-page text by browser.search but could not be re-opened in this session because browser.open returned a rate-limit (429) after the first batch of opens; those facts are flagged Verify by direct re-open before republication, per the sourcing rules. The LCB October 2025 compact template (.docx) returned no extractable content on the one attempt made and was not retried (per the session instruction on failed opens); its terms are therefore Verify, and this report relies instead on the statute text (opened) and on individual compact/ordinance texts (opened or search-indexed, as labelled). No personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced; agency mailboxes and public-records numbers that appear on the LCB's own public page are official public contacts and are identified as such.
  • Place-name care: Vancouver WA is not Portland OR (across the Columbia River); Spokane WA / Spokane Valley WA are not Coeur d'Alene ID; Clarkston WA is not Lewiston ID (across the Snake River); Walla Walla WA is not Milton-Freewater OR; Blaine WA is the U.S. side of the Peace Arch / Pacific Highway crossings to Surrey, British Columbia — not Vancouver, British Columbia, itself.

PART 9 — REGULATORS AND HISTORY

9.1 Every state agency regulating cannabis and hemp

Agency Cannabis / hemp role (as sourced) Website / official public contact route Source + Accessed
Washington State Liquor and Cannabis Board (LCB, formerly Liquor Control Board; renamed Liquor and Cannabis Board effective 24 July 2015) Primary cannabis regulator: licensing of producers, processors, retailers, transporters and research licensees; rulemaking under WAC 314-55; enforcement and inspection (WAC 314-55-185); administration/collection of the 37% cannabis excise tax; social-equity programme; negotiation of tribal cannabis compacts under delegation from the Governor; traceability (CCRS, the state-built system that replaced Leaf Data Systems). lcb.wa.gov; Cannabis Compacts page: https://lcb.wa.gov/tribal/cannabis_compacts — Tribal Liaison agency mailbox and LCB Public Records Office number as published on that page (official public contacts, not personal contacts) LCB Cannabis Compacts page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01; LCB history page — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01 (see §9.2)
Washington State Department of Health (DOH) Medical cannabis programme administration: Medical Cannabis Authorization Database (RCW 69.51A.230); medical marijuana consultant certification (WAC 246-72); product-compliance / DOH-compliant product standards for the medical excise/sales-tax exemptions; Memoranda of Agreement (MOAs) with compacted tribes that wish to serve patients and access the Database (see §10.1.5). DOH does not issue a separate medical dispensary licence — medical sales run through LCB-licensed retailers with a medical endorsement. doh.wa.gov; medical-cannabis agency mailbox as published on the LCB compacts page LCB Cannabis Compacts page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01
Washington State Department of Agriculture (WSDA) Hemp programme under RCW 15.140 and WAC 16-306: hemp producer licensing, THC certification / pre-harvest sampling, transport documentation (WAC 16-306-130), and the USDA-approved state hemp plan (Run 1 anchor; see §9.2). WSDA does not license LCB cannabis businesses. Note for the combined file: a 2021 Task Force topic asked about shifting primary regulation of cannabis production from LCB to WSDA — that was a recommendation topic, not a transfer that occurred (see §9.1.1). agr.wa.gov (WSDA hemp pages) WAC 16-306-130 — Search-indexed official text, Accessed 2026-11-01; Run 1 Part 1 (USDA-approved state plan)
Washington State Department of Revenue (DOR) State and local sales/use tax and B&O tax on cannabis businesses (the excise tax itself is administered/collected through LCB, per Run 1 Part 2); retail-tax compact location codes that distribute a portion of state tax revenues from certain retail transactions to compacting tribes (see §10.1.6); consultation on tribal compact tax provisions (LCB compact workflow: DOR reviews tax provisions). dor.wa.gov; Retail taxes — compacts: https://dor.wa.gov/taxes-rates/retail-sales-tax/retail-taxes-compacts DOR compacts page — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01; LCB compact workflow — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01
Washington State Patrol (WSP) General law enforcement; fingerprint / criminal-history comparison for LCB licence applicants (fingerprints submitted to WSP and the FBI, WAC 314-55-310, transporter rule — Search-indexed); seizure of unlicensed hemp found off a registered land area without WSDA THC certification (WAC 16-306-130(2): "subject to seizure by the Washington state patrol or any law enforcement officer"). WSP is not the cannabis licensing regulator. wsp.wa.gov (website name only; no contact detail sourced in this track — Verify) WAC 314-55-310 and WAC 16-306-130 — Search-indexed official text, Accessed 2026-11-01
Governor's Office / Governor's Office of Indian Affairs (GOIA) The Governor holds the compacting authority under RCW 43.06.490 and delegates negotiation to LCB; final compact approval is by the Governor's signature (LCB workflow, step 10). GOIA is the state's government-to-government coordination office and the source of the "29 federally recognized tribes" count (see §10.1.1); the Centennial Accord (GOIA + Attorney General) is the state's guiding government-to-government policy document, per the Aug 2020 LCB caucus summary (secondary). governor.wa.gov; goia.wa.gov — GOIA tribal directory: https://goia.wa.gov/tribal-directory/federally-recognized-tribes-washington-state GOIA FAQ — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01; LCB workflow — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01
Washington State Department of Commerce Social-equity technical assistance grants and mentorship (Commerce administers the grant programme funded from the Dedicated Cannabis Account; Run 1 Part 4: $1,100,000 per year under the 2020 law, with later/conflicting scopes flagged there — do not add the scopes together). Commerce is not a cannabis licensing agency. commerce.wa.gov (website name only; grant detail in Run 1 Part 4) Run 1 Part 4; LCB social-equity history — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01
Washington State Office of Equity (WA Equity) Created 2020 (HB 1783, signed April 2020; funded in the 2021 session after an initial pandemic funding cut) to promote equitable access across government. Its cannabis role is consultation / equity lens, not licensing: a Dec 2024 House Regulated Substances & Gaming briefing covered "consultation by the Office of Equity" alongside LCB equity licensing and Commerce grants (secondary summary). The Office of Equity must not be confused with the Social Equity in Cannabis Task Force (below) or with LCB's Social Equity Programme. healthequity.wa.gov (Task Force materials were hosted there; Office of Equity site name only in this track — Verify current URL) Cannabis Observer organisation summary — Secondary, Accessed 2026-11-01; House RSG briefing summary — Secondary, Accessed 2026-11-01
Social Equity in Cannabis Task Force (SECTF) — expired, not a current regulator Legislative task force created by E2SHB 2870 (2020), expanded by ESHB 1443 (2021); first meeting October 2020; final recommendations due 9 December 2022; statute expired 30 June 2023 (RCW 69.50.336). Purpose: recommend to LCB a social-equity programme for issuance/reissuance of retail, processor and producer licences, and advise the Governor and Legislature. It was advisory only — LCB issues licences, and a third-party contractor scored applications (see Run 1 Part 4). Final Report (Dec 2022) hosted at healthequity.wa.gov (see §9.2). No current website (expired body); final report: https://healthequity.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2022-12/SECTF%20Final%20Report.pdf (as cited in a 2025 Court of Appeals brief — Verify by direct open) RCW 69.50.336 summary — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01; LCB social-equity history — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01; WA Court of Appeals brief (No. 61413-8, 2025) — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01
Other bodies with a real, narrower role Office of the Attorney General: legal advice / review in compact negotiations and LCB enforcement litigation; co-manager (with GOIA) of the Centennial Accord framework. Washington State Institute for Public Policy (WSIPP): legislatively mandated I-502 evaluation reports (10-year review, June 2023; public-health outcomes, Sept 2023) — research, not regulation. Local governments (cities/counties): zoning, buffer reductions and local bans/moratoriums (Run 1 Part 3) — not state agencies. Tribal regulators (e.g. Tulalip Tribal Cannabis Agency) regulate inside Indian Country under compacts/tribal codes — see §10.1.4; they are sovereign governments, not state agencies. U.S. Department of Agriculture (federal): approves Washington's hemp plan; FDA (federal): food/drug authority over hemp-derived products — neither is a Washington agency, listed here only to prevent misattribution. atg.wa.gov; wsipp.wa.gov (website names only) WSIPP reports — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01

Official public contacts sourced from the opened LCB compacts page (agency contacts, not personal contacts): LCB Tribal Liaison — Tribal.Liaison@lcb.wa.gov; LCB Public Records Office (for copies of specific compacts) — (360) 664-1753, lcbpublicrecords@lcb.wa.gov; DOH medical-cannabis MOA contact — medicalcannabis@doh.wa.gov, (360) 236-4819. Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/tribal/cannabis_compacts — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01. No other agency phone/email was sourced in this track (see Verify log item 18).

9.1.1 What the Office of Equity / Task Force do NOT do (to prevent combined-file errors)

  • The Task Force did not issue, score or revoke any licence. LCB issues licences; the Task Force recommended, and a contractor scored (Run 1 Part 4; Court of Appeals brief summary, search-indexed).
  • The Office of Equity did not replace the Task Force and does not run the Social Equity Programme. The programme sits with LCB (licensing) and Commerce (grants).
  • No transfer of cannabis production regulation from LCB to WSDA occurred. The Task Force was asked to consider the social-equity impact of such a shift (ESHB 1443 topic list) — that is all.

3 Nov 1998 I-692 approved — Medical Use of Marijuana Act Affirmative defence only at first; no licensed businesses, no dispensaries. 2010 I-1068 (Marijuana Reform Act) fails to qualify Would have removed state criminal/civil penalties for adults 18+. Needed 241,153 signatures by 2 July 2010; sponsors announced 1 July 2010 that enough had not been collected (approx. 200,000 reported). No vote was held — there is no I-1068 vote percentage. 6 Nov 2012 I-502 approved — adult-use legalisation Initiative to the Legislature; filed with SOS 10 May 2011; 340,000+ signatures submitted 29 Dec 2011; Legislature took no action, so it went to voters. Certified no later than 6 Dec 2012; possession by adults 21+ became legal under state law 6 Dec 2012, but cultivation/sale/gifting remained illegal until licensing. Vote: §9.3 (official SOS page opened). SOS results page — Opened, Accessed 2026-11-01; HistoryLink — Secondary, Accessed 2026-11-01

HB 2136 (signed 30 June 2015, effective 1 July 2015): replaced three 25% tier taxes (producer→processor, processor→retailer, retailer→customer) with a single 37% retail excise tax on the buyer. Seattle: 77% support/inclined; Eastern Washington: 41% support/inclined, 48% definitely opposed. Old, pre-vote, small sample — context only. Do not convert ICPS use rates into support rates. Polling gap: No Washington-specific, post-2020, methodologically complete legalisation-support poll (pollster + field dates + sample + wording + margin) was located in this track beyond the Civiqs compilation figure above. That gap is stated, not filled.

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.1 Washington's 29 federally recognised tribes and cannabis compacts

10.1.1 The 29 — and the master-list problem (read before the table)

  • Count confirmed: "There are 29 federally recognized tribes in Washington State." — Governor's Office of Indian Affairs (GOIA) FAQ, search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01. The full GOIA list (alphabetical in GOIA's directory) is: Chehalis (Confederated Tribes of the Chehalis Reservation), Colville (Confederated Tribes of the Colville Reservation), Cowlitz, Hoh, Jamestown S'Klallam, Kalispel, Lower Elwha Klallam, Lummi Nation, Makah, Muckleshoot, Nisqually, Nooksack, Port Gamble S'Klallam, Puyallup, Quileute, Quinault, Samish, Sauk-Suiattle, Shoalwater Bay, Skokomish, Snoqualmie, Spokane, Squaxin Island, Stillaguamish, Suquamish, Swinomish, Tulalip, Upper Skagit, Yakama (Confederated Tribes and Bands of the Yakama Nation). Note: GOIA's directory page also lists neighbouring-state tribes (e.g. Umatilla, Warm Springs) in its wider table — those are not Washington tribes and are excluded here.
  • No current official master list of cannabis compacts was located. The LCB Cannabis Compacts page (opened) names no tribe at all and no count; it says only that the Governor is authorised, negotiation is delegated to LCB, a template (updated Oct 2025) exists, and copies of specific compacts are available from the LCB Public Records Office. LCB's Tribal Resources page likewise says compacts placed on a Board agenda will be public, but publishes no master list. Consequence: this report does NOT assert a 2026 total number of compacted tribes. The last dated totals located are: 11 compacts (Apr 2019), 16 of 29 (11 Aug 2020), 17th (Chehalis, Nov 2020), 18th (Nooksack, 17 Mar 2021). Everything after March 2021 is evidenced tribe-by-tribe below, with its own date/status flag. Any combined-file sentence of the form "X of 29 tribes have compacts (2026)" would be unverified on this evidence and must not be written.
  • Absence of a compact is NOT a ban. A tribe with no compact evidenced here may (a) have a compact whose date was not located, (b) be in negotiation, (c) have chosen not to enter the cannabis market, or (d) prohibit cannabis on its lands. Only Yakama has a ban evidenced in the sources located (see §10.1.8), and even that ban's current tribal-code text was not located. Do not infer bans for any other tribe.

10.1.2 Legal framework — verbatim statute (opened)

RCW 43.06.490 (opened, https://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=43.06.490 — Accessed 2026-11-01). Operative text, quoted verbatim (subsections (1)–(4)):

"(1) The governor may enter into agreements with federally recognized Indian tribes concerning cannabis. Cannabis agreements may address any cannabis-related issue that involves both state and tribal interests or otherwise has an impact on tribal-state relations. Such agreements may include, but are not limited to, the following provisions and subject matter: (a) Criminal and civil law enforcement; (b) Regulatory issues related to the commercial production, processing, sale, and possession of cannabis, and processed cannabis products, for both recreational and medical purposes; (c) Medical and pharmaceutical research involving cannabis; (d) Taxation in accordance with subsection (2) of this section; (e) Any tribal immunities or preemption of state law regarding the production, processing, or marketing of cannabis; and (f) Dispute resolution, including the use of mediation or other nonjudicial process."

"(2)(a) Each cannabis agreement adopted under this section must provide for a tribal cannabis tax that is at least one hundred percent of the state cannabis excise tax imposed under RCW 69.50.535 and state and local sales and use taxes on sales of cannabis. Cannabis agreements apply to sales in which tribes, tribal enterprises, or tribal member-owned businesses (i) deliver or cause delivery to be made to or receive delivery from a cannabis producer, processor, or retailer licensed under chapter 69.50 RCW or (ii) physically transfer possession of the cannabis from the seller to the buyer within Indian country."

"(2)(b) The tribe may allow an exemption from tax for sales to the tribe, tribal enterprises, tribal member-owned businesses, or tribal members, on cannabis grown, produced, or processed within its Indian country, or for activities to the extent they are exempt under state or federal law from the state cannabis excise tax imposed under RCW 69.50.535 or state and local sales or use taxes on sales of cannabis. Medical cannabis products used in the course of medical treatments by a clinic, hospital, or similar facility owned and operated by a federally recognized Indian tribe within its Indian country may be exempted from tax under the terms of an agreement entered into under this section."

"(3) Any cannabis agreement relating to the production, processing, and sale of cannabis in Indian country, whether for recreational or medical purposes, must address the following issues: (a) Preservation of public health and safety; (b) Ensuring the security of production, processing, retail, and research facilities; and (c) Cross-border commerce in cannabis."

"(4) The governor may delegate the power to negotiate cannabis agreements to the state liquor and cannabis board. In conducting such negotiations, the state liquor and cannabis board must, when necessary, consult with the governor and/or the department of revenue."

Legislative intent note on the same page (2015 c 207; re-enacted in substance 2025 c 58): agreements "will facilitate and promote a cooperative and mutually beneficial relationship," "enhance public health and safety, ensure a lawful and well-regulated cannabis market, encourage economic development, and provide fiscal benefits to both the tribes and the state," "particularly in light of the fact that federal Indian law precludes the state from enforcing its civil regulatory laws in Indian country."

Companion state-law rules (Run 1 anchors, repeated for cross-check):

  • RCW 69.50.331(8)(f) (Run 1 Part 3): LCB "may not issue a license for any premises within Indian country … without the consent of the federally recognized tribe associated with the reservation or Indian country." (Text as returned in Run 1 — Verify verbatim in Part 11.)
  • RCW 69.50.360(4) / tribal purchase rules and RCW 69.50.3251 (tribal-agreement exception to LCB-licensee-only sales): Run 1 Part 1 states the rule in one sentence as: a product with any detectable THC "may be manufactured/sold/distributed only by an LCB licensee (RCW 69.50.3251(1)), or under a tribal agreement (RCW 43.06.490)." That sentence is preserved here; verbatim RCW 69.50.3251 text was not re-opened in this track — Verify.
  • Federal-law caveat in every compact located: the Suquamish compact (opened) states that "pursuant to federal law, 21 U.S.C. § 812, marijuana is a Schedule I controlled substance and that this Compact does not protect the sales or regulation of marijuana in Indian Country from federal law." That 2015 wording predates the given 28 April 2026 Schedule III rule for state-licensed medical marijuana; whether current compacts/template update that sentence is Verify (template not extracted; see §10.1.7).

10.1.5 Compact terms — tax, testing, trade, locations, medical (with verbatim ordinance/compact text)

A. Tax in lieu of state excise (statute + compacts agree). - Statute (opened): tribal tax "at least one hundred percent of the state cannabis excise tax imposed under RCW 69.50.535 and state and local sales and use taxes" (RCW 43.06.490(2)(a), verbatim in §10.1.2). The tribe keeps the tribal tax; state excise does not apply to compact sales activity as such (Suquamish summary: "No state taxes will apply to tribal marijuana sales activity" — secondary, but consistent with the in-lieu structure). - Chehalis ordinance, §9.50.060 (opened, verbatim in substance): "(a) There is a Tribal Marijuana Tax equal to one hundred percent (100%) of the state tax then in effect levied on all Commercial Marijuana Activity ('Tribal Marijuana Tax'). (b) The Tribal Marijuana Tax must be remitted to the Tribe on a quarterly basis. … (c) The Tribe will use the proceeds of such tax for Essential Government Services, including but not limited to land acquisition. (d) No other tax besides the Tribal Marijuana Tax may be imposed on Commercial Marijuana Activity unless determined by the Business Committee." Exemptions (§9.50.060(e)): sales to the Tribe/CTE/tribal members of product grown/produced/processed within Indian Country; activities otherwise exempt under state/federal law; and medical products used in tribal clinic/hospital treatment (mirroring RCW 43.06.490(2)(b)). - Suquamish code, §11.10.6 (search-indexed, tribal site): "(a) There is a tribal marijuana tax equal to one hundred percent (100%) of the state tax then in effect levied on all commercial marijuana activity. (b) The tribal marijuana tax must be remitted to the Tribe on a quarterly basis. (c) The Tribe will use the proceeds of such tax for essential government services. (d) No other tax besides the tribal marijuana tax may be imposed on commercial marijuana activity." — same structure as Chehalis. - Swinomish code, §15-08.110 (search-indexed): "There is levied a Tribal Cannabis Tax on the retail sale of cannabis products. The Tribal Cannabis Tax shall be equal to one hundred (100) percent of the State marijuana excise tax set forth in RCW 69.50.535" (plus Swinomish retail sales tax under its own Title 17, ch. 3, which is separate and "not part of the measure of the sales and use tax" — Verify how that interacts with the RCW 43.06.490 "at least 100% of excise + sales/use" floor before quoting a Swinomish combined rate). - "Essential Government Services" (Suquamish compact, opened): "services provided by the Tribe including, but not limited to, administration, public facilities, fire, police, health, education, elder care, social services, sewer, water, environmental and land use, transportation, utility services, community development, and economic development." (Compact §IV.D, opened.)

B. Testing, packaging, labelling, traceability. - Chehalis ordinance (opened): "Before sale to any retailer, marijuana products must be tested by a tribally or state-accredited lab that follows quality assurance testing protocols at least as restrictive as state law" (§9.50.070(e)); "All marijuana products must be packaged and labeled in accordance with CTE and/or Business Committee policy" (§9.50.070(f)); packaging/labelling "designed to be especially appealing to children are prohibited"; "All marijuana products sold by CTE to a state licensee must meet the testing, packaging, and labeling requirements otherwise required under state law. Such sales must be input into the state's traceability system" (§9.50.080, opened text). - Suquamish compact (opened, §V.B.3): purchases from a state-licensed producer/processor "will be verified upon delivery in Indian Country, and confirmation of receipt will be made by executing the delivery invoice. The Retailer will input or cause to be input all delivered purchases into the State's tracking system within 24 hours of making any such delivery." Inter-tribal transfers (Tribe/enterprise to another compacted Washington tribe's government/enterprise/member, and vice versa) "will be recorded in either the [State tracking system or tribal system — sentence truncated in the opened extract at line 348; Verify the full sentence before quoting]." - Suquamish code (search-indexed): same testing rule as Chehalis ("tribally or state-licensed lab … at least as restrictive as state law"); sales to a state licensee must meet state testing/packaging/labelling and "must be input into the state's traceability system." - Lummi compact (search-indexed): "All transactions between the Tribe or Tribal Enterprise and State Licensees must be reported through the State reporting system, and cannabis products purchased from or sold to State Licensees must be fully compliant with all State cannabis laws and rules, including packaging, testing, and labelling." And: "The State will not cite, fine, or otherwise take any other adverse licensing or other action against any State Licensee for the mere fact that it bought or sold cannabis products from or to the Tribe or a Tribal Enterprise in accordance with the terms of this Compact and the Tribal Code." (LCB packet text, search-indexed — Verify by direct open.) - General compact monitoring (secondary summary of Suquamish): "mandatory product traceability compliance, random inspections, etc." by LCB — Verify current template/inspection terms.

C. Trade with LCB licensees. - Suquamish compact (opened, §V.A): the compact "applies to the production, processing, and sale of marijuana products in Indian Country where the Tribe, Tribal Enterprise, or Tribal Member Business (i) delivers or causes delivery to be made to or receives delivery of marijuana products from a State Licensee or (ii) physically transfers possession of marijuana products from the seller to the buyer within Indian Country. Except as otherwise provided herein, the production, processing, sale, and possession of marijuana products in Indian Country pursuant to the STC and in accordance with this Compact are not subject to the terms of RCW 69.50, RCW 69.51A, WAC 314-55, or any other regulations promulgated under those RCW Chapters and any such activities will not be a criminal or civil offense under Washington state law." - In plain terms: a compacted tribe may buy from and sell to LCB licensees and to other compacted Washington tribes, subject to the state-law compliance + traceability conditions for state-licensee transactions above. A tribe without a compact has no such state-law safe harbour in the sources located — its sales to state licensees would be outside both RCW 69.50 licensing and RCW 43.06.490. Off-reservation (outside Indian Country) sales by a tribal enterprise require a state licence like any other operator (secondary summary — Verify, but consistent with the compact's Indian-Country applicability clause). - Who may buy (retail): Tribal retail under compacts is not members-only in the texts located. Suquamish retail is conducted "in Indian Country pursuant to the STC and this Compact" with no membership restriction in the opened sections; the tax exemption (not purchase eligibility) is what turns on tribal membership / tribal-grown product (RCW 43.06.490(2)(b)). Age and ID conditions apply: Chehalis/Swinomish codes bar persons under 21 from entering or being employed at producing/processing facilities and require age proof at retail (Suquamish code §11.10.9 conditions of sale: "Acceptable identification for proof of age includes: driver's [licence — text truncated in the search extract; Verify]"). Adult-use purchase age is 21+ (Run 1 anchor). Verify per-tribe retail age/ID and any member-priority rules before publishing a "who may buy" sentence for a specific tribe.

D. Retail locations. - Suquamish (opened): first location 15915 State Highway 305 NE, Poulsbo, WA 98370 (SEC). Any other Tribe/Tribal Enterprise retail location requires 30 days' notice to the State before opening, with operator identity, premises location, and certification that the premises is in Indian Country (§V.B.2). At the time of the original compact, the Suquamish Tribal Code did not permit Tribal Member Businesses to conduct retail sales; if the code were revised to allow it, a member business could not purchase from a State Licensee until the compact was amended (§V.B, opened). - Chehalis (opened): production/processing buffers — "No producing or processing of marijuana products may occur within one thousand (1,000) feet of a school or playground or tribal housing" (§9.50.070(a)); no under-21 entry/employment at production/processing facilities (§9.50.070(b)); facilities may be indoors or outdoors if "fully secure, have physical barriers, and meet safety and security protocols" (§9.50.070(c)). - Suquamish expansion (secondary): "If the Tribe wants to expand beyond its one retail store, it need only notify the [LCB]" and the compact "does not limit the Tribe to three retail storefronts, as is the case for its non-tribal competitors" — the "three-store" comparison refers to the state retail-ownership cap as it stood in 2015 coverage; Run 1 Part 4 gives the current state cap as 5 retail licences per owner (with ESSB 5403 clarifications) — do not carry the 2015 "three" figure into the combined file as current law. - Any business locating on Suquamish land must first obtain the Tribe's express written consent plus any additional Suquamish Tribal Code licensing (secondary summary of the compact — Verify against current compact/code).

E. Medical provisions and DOH MOAs (see also §10.1.7). - LCB compacts page (opened): "Federally recognized Indian tribes who have a compact agreement with the State of Washington can also negotiate a Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) with the Washington State Department of Health (DOH). Tribes who plan on serving the cannabis patient community will need an MOA with DOH to access and enter information into the Medical Cannabis Authorization Database." - LCB/DOH workflow (search-indexed): an MOA is "necessary for medical endorsement and use of the Medical Marijuana Authorization Database"; it requires a certified consultant on staff and proper equipment (heat laminator, computer, printer) per the Apr 2019 Tribal Advisory Council deck (secondary/ search-indexed); the executed MOA is submitted as an exhibit to the final compact. - Per-tribe variation is the rule, not the exception: Nooksack's 2021 compact had "at this point no medical program" (secondary). Suquamish's compact/MOA materials include a DOH MOA exhibit (the opened Suquamish PDF's later pages contain an MOA with database-access provisions — those pages were not opened in this session due to the rate limit; Verify). Do not state that any named tribe (other than as evidenced by its own MOA) currently serves patients, holds a medical endorsement, or sells DOH-compliant medical product.

10.1.6 DOR retail-tax compacts — a different compact, do not conflate

DOR's "Retail taxes — compacts" page (search-indexed) describes general retail-tax compacts (government-to-government agreements under which "a portion of state tax revenues is distributed to the compacting tribe from certain retail sales transactions sourced to designated areas," including state sales tax, use tax and certain B&O taxes on nonmember-to-nonmember retail transactions, via location codes). Examples in Run 1 Part 6: Puyallup Tribe (effective 1 Jan 2022), Squaxin Island Tribe (1 July 2021; Tumwater code 1 Jan 2025), Colville Tribes compact (effective 1 Apr 2025, location codes effective 1 Jan 2026). These are not cannabis compacts and their location codes are not cannabis sales data. They are listed here only because a combined-file reader will otherwise mistake a DOR "compact effective" date for a cannabis-compact date. Cannabis-compact tax is governed by RCW 43.06.490(2) (§10.1.5A), not by DOR location codes.

10.1.7 Tribal Schedule III / 280E — Verify (Run 1 flag carried forward, with one new angle)

  • Flag preserved: Whether any medical activity by a tribal business operating under a state compact (RCW 43.06.490) counts as "state-licensed" for the federal Schedule III rule (effective 28 Apr 2026, given baseline) is Verify, for the same reason as endorsement sales plus the additional question whether government-to-government compact licensing is "state" licensing at all (Run 1 Part 1).
  • New angle found in this track (does not resolve the flag): secondary summaries of the 2015 Suquamish compact report a clause requiring renegotiation "if federal law reschedules marijuana." If that clause survives in the October 2025 template or in current compacts, rescheduling would trigger a compact renegotiation — but the template was not extracted and no current compact's renegotiation clause was opened, so whether any current compact contains such a clause, and what it would require after the April 2026 medical Schedule III rule, is Verify. Do not present renegotiation as having occurred.
  • Adult-use tribal activity remains Schedule I and fully subject to 280E on the given baseline, exactly as for non-tribal adult-use activity. No tribal 280E relief is asserted anywhere in this report.

10.1.8 Which tribes ban cannabis — only Yakama is evidenced, and its current text is Verify

  • Yakama Nation (Confederated Tribes and Bands of the Yakama Nation): Multiple secondary sources agree the Nation decided in 2013–2014 not to recognise Washington's adult-use law on its 1.2-million-acre reservation, banned growing/selling/consumption there, and in 2014 filed challenges to almost 1,300 pending LCB licence applications in the 10 counties covering its ceded lands (10.8 million acres under the 1855 treaty, where it retains hunting/fishing/gathering rights). A 2025 peer-reviewed paper states: "The Yakama Nation is an exception; the tribe ruled against recreational marijuana in 2014 and continues to ban liquor and cannabis sales on tribal lands including all 10 counties making up the reservation" (citing an LCB 2021 Board handout on Yakama liquor applications). The LCB position reported in 2013 was that it would not issue permits for locations on reservations where the tribe objected, consistent with RCW 69.50.331(8)(f) consent (Run 1 Part 3).
  • Limits: No Yakama tribal-code text prohibiting cannabis was opened in this track; the 2013–2014 statements are tribal-chair / tribal-attorney statements as reported contemporaneously, and the "continues to ban" claim rests on a 2025 secondary paper. Current Yakama code text and current enforcement position are Verify. The ceded-lands claim (ban extending beyond the reservation to 10.8 million acres) was contested at the time (LCB "unlikely to side entirely with the tribe," per 2014 secondary coverage) and its legal outcome was not located — Verify; do not state that the ceded-lands ban is in force.
  • Spokane Tribe: A 2014 secondary account describes the Spokane Tribe's Law and Order Code as stating marijuana is illegal for use on the reservation (in a court-case context). That is a single, dated, secondary account; the Tribe later negotiated a cannabis compact (Sept 2022, §10.1.3). Do not label Spokane as currently banning cannabis — its current code/compact status is Verify.
  • All other tribes: No ban evidenced in the sources located. Unavailable — do not infer.

10.2 Interstate issues

10.2.1 The core rule: cannabis does not travel; hemp (as defined) does — with Washington's detectable-THC overlay

Movement Rule as sourced Source + Accessed
Cannabis (adult-use or medical) across any state line, WA → OR / ID / any state Federal crime to transport, even between two legal states. Secondary law-firm summary (updated Sept 2025): "taking marijuana from one marijuana legal state to another — even though both jurisdictions legally allow for recreational marijuana use — still constitutes a federal crime" (21 U.S.C. § 812, Schedule I at the time of writing; adult-use remains Schedule I under the given 2026 baseline). Keep and consume in the state of purchase. This is a general federal principle, not a Washington statute — Washington law does not authorise export, and LCB licences are intrastate (transport only "between licensed cannabis businesses within Washington state," WAC 314-55-310, search-indexed). Harris Sliwoski summary — Secondary, Accessed 2026-11-01; WAC 314-55-310 — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01
Cannabis into Canada / out of Canada Illegal in both directions, even though cannabis is legal in Canada and in Washington. CBSA (official, search-indexed): "it remains illegal to bring cannabis (and cannabis products) into or out of Canada – whether it is a gift or for personal use"; "Under the Cannabis Act, it remains illegal to import into Canada, or export from Canada, cannabis and cannabis products without a valid permit or exemption (including CBD products derived from cannabis or hemp)"; non-declaration risks seizure, arrest and/or monetary penalties. A medical prescription "does not count as Health Canada authorization" (CBSA Easter 2026 tips, search-indexed). Relevant crossings from Washington: Peace Arch (Blaine WA / Surrey BC) and Pacific Highway (Surrey) — see §10.2.3. CBSA, Canada.ca — Search-indexed official, https://www.canada.ca/en/border-services-agency/news/2022/05/cbsa-reminds-canadians-of-cannabis-border-laws.html — Accessed 2026-11-01
Hemp across state lines (general federal rule) 2018 Farm Bill §10114(b) (7 U.S.C. § 1639o note): "no state or tribal government may prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with [the Farm Bill]" (secondary summary of the statute — Verify verbatim text). Hemp = Cannabis sativa L. with ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis under the 2018 definition (secondary summary). Washington overlay (below) and the given 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 federal redefinition change this picture — see next row. Secondary summaries — Accessed 2026-11-01
Hemp in / through Washington specifically WSDA transport rule, WAC 16-306-130 (search-indexed official text): hemp "may not be transported from a registered land area … until THC certification by the department … is obtained … prior to transport"; the person in possession during transport must carry either (a) copies of the producer licence + department THC certification, or (b) "a bill of lading or other proper documentation demonstrating that the hemp was legally imported or is otherwise legally present in the state of Washington under applicable state and federal laws relating to hemp." Hemp from a licensed WA producer found off-premises without certification "is deemed to be contraband and subject to seizure by the Washington state patrol or any law enforcement officer," destruction at licensee expense, possible licence suspension/revocation. And Washington's detectable-THC rule (SB 5367, Run 1): a product intended for internal consumption with any detectable THC is a "cannabis product" sellable only by an LCB licensee or under a tribal agreement — so hemp-derived THC products that are lawful hemp elsewhere are not freely saleable as general hemp retail in Washington. Out-of-state hemp THC "may not be imported into LCB retail as a hemp product" (Run 1 Part 1, LCB THC guide). WAC 16-306-130 — Search-indexed, Accessed 2026-11-01; Run 1 Part 1
Hemp after the given federal dates Given baseline: PL 119-37 §781 eff. 12 Nov 2026, limited by PL 119-103 §2019 until 11 Dec 2026; after 11 Dec 2026 the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. No Washington conforming law/guidance located in this track (same gap as Run 1). Interstate hemp shipments after 11 Dec 2026 must therefore be assessed against both the new federal definition and Washington's detectable-THC / LCB-channel rules — how those interact is Verify after 11 Dec 2026. Do not advise that a shipment lawful under the old 0.3% delta-9 definition remains lawful after that date. Given baseline; Run 1 Part 1
Mail / commercial carriers Secondary carrier summaries: USPS Publication 52 permits mailing hemp products complying with federal/state law (≤0.3% delta-9 THC under the old definition); UPS/FedEx restrict or prohibit cannabis/hemp shipments by policy. Carrier policy is not law and changes without a statute changing — Verify with the carrier before quoting. No Washington-specific mail rule was located. Secondary — Accessed 2026-11-01 — Verify

10.2.2 Out-of-state buyers — Idaho and Oregon (Run 1 Unavailable preserved)

  • What Washington law allows: Adult-use purchase is from LCB-licensed retailers by adults 21+ (Run 1 Part 1). No Washington residency requirement for adult-use purchase is stated in the Run 1 materials; medical purchase has no out-of-state reciprocity (Run 1 Part 1: "no out-of-state reciprocity"). A secondary market summary states adult-use "Recreational: Yes" for accepting other states' buyers (Brightfield, search-indexed) — that is consistent with, but not a substitute for, the statutory text; Verify the residency point against RCW 69.50.360 before the combined file states it as law.
  • What is NOT known (Run 1 Part 6, preserved): No official Washington publication of buyer-residency share (Idaho / Oregon / other) was located in Run 1 or in this track. Do not estimate an Idaho or Oregon share of Washington sales, and do not present border-store anecdotes as market shares.
  • What buyers face at home: Idaho — marijuana illegal, no medical programme, remains Schedule I (Run 1 Idaho anchor in the parent's memory file; Idaho law itself is Part 12 / Idaho-dossier scope, not re-proved here — cite as "per the Idaho dossier" if the combined file needs it, otherwise state only that Washington legality does not travel). Oregon — adult-use legal (Oregon dossier scope); Oregon's own possession limits and public-use ban apply once across the line, and Oregon law likewise does not authorise import (secondary Oregon travel summary: "you cannot carry marijuana across state lines (even to neighboring Washington and California, where recreational marijuana is also legal)" — secondary, Accessed 2026-11-01).
  • No interstate cannabis agreement was located. Washington has no state-to-state cannabis import/export, reciprocity, or "interstate commerce" agreement in the sources located (contrast: tribal compacts are government-to-government agreements with sovereign tribes under RCW 43.06.490, not interstate agreements; hemp interstate shipment protection is a federal rule, §10.2.1). If the combined file needs a definitive "none exists" sentence, it requires a negative check of leg.wa.gov / LCB interstate pages — Verify.

10.2.3 Border markets — geography and law only (no sales claims)

Border market (WA side) Neighbour across the line Why it is named in the brief / what can lawfully be said What is NOT claimed
Spokane Valley, WA (Spokane County) Coeur d'Alene, ID (Kootenai County) — east on I-90 Eastern-Washington retail cluster near the Idaho line; Idaho buyers would face Idaho prohibition on return and federal interstate-transport exposure (§10.2.1). Spokane Valley is a separate city from Spokane and from Coeur d'Alene. No Spokane Valley sales total, Idaho-buyer share, or "border premium" figure — Unavailable (Run 1 Part 6).
Clarkston, WA (Asotin County) Lewiston, ID (Nez Perce County) — across the Snake River Small border city pair; same Idaho-return / federal-transport analysis. Clarkston's local cannabis history (Ord. 1532 repeal of a ban, Run 1 Part 3) is a local-zoning fact, not a border-sales fact. No sales or buyer-share figure.
Vancouver, WA (Clark County) Portland, OR — across the Columbia River (I-5 / I-205) Both sides adult-use legal, but that does not make cross-river transport lawful (§10.2.1). Vancouver WA is not Vancouver BC. Retail-cap history for Vancouver (6- vs 12-store cap conflict) is Run 1 Part 3 — Verify, not repeated as settled here. No cross-river sales flow figure.
Blaine, WA (Whatcom County) Surrey, BC, Canada — Peace Arch (I-5 / BC Hwy 99) and Pacific Highway crossings Northern border city; Canada legality does not authorise carrying cannabis either way (§10.2.1, CBSA). Blaine is the U.S. crossing town, not the Canadian destination (Surrey / Vancouver BC). No seizure or crossing-volume figure for cannabis at Blaine — Unavailable in this track.
Walla Walla, WA (Walla Walla County) Milton-Freewater, OR (Umatilla County) — south across the state line Southern border pair named in the brief; same WA↔OR transport analysis as Vancouver/Portland. Walla Walla's LCB retail allocation was stated as 3 outlets in 2016–17 materials (Run 1 Part 3) — historical, Verify current allocation. No sales flow figure.
Other crossings / corridors I-90 (Spokane ↔ Coeur d'Alene), US 2 (northern WA ↔ northern ID), I-82/US 395 (Tri-Cities ↔ eastern OR), SR 14 / Columbia Gorge (WA ↔ OR) Corridors are named for orientation only. The legal analysis does not change by corridor: state-line crossing with cannabis = federal exposure; with compliant hemp = documentation rule (§10.2.1). No corridor traffic or enforcement figure.

10.2.4 Ferries — state ferries and the federal-waters issue (flagged, not settled)

  • What Washington State Ferries (WSF) is: the state ferry system, the largest in the U.S., with Puget Sound routes to Bainbridge, Vashon, Whidbey and the San Juan Islands, plus the Keller Ferry across the Columbia River between Ferry County (including Colville Reservation access) and Lincoln County (secondary travel summary — Verify against WSDOT/WSF route pages). County ferries (Pierce County to Anderson/Steilacoom; Skagit County Anacortes–Guemes) are separate operators.
  • No WSF-specific cannabis rule was located in this track. No WSF tariff, passenger code, or WSDOT page stating a cannabis possession/use rule for state ferries was found. Do not state that cannabis is "allowed" or "prohibited" on WSF as a WSF rule.
  • What can be said without inventing a ferry rule: (1) Washington's public-consumption ban (RCW 69.50.445, Run 1 Part 1) and open-container/vehicle rules apply on land and, as state law, to conduct within Washington; smoking/vaping in public places is separately restricted (ch. 70.160 RCW, Run 1). (2) Adult-use cannabis remains federally illegal (Schedule I, given baseline), and vessels on navigable waters raise federal-jurisdiction questions (U.S. Coast Guard / federal maritime law) that no source located in this track resolves for WSF passenger possession. Whether a state ferry in Puget Sound counts as "federal waters" or "federal property" for possession purposes, and whether WSF crew/passenger rules differ from the general rule, is therefore Verify — seek a WSF/WSDOT statement and, if needed, a federal-maritime source before publishing a ferry rule. (3) A distinct, evidenced ferry issue is crew drug testing: federal DOT testing rules for safety-sensitive maritime workers (including ferry operators) continue to apply and a positive THC test can cost a worker the job even for off-duty legal use, per 2025–2026 congressional/press coverage (secondary; DOT bulletin: marijuana remains prohibited for DOT-tested workers until any rescheduling is finalised, and rescheduling alone would not automatically change DOT rules because marijuana is listed by name — secondary summaries, Accessed 2026-11-01 — Verify against the DOT bulletin). That is an employment rule, not a passenger-possession rule — do not conflate them.
  • International ferries: The Sidney, BC–Anacortes service is suspended and "will not resume until at least 2030" (secondary travel summary — Verify against WSF/BC Ferries notices). Victoria Clipper (Seattle ↔ Victoria, BC, passenger-only) is an international crossing: the Canada rule in §10.2.1 (no cannabis either way) applies in full. Private-boat crossings to Canada are subject to the same CBSA/CBP border rules.

10.2.5 Federal land, federal buildings, airports, military bases

  • General principle (secondary, not a Washington-specific rule): Cannabis possession/use on federal land — national parks (e.g. Olympic, Mount Rainier, North Cascades), national forests, military installations (e.g. Joint Base Lewis-McChord), federal buildings and airports — follows federal law, not Washington law, even inside Washington's borders. A state medical card is not a federal prescription (secondary summaries). Adult-use remains Schedule I federally (given baseline), so the principle holds for adult-use regardless of the medical Schedule III rule; whether state-licensed medical product on federal land is treated differently after 28 Apr 2026 is Verify (no federal-land guidance located).
  • No Washington-specific federal-land enforcement figure or WSP/NPS memorandum was located for this track. Do not cite a park-specific seizure count.
  • Airports: Flying with cannabis is illegal even between two legal states (secondary summary, §10.2.1) — federal jurisdiction over air travel. No Washington airport-specific cannabis policy (e.g. Sea-Tac) was located — Verify if the combined file needs an airport paragraph; do not assume Sea-Tac permits possession in the terminal.

10.2.6 Interstate laws and agreements — summary

Instrument Status for Washington cannabis/hemp
State-to-state cannabis compact / reciprocity agreement None located — Verify by leg.wa.gov / LCB search before a definitive "none" sentence (see §10.2.2).
Tribal cannabis compacts (RCW 43.06.490) Government-to-government, not interstate; 18 dated through Mar 2021 + Lummi 2024 (execution Verify) — §10.1.3.
DOR retail-tax compacts General tax-revenue compacts with tribes, not cannabis compacts — §10.1.6.
Federal hemp interstate-shipment protection (2018 Farm Bill) General federal rule, subject to Washington's transport-documentation rule and detectable-THC overlay, and to the given Dec 2026 redefinition — §10.2.1.
Federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider (through 11 Dec 2026, given baseline) Federal spending restriction, not an interstate agreement; its post-11 Dec 2026 status is Verify (given baseline gives no extension).
Cole Memorandum (29 Aug 2013) + Wilkinson Memorandum (28 Oct 2014, Indian Country) Cited in the 2015 Suquamish compact preamble (opened) as the then-current federal enforcement guidance. Both were later withdrawn (Chehalis ordinance findings note the withdrawal, opened text: "Subsequently the above Letter and Memorandum were withdrawn, but after due consultation with the US Attorney for the Western District of Washington at Seattle, the Tribe has determined that moving forward … falls within the sovereign authority of the Tribe"). Do not cite Cole/Wilkinson as current federal policy. Current federal enforcement posture for tribal cannabis after the 2026 Schedule III rule is Verify.

Parts 11–12 — Operations Rules & Consumer / Social Rules

Washington Cannabis & Hemp — Run 2, Parts 11–12 ONLY

Research date / cut-off: 2026-11-01. Scope: This is a working report for Parts 11–12 only. It is NOT the combined Parts 0–12 file. The parent agent assembles the combined file. Do not deliver this file as HTML.

Run 1 source checked: ~/workspace/your_files/washington-cannabis-hemp-run1/washington-cannabis-hemp-run1-parts-0-6-2026-11-01.html and the working reports in ~/workspace/washington-cannabis-hemp-run1/ (part01-report.md, part23-report.md, part45-report.md, part6-report.md).

Method and citation rule used in this report: Law is stated from official text only, with verbatim quotes where operative. Every fact carries a source link and “Accessed 2026-11-01”. Where a page could not be opened in full, or where a secondary summary was the only source found, the item is marked Verify or Unavailable and is not upgraded to settled law. No personal phone numbers or emails are reproduced in this report.

Federal baseline given for this dossier — not re-researched in Parts 11–12:

  • State-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I.
  • IRS 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to 280E.
  • Hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply.
  • USDA DEA-registered lab testing enforcement is delayed to 31 December 2026.
  • The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026.
  • No federal cannabis banking law.

These figures are repeated from Run 1 and must not be contradicted in the combined file. Where Run 1 flagged a conflict or Verify item, it is preserved here.

Item Run 1 anchor
Adult limits, per retail transaction 1 oz usable cannabis / flower; 16 oz cannabis-infused product, solid form; 72 oz liquid form, unless packaged in individual units ≤4 mg THC per unit; low-dose liquid units (≤4 mg THC/unit): 200 mg THC total; 7 g concentrate. Source in Run 1: RCW 69.50.360.
Patient limits, recognition card, standard 3 oz flower; 48 oz solid; 216 oz liquid; 21 g concentrate; grow 6 plants at domicile, possess up to 8 oz from own plants.
Patient limits, practitioner-recommended higher need Same retail amounts in the statute excerpt; grow up to 15 plants; possess up to 16 oz at domicile. Whether retail limits also rise was Verify in Run 1.
Authorisation only, NOT in Medical Cannabis Authorization Database Adult retail limits only; grow up to 4 plants; possess up to 6 oz at domicile. No more than 15 plants in a housing unit even if multiple patients/providers live there, except a registered cooperative. From DOH patient page in Run 1.
Cooperatives Maximum 4 members; all must hold valid recognition cards and be at least 21, except a designated provider for a patient under 21 may be a member on that patient's behalf; up to the total authorised per member, maximum 60 plants at the location; possess up to 72 oz usable cannabis at the location. Run 1 flagged a conflict: a DOH patient-page shorthand says cooperative members possess “up to 8 ounces” — the statute figure, 72 oz at the location, is controlling.
Edibles / servings General adult-use 10 mg active THC per serving/unit; 100 mg per package; maximum 10 servings per package (WAC 314-55-095). Single concentrate unit ≤1 gram. DOH High THC products (capsules, tablets, tinctures, patches, suppositories only): up to 50 mg per serving, 500 mg per package, maximum 10 servings. Reconciliation: the 10 mg / 100 mg rule is the general rule; 50 mg / 500 mg is the separate DOH High THC category. Do not merge them.
Delivery to consumers PROHIBITED (WAC 314-55-079(5)).
Buffer 1,000-foot rule, reducible locally to 100 feet except for schools/playgrounds.
Traceability history BioTrackTHC 2013–2017; Leaf Data Systems / MJ Freeway 2017–2021, never fully deployed; Contingency Reporting System as backup; Cannabis Central Reporting System (CCRS) from December 2021 to present, described by LCB and JLARC as a reporting system, not a full seed-to-sale traceability system; reporting at minimum weekly.
Employment SB 5123 (2023) → RCW 49.44.240, signed 9 May 2023, effective 1 January 2024; initial hiring only.
Fees Producer / processor / retailer annual issuance/renewal $1,781 from 1 July 2026 (EHB 2681; was $1,381), CPI-adjusted from 1 July 2027. Transporter annual fee $1,300 per WAC 314-55-310 snippet was Verify in Run 1. Research annual fee was Verify / Unavailable in Run 1.
Ownership caps 5 retail licences; 3 producer licences; 3 processor licences per entity/principals. ESSB 5403 clarification on financial interest, effective 1 January 2026 — scope was Verify where Run 1 flagged it.
Application windows on 2026-11-01 General producer / processor / retailer window closed; last social-equity registration window 2 June–1 July 2025, closed. Transporter, research and cooperative routes were the remaining routes identified in Run 1.

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

11.1 Testing, sampling, failed tests, recalls, lab certification

11.1.1 Required testing — WAC 314-55-102

WAC 314-55-102 was opened directly on 2026-11-01 (official PDF text, certified 14 May 2025; latest amendment cited in the text: WSR 25-09-167, filed 23 April 2025, effective 24 May 2025). Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-102-html&pdf=true — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Required fields for LCB-certified laboratory testing:

Field Note
(i) Water activity Required field
(ii) Cannabinoid concentration analysis Required field
(iii) Foreign matter inspection Required field
(iv) Microbiological testing Required field
(v) Mycotoxin testing Required field
(vi) Pesticide testing Required field
(vii) Residual solvent testing Required field

Heavy-metal testing and terpene analysis are additional certifications a lab may hold; they are not listed as one of the seven required fields in the same way. See the DOH-compliant product rule below for heavy metals.

Flower required tests: water activity, cannabinoid concentration, foreign matter, microbiological, mycotoxin, pesticide. If sold as usable flower, no further testing is required under that subsection.

Intermediate products: must be homogenised before quality-assurance testing; batch = a single run through extraction or infusion; cannabis mix chopped/ground so no particles are greater than 3 mm. Test sets vary by type:

  • Hydrocarbon / CO2 / ethanol / food-grade solvent concentrates: cannabinoid + mycotoxin + residual solvent + pesticide (food-grade solvent adds microbiological).
  • Nonsolvent concentrates (kief, hash, rosin, bubble hash): cannabinoid + microbiological + mycotoxin + pesticide.
  • Cannabis mix: all six flower-type tests.
  • Infused cooking oil/fat in solid form: cannabinoid + microbiological + mycotoxin + pesticide.

End products sold processor → retailer:

  • Infused solid edible: cannabinoid + water activity.
  • Infused liquid (soda/tonic), infused topical, packaged cannabis mix, infused cannabis mix, and concentrate / infused product for inhalation: cannabinoid concentration analysis as listed in the rule.
  • End products consisting of only one unchanged intermediate product are not subject to cannabinoid concentration analysis.

Limits in WAC 314-55-102(3):

  • Water activity fails above 0.65 aw for usable cannabis, above 0.85 aw for solid edibles.
  • Foreign matter fails above 5% stems ≥3 mm diameter, 2% seeds or other foreign matter, or one insect fragment / hair / mammalian excreta in the sample.
  • Microbiological limits: unprocessed plant material BTGN 10,000 CFU/g, STEC <1, Salmonella spp. <1; processed plant material BTGN 1,000 CFU/g, STEC <1, Salmonella <1.
  • Mycotoxins: aflatoxins (sum of isomers) and ochratoxin A 20 µg/kg as tabled.
  • Residual solvents: class 3 >5,000 ppm, class 2 >50 ppm, class 1 >2 ppm fail if not tabled, with a table including e.g. acetone 5,000, benzene 2.0, butanes 5,000, ethanol 5,000, hexanes 290, methanol 3,000, toluene 890, xylenes 2,170 µg/g.
  • Heavy metals: arsenic 2.0, cadmium 0.82, lead 1.2, mercury 0.40 µg/g.

Heavy-metal testing is required for all DOH-compliant product under chapter 246-70 WAC and optional for non-DOH-compliant product, but the heavy-metal limits apply to all products; any product exceeding them is subject to recall and destruction. The Board may conduct random or investigation-driven heavy-metal testing.

Total THC / CBD formulas in WAC 314-55-102(3)(a): total delta-9 THC = delta-9 THC + (0.877 × delta-9 THCA); total CBD = CBD + (0.877 × CBDA); total THC for non-delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinols present above 0.2 mg/g uses the molar-mass ratio formula stated in the rule. Cannabinoid analysis must include at least CBD, CBDA, delta-9 THC, delta-9 THCA, any THC compound labelled/advertised/marketed as part of the product, total delta-9 THC, total THC for other tetrahydrocannabinols, and total CBD.

General lab duties in WAC 314-55-102(2): record acknowledgement of sample receipt; verify whether any unused portion is destroyed after testing; report results directly to the Board in the required format; product must not be converted, transferred or sold until required tests are reported to the Board and the licensee; fail a sample if any limit test is above allowable levels even if that limit test is not required in the testing tables; test “as is” / “as received”; two significant figures except foreign matter.

Sale before testing, WAC 314-55-102(5): usable flower, a batch of concentrate, or a batch of infused product may not be sold until required quality-control testing is completed and passed, except: (a) wholesale/transfer of flower and other material for further extraction and cannabis mix / nonsolvent extracts before testing; (b) transfer between multiple locations under the same UBI before testing; (c) wholesale/transfer of failed flower batches for extraction under subsection (6), unless failed for tests requiring immediate destruction.

Failed tests, WAC 314-55-102(6): on Board approval, failed quantities/batches may be used to create extracts; the resulting extract must pass all required tests before sale, unless failed for heavy-metal or pesticide tests requiring immediate destruction. Retesting: producer/processor must request it; the Board may authorise retest case-by-case to validate a failed result; the requesting producer/processor pays all retest costs. Remediation may occur after the first failure depending on the failure, or if a retest results in a second failure; pesticide failures may not be remediated. Remediation must not impart a toxic/harmful substance; solvents/methods must be disclosed to a licensed processor, the transferring producer/producer-processor, a retailer carrying derived product, or the consumer on request; the entire quantity from which failed samples were deducted must be remediated; no remediated quantity may be sold/transported until required testing is completed; if a failed quantity is not remediated/reprocessed after a first failure, it cannot be retested and any later certificate of analysis without remediation/reprocessing does not supersede the original compliance certificate.

Referencing, WAC 314-55-102(7): certified labs may subcontract fields of testing to other LCB-certified labs, but may not reference retesting for fields they already analysed. Chain-of-custody manifest must include lab name, certification number, transfer date, address, contact information, delivery personnel, sample IDs, field of testing, receiving personnel. Subcontracted results must be clearly indicated on the certificate of analysis with the testing lab's name, address and certification number.

Other WAC 314-55-102 points: certified labs are not limited in the amount of usable cannabis/products on premises, but must hold records proving all such material is held only for testing purposes described in the chapter. A certificate of analysis for a product subject to the chapter and chapter 246-70 WAC that has not already been transferred to retail expires 12 calendar months after issuance. The Board or its designee may request samples of cannabis/products, growing medium, soil amendments, fertilisers, crop production aids, pesticides or water for random/investigatory compliance checks.

11.1.2 Sampling — WAC 314-55-101 — Verify the current table

Status: the WAC 314-55-101 rule itself was not opened in full in this run. The sampling table below is therefore marked Verify and must not be presented as confirmed current law until the current rule page is opened.

From lab guidance and a DOH draft text, flower-lot minimum sample counts found were: <10 lb lot: 8 one-gram samples; 10–20 lb: 12; 20–30 lb: 15; 30–40 lb: 18; 40–50 lb: 19.

An older LCB presentation described a 5 lb lot / 4 separate samples from different quadrants, each at least 1 gram, total at least 4 grams.

Do not present both as current law. On final assembly, open WAC 314-55-101 directly and state which lot-size/sample-count table is current; if it cannot be opened, keep the sampling table as Verify.

Sources found for the table (secondary / draft, not the rule itself): https://www.conflabs.com/faq/how-should-i-select-my-samples/ — Accessed 2026-11-01; DOH chapter 246-70 draft text found in search — Accessed 2026-11-01. Rule to retry: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-101

11.1.3 Recalls — WAC 314-55-225

WAC 314-55-225 was opened directly on 2026-11-01 (WSR 24-16-064, filed 31 July 2024, effective 31 August 2024; earlier WSR 22-14-111 and WSR 16-16-050 cited). Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-225 — Accessed 2026-11-01. Recall notices page: https://lcb.wa.gov/examiners/recalls — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • “Affected product” and “affected licensee” are defined; more than one licensee may be affected.
  • Exempt market withdrawal: licensee may withdraw for non-risk reasons (e.g. aesthetic / packaging deficiencies); must notify LCB by contacting the local LCB enforcement officer within 48 hours of beginning the withdrawal; such withdrawals are then exempt from the rest of the section.
  • Recall required when circumstances pose a risk to consumers, including: unapproved pesticides above Board-rule action levels; residual solvents above Board-rule action levels; or another condition posing risk, including infused-product ingredients unfit for human consumption.
  • Licensee-initiated recall: immediately notify the local LCB enforcement officer; secure, isolate and prevent distribution of all possibly exposed product; prohibited from destroying affected product before notifying LCB and coordinating destruction with the local officer. If the licensee's efforts do not meet the urgency, LCB may seek a Board-directed recall.
  • LCB investigation-initiated recall: if no recall is appropriate, enforcement may release administrative holds unless a hold is needed for a continuing investigation; if a recall is appropriate, LCB notifies the Board and requests the Board issue a recall, then notifies the affected licensee that is the source of the issue.
  • Recall plans: every licensee must develop a recall plan within 60 days of the section's effective date, including a designated recall coordinator, procedures to identify/isolate product, procedures to retrieve/destroy product, and a communications plan (notify other licensees holding recalled product; press release and other appropriate public notification if product was distributed to consumers/retailers). The affected licensee must immediately notify all licensees that received affected product and issue a press release / public notification identifying the recalled product. Recall must follow the plan unless otherwise agreed by LCB; procedures must maximise recall and minimise risk.
  • Destruction: coordinate with the local LCB enforcement officer; LCB enforcement oversees destruction of recalled product posing risk.
  • Reports/audit: track total affected product and amount returned; report to LCB periodically, at minimum once a week or as specified/agreed in the recall plan.
  • Closure: if LCB determines efforts succeeded and public-health/safety risk no longer present, LCB may recommend closure to the Board.
  • Board-directed recall: Board may issue on enforcement recommendation if the licensee does not comply with subsection (3), does not comply with the plan/reporting, or product may be/is being diverted or another circumstance makes licensee destruction inadvisable/risky. Under a directed recall, LCB notifies consumers and all possibly affected licensees if not already notified; enforcement may seek a Board destruction order; if issued, enforcement may seize and destroy; a destruction order includes notice and opportunity for hearing before destruction, unless immediate danger justifies an immediate order with an expedited hearing after destruction. If LCB seizes/destroys under a destruction order, the affected licensee may be responsible for reimbursing LCB destruction costs. If immediate danger requires immediate LCB action, a licensee may also be subject to summary suspension under RCW 66.08.150(4).
  • LCB must maintain a recall web page of all current and closed recalls of record.

Recall-page examples found in search on 2026-11-01: 4 February 2026 Hitterz pre-rolls (producer/processor HANNAH INDUSTRIES 424223), reason stated as a QC failure at Alis Group for heavy metals; and an Okanogan Gold entry stating reason “Above pesticide action levels.” Treat the second entry's date as Verify at assembly time because the search excerpt mixed a 2026 listing with a 16 January 2023 entry.

11.1.4 Lab certification and enforcement — structure, and a transfer to handle carefully

Run 1: labs must first be WSDA-accredited (chapter 16-310 WAC), then LCB-certified (WAC 314-55-0995, 314-55-102). WAC 314-55-102(1) as opened confirms: “Prior to becoming certified, laboratories must be accredited by the WSDA as specified in chapter 16-310 WAC.” Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-102-html&pdf=true — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Transfer: HB 1859 (chapter 135, Laws of 2022) and 2SHB 2151 (chapter 69, Laws of 2024), codified as RCW 69.50.348 and both enacted 1 July 2024, transferred laboratory quality standards and accreditation authority from LCB to WSDA.

A proposed rule (WSR 25-06-033, filed 26 February 2025; hearing 9 April 2025) would amend WAC 314-55-0995, 314-55-102, 314-55-1035 and 314-55-109, and repeal WAC 314-55-1025 (proficiency testing) and WAC 314-55-103 (good laboratory practice checklist), moving WSDA-transferred proficiency/practice requirements to chapters 16-309 / 16-310 WAC. Final adoption status of that CR-102 package was not confirmed in this run — mark Verify; do not state WAC 314-55-1025 or 314-55-103 as repealed or as current without checking the current WAC chapter.

WAC 314-55-0995 (from the 2025 proposal text): certification requirements are continuing requirements; LCB may summarily suspend a lab's certification if found out of compliance. Conditions include WSDA accreditation under chapter 16-310 WAC before conducting required tests and conspicuous display of certification. WAC 314-55-1035 covers lab certification suspension/revocation; detailed penalty tiers in the proposal text were not fully verified — mark specific suspension/revocation penalty language Verify unless the current WAC 314-55-1035 page is opened at assembly time.

Secondary lab requirements (WashingtonStateCannabis, not official text — label as secondary if used): no person with a direct/indirect financial interest in a licensed producer/processor may have a financial interest in a certified independent lab; lab must hold ISO/IEC 17025 accreditation; must employ a scientific director; must adopt minimum good lab practices, a QC/QA programme and internal SOPs; must participate in proficiency testing under the pre-transfer rule. Do not present the $120-per-test figure (microbial + mycotoxin + potency, from the same secondary page) as an official fee.

11.1.5 Certified-lab counts — preserve the conflict, count only as instructed

Do not publish a single “number of certified labs” without explaining the basis (WSDA accreditation vs LCB certification vs labs actually testing).

Source Date / basis Count / statement
LCB Frequently Requested Lists page List titled “Cannabis Approved Testing Labs — 10-29-2026” as a download; the list itself was not counted in this run Count not verified in this run. Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/records/frequently-requested-lists — Accessed 2026-11-01
WSDA cannabis page, status table Updated 3 June 2026 Four laboratories listed as “Accredited” with all four parameters (cannabinoids, heavy metals, pesticides, residual solvents) “Approved”: Confidence Analytics, Green Growers, Integrity Labs, Medicine Creek Analytics. Source: https://agr.wa.gov/departments/marijuana/ — Accessed 2026-11-01
Secondary CannBus Q3 2026 report Citing LCB FY2025 Annual Report 6 certified labs in FY2025; also states 112 quality-control failures at certified labs in FY2025, down from 130 in FY2024. Treat both as secondary, not opened official figures.
DOH medical-cannabis proposal material (WSR 26-20-094) Filed 6 October 2026; DOH knowledge as of 29 September 2026 DOH knew of only four labs testing cannabis in Washington as of 29 September 2026, while DOR reported 203 businesses categorised as Testing Laboratories and Services. This proposal is NOT law; its four-lab statement conflicts in basis with the FY2025 “6 certified” figure and must not be merged into a single count.

DOH proposal contents (proposal only, not law): mandatory terpene testing (quantify at least nine terpenes when present above 500 µg/g), sharply lower heavy-metal limits, more label information for patients, updated THC/CBD thresholds and QR-code / transparency requirements, amended WAC 246-70-010 through 246-70-090. Current medical rule as summarised in the proposal material states “terpene analysis is not required”; if a label lists terpenes, an analysis from a certified third-party lab only has to be available when a consumer asks. Do not present the proposal's terpene/heavy-metal changes as current law.

DOH proposal procedural dates (proposal only): filed 6 October 2026; hearing 10 November 2026; intended adoption 17 November 2026 (not the effective date). The filing's individual contact block is not reproduced here, per the no-personal-contact rule.

11.2 Licence rules — transfers, ownership changes, renewal, caps, relocation, surrender/revocation, insurance/bonding

11.2.1 Annual renewal

RCW 69.50.325 licences (producer, processor, retailer) are “subject to annual renewal” — from a 2019 bill-amendment text excerpt; the current RCW 69.50.325 fee subsection should be cited at assembly time. Annual fee $1,781 from 1 July 2026 per Run 1.

Social-equity annual-fee waiver: Run 1 recorded a secondary summary stating waiver for social-equity retail licences through 1 July 2032 — Verify in current RCW 69.50.335 before repeating as law; a 2023 bill analysis states the Programme expiration was extended to 1 July 2032 and that producer/processor licences subject to forfeiture/revocation/cancellation may be issued/reissued to qualifying applicants until then, with up to 100 processor licences immediately and up to 10 producer licences from 1 January 2025, each producer licence in conjunction with a processor licence.

11.2.2 Renewal / violation-response timeline — WAC 314-55-5055

From Justia, current through Register Vol. 25-12, 15 June 2025. Source: https://regulations.justia.com/states/washington/title-314/chapter-314-55/section-314-55-5055/ — Accessed 2026-11-01.

A licensee must respond to an administrative violation notice within 20 calendar days of receipt, on a Board form, by (a) accepting the recommended penalty, (b) requesting a settlement conference in writing, or (c) requesting an administrative hearing in writing. If no response within 20 days, recommended penalties including suspension, monetary penalties and inventory destruction may take effect on the 21st day. If the recommended penalty is monetary only (no suspension / inventory destruction), the licensee must pay a 25% late fee in addition, and the Board must receive payment plus late fee no later than 30 days after the notice receipt date. Licensees who do not respond will not be eligible to renew; licensees who do not pay monetary penalties for two or more administrative violation notices in a two-year period will not be eligible to renew. A settlement conference may include deferral if: first violation in the category; no other violation history in that penalty category within a two-year window; and, per the excerpt, no violation notice / noncompliance notice during the year following deferral approval means the deferred notice will not be considered for renewal or penalty escalation.

Do NOT transfer the liquor-licensee FAQ renewal notice (45 days before expiration) to cannabis licences. That FAQ page (https://lcb.wa.gov/content/current-licensee-faq — Accessed 2026-11-01) is headed for liquor licences. The cannabis-specific BLS renewal window / deadline / late-renewal penalty was not confirmed from an opened official page in this run — mark Verify.

11.2.3 Licensing change requests — WAC 314-55-020

Each application is evaluated individually; the Board may inquire/request documents on all matters connected with the application. Qualification includes a background check under WAC 314-55-040 and 314-55-045, an inspection of the proposed/current location, and a residency check. Under RCW 69.50.331(1)(b) as reproduced in the rule summary, all applicants must have resided in Washington for at least six months before applying; business entities must be formed in Washington. Applicants must be current in tax obligations to DOR and other state agencies and sign an attestation under penalty of denial/loss of licensure. The Board may withdraw licensing change applications pending an adjudicative proceeding on a violation, with reapplication possible depending on outcome.

Examples of licensing change applications that may be affected include: (i) application for additional funding; (ii) application to add a medical cannabis endorsement; (iii) assumption of a licence; (iv) change in governing people, percentage owned, or stock/unit ownership; (v) change of location; (vi) expanding plant canopy to maximum allotted; (vii) request to change cannabis site or operating plan; (viii) request to add a processor licence; (ix) splitting a producer or processor licence.

Run 1 also listed LCB forms-page change applications: Cannabis Alteration Request (including expanding plant canopy), Reinstate Retail Cannabis License from Title Certificate, Change of Location, Change in Governing People or Percentage, Splitting a Producer and Processor License. A secondary guide's $75 fee for Change in Owners/Officers/Location was Verify in Run 1 — do not present it as official.

“Assumption of a licence” is the rule's transfer mechanism language. Do not describe a licence as freely assignable like ordinary property; the transfer/change route requires Board approval through the change-application process, and the new true parties of interest / financiers must qualify.

11.2.4 Change of location — WAC 314-55-125
