From lawfiles text, certified 12 October 2026: changing a cannabis licence to a new location requires a change-request application to the Board initiated through Business Licensing Services as described in WAC 314-55-020; Board approval must be obtained before any change of location; a change of location occurs any time a move results in any change to the physical location address. Statutory authority cited: RCW 69.50.335, 69.50.336, 69.50.342 and 2022 c 16; WSR 22-21-058, filed 12 October 2022, effective 12 November 2022. Source: https://lawfilestestext.leg.wa.gov/LawTest/WACPDF/WAC%20314%20%20TITLE/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20%20CHAPTER/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20-125.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.

General relocation must still clear the 1,000-foot buffer (reducible locally to 100 feet except schools/playgrounds), local zoning/permitting and, for social-equity licences, the mobility rules below.

11.2.5 Social-equity relocation — two distinct tracks, do not merge

SB 5080 applicants (2025 round): LCB Social Equity FAQ states: “SB 5080 allots to move anywhere in WA as long as approved by County, City, or town (RCW: 69.50.335 (1)(e)(i)).” Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/se/se-cannabis-faq — Accessed 2026-11-01.

HB 2870 applicants (2023 round), retailer mobility, WAC 314-55-570(8)(d), effective 1 January 2026: a qualifying licensee may change the initial business location from the currently allocated local jurisdiction only if (i) the qualifying licensee has not secured a location in the initial county where the licence is allocated, and (ii) any relocation into a different county requires an application score that exceeds the lowest score awarded for successful applicants in the desired county; the lowest scores awarded by county under chapter 236, Laws of 2020, are to be posted online. Title Certificate Holders are NOT eligible for this mobility option because they qualified but did not score, so there is no score to compare. Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/content/2870-applicant-resources — Accessed 2026-11-01.

A September 2025 rulemaking petition asking for unrestricted sale/transfer of social-equity licences was noted in search results; it is a petition, not law, and LCB material in that petition notes that unrestricted transfer without social-equity eligibility limits is likely a legislative matter under RCW 69.50.335. Do not present unrestricted transfer as current law.

Social-equity qualifying applicant test from Run 1 (SB 5080 definition): at least 51% ownership AND control by one or more individuals meeting at least two of: (1) lived in a disproportionately impacted area in Washington for at least five years between 1980 and 2010; (2) arrested/convicted of a cannabis offence, or a family member arrested/convicted; (3) household income in the year before application below Washington median household income as calculated by the U.S. Census Bureau; (4) socially and economically disadvantaged individual as defined by OMWBE under RCW chapter 39.19. The older 2870 definition differs and must not be mixed in.

11.2.6 Ownership caps

Repeat Run 1 exactly: 5 retail; 3 producer; 3 processor per entity/principals. ESSB 5403 effective 1 January 2026 clarified financial-interest counting — scope Verify where Run 1 flagged it.

11.2.7 Residency — conflict preserved

Older summaries state six months' residence for a sole proprietor and, for entities, Washington formation with 75% of interest holders meeting residency and non-resident holders being U.S. citizens; other summaries say all principals must be Washington residents for six months. Run 1 instructed: do NOT publish a single residency sentence as settled without re-opening current RCW 69.50.331. A 2025–2026 bill, HB 2037, would remove the residency requirement and create a different out-of-state renewal fee ($1,657 in a fiscal-note summary) plus B&O exemptions — HB 2037 was not confirmed enacted in Run 1 and must not be presented as law.

11.2.8 Surrender / revocation / cancellation framework

WAC 314-55-520, Category I (opened via search result, WSR 22-14-111, filed 6 July 2022, effective 6 August 2022): violations of a severity that would make a licence eligible for cancellation on a first offence; the Board may not cancel for a single violation unless it proves a Category I violation by a preponderance of the evidence. Category I examples: cannabis purchased from an unlicensed entity (WAC 314-55-083(4)) — licence cancellation; cannabis sold to an unlicensed, non-retail source / illegal sales out of the licensed marketplace (WAC 314-55-083(4)) — cancellation; condition-of-suspension violation — original penalty plus 10-day suspension with no monetary option on first violation, cancellation on second in a two-year window; transportation/storage to/from an unlicensed source, diversion, or both (WAC 314-55-083(4)) — cancellation; transportation outside Washington state boundaries (RCW 69.50.342(1)(k), RCW 69.50.345(10), WAC 314-55-310(1)) — cancellation; true party of interest — allowing a person to exercise ownership/control who would not have qualified based on affiliation with a criminal enterprise under chapter 69.50 RCW (WAC 314-55-035(1)) — cancellation; financier — receiving money from an undisclosed/unapproved financier with criminal history demonstrating affiliation with criminal enterprises, gangs or cartels, or money originating from such enterprises (WAC 314-55-035(4)) — cancellation. Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-520 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

WAC 314-55-521, Category II (violations creating a direct/immediate threat to public health/safety): examples found include intentional use of unauthorised pesticides, soil amendments, fertilisers or other crop production aids (RCW 69.50.342; WAC 314-55-084) — Tier 1 $2,500 / Tier 2 $5,000 / Tier 3 $7,500 monetary fine and destruction of affected cannabis on first violation; Tier 1 $7,500 / Tier 2 $15,000 / Tier 3 $22,500 and destruction on second; licence cancellation on third in a two-year window (tier meaning here is the penalty tier in the table, not the producer canopy tier — state this distinction if the table is reproduced). Adulterated usable cannabis with an organic/nonorganic chemical or compound (WAC 314-55-077(5)(b); WAC 314-55-101) carries the same tiered fines/destruction/cancellation pattern in the excerpt. Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-521 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Retailer forfeiture, WAC 314-55-055, was listed in the chapter index but its text was not opened in this run — mark forfeiture triggers/timelines Verify; do not invent a non-operation period.

A specific surrender procedure / form was not located in the official pages opened — state surrender mechanics as Verify / Unavailable if no LCB surrender form is opened at assembly time, rather than inventing a notice period.

11.2.9 Insurance and bonding — WAC 314-55-082

Text obtained from search result; direct open hit a rate-limit in this run — mark the direct-open status honestly, but the rule text itself was reproduced in full in the search result. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-082 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

“Cannabis licensees must obtain insurance coverage. Insurance is required to protect the consumer if any claims, suits, actions, costs, damages or expenses arise from any negligent or intentional act or omission of the cannabis licensees.”

  • (1) Commercial general liability insurance: licensee must carry and maintain CGL or commercial umbrella insurance for bodily injury and property damage arising out of licensed activities at all times; limits not less than $1,000,000; on Board request, must provide proof. Coverage must cover claims caused by any act, omission or negligence of the licensee or its officers, agents, representatives, assigns or servants, and bodily injury including disease, illness and death, and property damage arising out of premises/operations, products, and personal injury.
  • (2) Carrier rating: insurance must be issued by a company authorised to do business in Washington, placed with a carrier rated A– Class VII or better in the most recently published Best's Reports; if the insurer is not admitted, policies/procedures must comply with chapters 48.15 RCW and 284-15 WAC.
  • (3) Additional insured: the state and its employees, agents and volunteers shall be named as additional insured.
  • (4) Failure to maintain or provide proof of insurance as required may result in licence cancellation.
  • Statutory authority cited: RCW 69.50.335, 69.50.336, 69.50.342 and 2022 c 16; WSR 22-21-058, filed 12 October 2022, effective 12 November 2022.

Bonding: No separate surety-bond minimum for cannabis licensees was located in WAC 314-55-082 or in the licence sections opened. State bonding as Unavailable (no bond minimum found in this run) unless a bond requirement is found in the full WAC 314-55-020 / licence-type sections at assembly time. Do not present the $1,000,000 CGL limit as a bond.

Product-liability debate: a secondary law-firm summary notes debate over whether WAC 314-55-082 requires separate product-liability coverage, with a 2018 regulator response reported as “no” and brokers reported as now saying licences will not be granted without products coverage. Present this only as an unresolved interpretive point, Verify, not as a separate minimum.

11.3 Cultivation rules

11.3.1 Producer licence — WAC 314-55-075

From lawfiles text and the 2026 EHB 2681 CR-105 excerpt. Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/law/WAC/WAC%20314%20%20TITLE/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20%20CHAPTER/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20-075.htm — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1)(a) A cannabis producer licence allows the licensee to produce, harvest, trim, dry, cure, and package cannabis into lots for sale at wholesale to processor licensees and to other producer licensees; also to produce/sell plants, seed and plant tissue culture to other producer licensees (Run 1 wording).
  • (1)(b) Production must take place within a fully enclosed secure indoor facility or greenhouse with rigid walls, a roof, and doors. Outdoor production may take place in nonrigid greenhouses, other structures, or an expanse of open or cleared ground fully enclosed by a physical barrier. To obscure public view, outdoor production must be enclosed by a sight-obscure wall or fence at least eight feet high. Outdoor producers must meet WAC 314-55-083 security requirements. An outdoor grow must be physically separated at least 20 feet from another licensed outdoor grow and cannot share common walls or fences.
  • (6) Maximum production space cannot exceed the amount licensed. Applicants must designate on their operating plan the size category and the actual square footage designated as plant canopy. Tiers: Tier 1 — less than 4,000 sq ft; Tier 2 — 4,000 sq ft up to 10,000 sq ft; Tier 3 — 10,000 sq ft up to 30,000 sq ft. These are plant-canopy square feet, not total premises.
  • (7) LCB may reduce a licensee's/applicant's canopy square footage for the reasons stated in the rule (reasons not fully reproduced in the search excerpt — mark the reduction triggers Verify if the full subsection is not opened).
  • (9) Maximum amount of cannabis on a producer's premises at any time: (a) outdoor or greenhouse grows — one and one-quarter of a year's harvest; (b) indoor grows — six months of annual harvest.
  • Application fee $250 (plus fingerprint vendor fees); annual fee $1,781 from 1 July 2026 per Run 1 (older secondary pages still showing $1,381 are stale).

A 2026 rulemaking petition from Eagle Trees Farm (Tier 2 outdoor sun-grown) asking to amend WAC 314-55-075(6) to allow up to 30,000 sq ft for demonstrated outdoor sun-grown single-harvest producers is a petition only, not law; do not present Tier 2 as 30,000 sq ft.

11.3.2 Patient and cooperative grow — repeat Run 1 exactly

See the Run 1 anchors table at the top of this report for patient limits (card standard 3 oz / 48 oz / 216 oz / 21 g, 6 or 15 plants; authorisation-only 4 plants / 6 oz; housing-unit cap 15 plants except a registered cooperative) and cooperative limits (max 4 members, max 60 plants, 72 oz at the location).

Cooperative location rules, WAC 314-55-410 (from lawfiles/Cornell text). Source: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Law/WAC/WAC%20314%20%20TITLE/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20%20CHAPTER/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20-410.htm — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Cooperative formed by qualifying patients and/or designated providers to share responsibility for growing/processing cannabis only for members' medical use; all members at least 21 (designated provider of a patient under 21 may be a member on that patient's behalf); all members must hold valid recognition cards as defined by RCW 69.51A.010; no more than 4 members; a member may only grow plants in the cooperative and may not grow plants elsewhere; members may grow up to the total authorised per recognition card, maximum 60 plants, and may possess at the location the amount producible from permitted plants, but no more than 72 ounces; a cooperative may not be located within a one-mile radius of a cannabis retailer; must be located at the domicile of one member; only one cooperative per property tax parcel; outdoor production must be enclosed by a sight-obscure wall/fence at least eight feet high.

Registration: location must be registered with LCB; the registered location is the only location where members may grow/process. Required: completed Cannabis Cooperative Registration Form; copies of each person's recognition card; deed, lease, rental agreement or other document establishing ownership/control — if leased/rented, a sworn statement from the property owner granting permission, including a telephone number and address where the owner can be contacted for verification; a sketch outlining where cannabis is planned to be grown. LCB may inspect a cooperative between 8:00 a.m. and 8:00 p.m. unless otherwise agreed by members and LCB staff.

Secondary cooperative summary adds: cannot be within 1,000 metres of a school, playground, recreation centre, childcare facility or other facility handling minors; no fee prescribed for registering a cooperative. The 1,000-metre figure was Verify in Run 1 (different units in different summaries) — do not state it as settled unless WAC 314-55-410(1) is opened in full at assembly time and the distance is confirmed there.

Cooperative extraction, WAC 314-55-430 (from search result). Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-430 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Qualifying patients/designated providers, including cooperative participants, may extract/separate resin using only noncombustible methods: (a) heat, screens, presses, steam distillation, ice water, and other methods without combustible solvents/gases to create kief, hashish or bubble hash; (b) dairy butter, cooking oils/fats from natural sources, or other home cooking substances; (c) food-grade glycerin and propylene glycol solvent-based extraction; (d) CO2 if used in a closed-loop system as referenced in WAC 314-55-104. Only food-grade substances may be used in any stage of processing. Use of combustible materials including butane, isobutane, propane, heptane and ethanol is expressly forbidden. Resins extracted/separated are for the personal use of the qualifying patient or cooperative members only.

11.4 Security, storage and transport

11.4.1 WAC 314-55-083 — opened directly on 2026-11-01

WSR 24-19-040, filed 11 September 2024, effective 12 October 2024; earlier WSR 22-14-111, WSR 18-22-055, WSR 16-11-110, WSR 15-11-107, WSR 14-07-116, WSR 13-21-104 cited. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/WAc/default.aspx?cite=314-55-083 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) Identification badge: all licensees/employees on licensed premises must hold and properly display an employer-issued badge at all times while on premises and engaged in transportation. Badge must list the licensee's trade name and include the person's full and legal name and photograph. Licensees/employees must have state-issued identification available to verify badge information. Nonemployee visitors other than retail customers must hold/display a licensee-issued badge at all times on premises. A log must be kept showing each visitor's full name, badge number issued, time of arrival, time of departure, and purpose of visit. Log records must be maintained on premises for three years, subject to inspection by any LCB employee or law enforcement officer, and copied/provided on request. Employees, visitors and others on premises, including transporters, must provide identification to an LCB enforcement officer on request.
  • (2) Alarm systems: at minimum, a security alarm system on all perimeter entry points and perimeter windows. Motion detectors, pressure switches, duress, panic and hold-up alarms may also be used.
  • (3) Surveillance: at minimum, a complete video surveillance system with minimum camera resolution 640 × 470 pixels or pixel equivalent for analog; the storage device and/or cameras must be IP-compatible; all cameras must be fixed and placement must allow clear and certain identification of any person and activities in controlled areas; all entrances/exits to an indoor facility must be recorded from both indoor and outdoor (ingress/egress) vantage points; all cameras must record continuously 24 hours per day at a minimum of 10 frames per second; the storage device must be secured on premises in a lockbox, cabinet, closet, or otherwise secured against employee tampering/criminal theft; recordings must be kept minimum 45 days on the licensee's recording device; all videos are subject to inspection by any LCB employee or law enforcement officer and must be copied/provided on request; recorded images must clearly and accurately display time and date, measured per U.S. National Institute of Standards and Technology standards.
  • Controlled areas include: (a) any area within an indoor, greenhouse or outdoor room/area where cannabis is grown, or cannabis or cannabis waste is moved within, processed, stored or destroyed — rooms/areas where cannabis or cannabis waste is never present are not control areas and do not require camera coverage; (b) all point-of-sale areas; (c) 20 feet of the exterior of the perimeter of all required fencing/gates enclosing an outdoor grow — any gate/entry point in the required outdoor enclosure must be lighted in low-light conditions; a motion-detection lighting system may be used for the gate area; (d) any room/area storing a surveillance system storage device.
  • (4) Traceability: to prevent diversion and promote public safety, licensees must track cannabis from seed to sale and provide required information on a system specified by LCB; all reporting costs are borne by the licensee. Seedlings, clones, plants, lots of usable cannabis/trim, leaves and other plant matter, batches of extracts, infused products, samples and cannabis waste must be traceable from production through processing into retail, including identifying which lot was used as base material for each extract/infused batch. Required, up-to-date information includes: (a) key “events” such as a plant entering the system (moved from seedling/clone area to vegetation production area at a young age); (b) when plants are to be partially/fully harvested or destroyed; (c) when a lot/batch of cannabis, extract, concentrates, infused product or waste is to be destroyed; (d) when usable cannabis, concentrates or infused products are transported; (e) any theft of usable cannabis, seedlings, clones, plants, trim or other plant material, extract, infused product, seed, plant tissue or other cannabis-containing item; (f) all plants eight or more inches in height or width must be physically tagged and tracked individually; (g) a complete inventory of all cannabis, seeds, plant tissue, seedlings, clones, all plants, lots of usable cannabis/trim, leaves and other plant matter, batches of extract, concentrates, infused products and waste; (h) all cannabis, usable cannabis, infused products, concentrates, seeds, plant tissue, clone lots and waste must be physically tagged with the unique identifier generated by the traceability system and tracked; (i) all point-of-sale records; (j) cannabis excise tax records, including records required for medical patient excise-tax exemptions in WAC 314-55-090; (k) all samples sent to an independent testing lab, any unused sample portion returned, and quality-assurance test results; (l) all vendor samples provided to another licensee for education or negotiating a sale; (m) all samples used for testing for quality by the producer/processor; (n) samples containing usable cannabis provided to retailers; (o) samples provided to LCB or its designee for quality-assurance compliance checks; (p) other information specified by the Board.

Note: the older “quarantine 24 hours before transport” language found in a 2014 amendatory text is not in the current WAC 314-55-083 text opened on 2026-11-01 and must not be carried forward as current law without checking WAC 314-55-085 / current quarantine provisions.

11.4.2 Transport — WAC 314-55-085

From official search excerpt; full page not opened in this run — mark full-text status accordingly. Sources: https://app.leg.wa.gov/WAC/default.aspx?cite=314-55-085 — Accessed 2026-11-01; LCB manifest guide (CIB 140, March 2026): https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2026-03/CIB140-Manifest%20Guide_3-24-26.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01; LCB Secure Transportation Storage Guide (CIB 82, May 2023): https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/Cannabis/Education/CIB82-Cannabis%20Secure%20Transportation%20Storage%20Guide-5-23.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) Notification of shipment: on transporting any cannabis/product, a producer, processor, retailer or certified third-party testing lab must notify LCB of type and amount/weight, transporter name, transporting vehicle information, departure and expected delivery times, reported in the traceability system described in WAC 314-55-083(4). Receipt: the receiving licensee must report amount/weight received in the traceability system.
  • (3) Transportation manifest: a complete printed transport manifest on a form provided by LCB, containing all information required by LCB, must be kept with the product at all times. LCB's March 2026 manifest guide (CIB 140) states a manifest is created in CCRS, is required every time cannabis moves between licensees, must be generated before departure and be with the product during transport, and includes origin/destination, type(s) and amount, departure/arrival times, and driver/vehicle details. Tribes with compacts may use their own manifest.
  • (4) Records of transportation must be kept minimum three years at the licensee's location, subject to inspection.
  • (5) Transport requirements: (a) only the licensee, an employee of the licensee, a transportation licensee, or a certified testing lab may transport product and/or occupy a transporting vehicle; (b) drivers/occupants must be 21 or older; (c) product must be in a sealed package/container approved by LCB under WAC 314-55-105; (d) sealed packages/containers cannot be opened during transport; (e) product must be in a locked, safe and secure storage compartment secured to the inside body/compartment of the vehicle; (f) vehicle must travel directly from shipping licensee to receiving licensee and must not make unnecessary stops except to other facilities receiving product; (g) live plants may be transported in a fully enclosed, windowless locked trailer, or in a secured area within the inside body/compartment of a van or box truck — a secured area is where solid/locking metal partitions, cages, or high-strength shatterproof acrylic create a secure compartment in the fully enclosed van/box truck, free of windows; live plants may not be transported in the bed of a pickup truck, a sports utility vehicle, or a passenger car.
  • (6) Any vehicle assigned for transporting cannabis/usable cannabis/concentrates/infused products is considered an extension of the licensed premises; transport vehicles are subject to inspection by LCB enforcement officers at any licensed location or while en route.
  • LCB Secure Transportation Storage Guide (CIB 82, May 2023, official LCB guide): products must have at least two levels of locked protection — first a lockable vehicle, second a storage compartment locked and secured to the vehicle body. Compliant examples: lockable totes/boxes secured to the body, lockable cage/metal netting secured to the body, lockable drawers/cabinets secured to the body. Securing with zip ties, straps, bungees, tape or similar methods does not meet WAC 314-55-085. Only licensed producers, processors, retailers, transportation licensees, research licensees, registered cooperatives, qualifying patients, designated providers, certified third-party testing labs, and Tribes with compacts may transport cannabis exceeding the personal possession/delivery limits in RCW 69.50.360(3) and RCW 69.50.4013.
  • Transporter licence itself: Run 1 — application $250 + fingerprint vendor fees; annual $1,300 per WAC 314-55-310 snippet, Verify current figure; transport/delivery between licensed businesses within Washington only; no consumer delivery.

11.5 Waste disposal — WAC 314-55-097

From official search excerpts; certified 9 April 2025 in the excerpt. Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-097 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) Solid and liquid wastes generated during production/processing must be stored, managed and disposed of per applicable state and local laws/regulations. “Sellable cannabis waste” means solid waste generated during production/processing with a THC concentration of 0.3% or less and not designated dangerous or hazardous.
  • (2) Wastewater generated during production/processing must be disposed of per applicable state and local laws/regulations.
  • (3) Wastes must be evaluated against dangerous-waste regulations (chapter 70A.300 RCW and chapter 173-303 WAC) to determine if they designate as dangerous waste.
  • Non-dangerous/non-hazardous waste that is not sold as provided in the section must be rendered unusable before leaving the licensed premises or certified laboratory. Rendering unusable involves grinding the waste and mixing it with other ground materials so the resulting mixture is at least 50% noncannabis waste by volume; other methods may be proposed but must receive prior LCB approval. Acceptable mixing materials include, but are not limited to: compostable mixed waste such as food waste, yard waste, vegetable greases/oils, or other LCB-approved compostable materials; noncompostable mixed waste such as paper waste, plastic waste, cardboard waste, or other LCB-approved noncompostable materials.
  • Rendered-unusable waste may be delivered to a permitted solid-waste facility for final disposition: compostable mixed waste to compost, anaerobic digester, or other facility with jurisdictional health department approval; noncompostable mixed waste to landfill, incinerator, or other facility with jurisdictional health department approval. Alternatively, rendered-unusable waste may be managed on-site by the licensee/lab per chapter 173-350 WAC standards.
  • Licensees are responsible for accurate/complete records of all sellable waste sales; licensees and labs are responsible for accurate/complete records of all evaluations, rendering and disposal activities; all required records must be kept per WAC 314-55-087. LCB may conduct inspections/audits for compliance with the section.
  • Sellable-waste sale: the excerpt states “The cannabis waste will not leave the state of Washington as part of the sale or delivery.” The full sale conditions (buyer eligibility, record contents, THC testing for sellable waste) were not fully reproduced — mark those details Verify unless the full current WAC 314-55-097 page is opened.
  • Legislative background from an LCB March 2025 CR-103 memo (search result): SSB 5376 (chapter 243, Laws of 2024) allowed producers/processors to sell cannabis waste to non-licensees; final rules filed with an effective date of 13 April 2025 (31 days after filing). Present the 2024 Act + 2025 rule as the sellable-waste authority, not as an older prohibition. Source: https://storage.googleapis.com/co-gcs-replication/organizations/washington_state_liquor_and_cannabis_board/events/2025/2025_03_12-wslcb-board_meeting/2025_03_12-wslcb-board_meeting-meeting_materials-5a-cannabis-sb_5376_implementation-cr_103-v1.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.
  • A Court of Appeals Division II appellant brief (Sunshine Tiki Hut, No. 55380-5-II) argued LCB misapplied WAC 314-55-097(6)(a)(ii) on a small incinerator not being a permitted/licensed disposal facility. This is advocacy in a brief, not a holding — do not cite it as decided law.

11.6 Environmental rules — water, pesticides, energy

11.6.1 Energy — Unavailable as a standalone LCB standard

No cannabis-specific energy rule was located in WAC chapter 314-55 in this run. State energy requirements for cannabis licensees as a standalone LCB energy cap/standard as Unavailable; do not invent a kWh, lighting or HVAC standard. Local building, fire, electrical, mechanical, energy and plumbing codes apply through local permitting (LCB greenhouse fact sheet, below).

11.6.2 Water — Department of Ecology guidance, Publication No. 14-11-003, April 2023 (revised)

“In 2012, Washington voters approved Initiative 502, which allows for the production, possession, delivery, and sale of cannabis. The initiative makes cannabis subject to the same water use regulations as any other industry in Washington State.”

  • A Water Right Permit from Ecology is needed for groundwater withdrawals over 5,000 gallons per day or any surface-water withdrawal.
  • A Water Right Permit Exemption is available for groundwater withdrawals of no more than 5,000 gallons per day; this is a “small use”; although no permit is needed, the withdrawal is still subject to the same requirements as a permitted water right; only one groundwater exemption is allowed for any one project, regardless of size. The industrial exemption (5,000 gpd for commercial/industrial purpose, including indoor and outdoor use) may be used to cultivate cannabis. If more water is needed, a water-right permit is required. Water-management rules in certain areas may prohibit new groundwater uses or impose conditions such as “water-for-water” mitigation.
  • Rainwater collection systems are allowed in Washington without requiring a water right; collected rainwater may be stored for later use; groundwater from exempt wells may be pumped to a rainwater collection system and stored until needed for beneficial use as long as the 5,000 gpd limit is not exceeded.
  • Source: https://apps.ecology.wa.gov/publications/documents/1411003.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.

11.6.3 SEPA and local environmental permitting — LCB Greenhouse Cannabis Producer fact sheet

Official LCB fact sheet, older; treat as guidance, not a current permit list. Source: https://lcb.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/Marijuana/I-502/fact-sheets/Greenhouse Cannabis Producer- Fact Sheet.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Producers may be subject to additional local, state and regional regulations with separate timelines/costs. Local governments administer business licensing and building, fire, electrical, mechanical, energy and plumbing codes. State Environmental Policy Act (SEPA) is an environmental review process, not a permit; LCB completed a SEPA review for the cannabis licensing rules. If SEPA is required, applicants must complete a checklist describing possible environmental impacts; processes such as wastewater and solid-waste disposal, CO2 use in the growing cycle, and odours may be included.

11.6.4 Pesticides — WAC 314-55-084

From official search excerpt; WSR 22-14-111, filed 6 July 2022, effective 6 August 2022. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-084 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) Only the following may be used in cannabis production: (a) pesticides registered by WSDA under chapter 15.58 RCW as allowed for use in production, processing and handling of cannabis, used consistent with label requirements; (b) commercial fertilisers registered by WSDA under chapter 15.54 RCW; (c) potting soil, crop production aids, soil amendments and other growing media commercially available in Washington. Producers growing outdoors are not required to meet land-eligibility requirements in 7 C.F.R. Part 205.202.
  • (2) Prohibited products include plant growth regulators not allowed for use on food crops, including: ancymidol; chlormequat chloride; clofencet; colchicine; colloidal silver; daminozide; dikegulac-sodium; flumetralin; flurprimidol; paclobutrazol. Vitamin-hormone products not intended for use on food crops are prohibited. Products containing the insecticide DDVP (dichlorvos) are prohibited in all areas where cannabis is being grown or processed.
  • (3) Soil amendments, fertilisers, growing media, other crop production aids and pesticides that do not conform to subsections (1) and (2) cannot be used, kept or stored on the licensed premises.

11.6.5 Pesticide action levels — WAC 314-55-108

From official search excerpt; certified 20 February 2023 in the excerpt. Source: http://app.leg.wa.gov/WAC/default.aspx?cite=314-55-108&pdf=true — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Only pesticides allowed under WAC 314-55-084 may be used, and they must be WSDA-registered under chapter 15.58 RCW. If LCB, WSDA, another LCB designee or a certified lab identifies a pesticide not allowed under subsection (1) above the action levels, that lot/batch has failed quality-control testing and may be subject to recall under WAC 314-55-225. Action levels are tabled by analyte; the action level for all other pesticides not listed or not allowed is 0.1 ppm. Table examples (µg/g / ppm): abamectin (sum of isomers) 0.50; acephate 0.40; acequinocyl 2.0; acetamiprid 0.20; aldicarb 0.40; azoxystrobin 0.20; bifenazate 0.20; bifenthrin 0.20; boscalid 0.40; carbaryl 0.20; chlorpyrifos 0.20; cyfluthrin 1.0; cypermethrin 1.0; daminozide 1.0; DDVP (dichlorvos) 0.10; diazinon 0.20; dimethoate 0.20; ethoprophos 0.20; etofenprox 0.40; etoxazole 0.20; fenpyroximate 0.40; fipronil 0.40; flonicamid 1.0. Do not reproduce the full action-level table from secondary summaries without opening the current WAC 314-55-108 page.

A licensed producer/processor that provided a sample failing quality-control testing must dispose of the entire lot/batch as provided by waste-disposal requirements in WAC 314-55-097 and document disposal under traceability (WAC 314-55-083(4)) and recordkeeping (WAC 314-55-087), except as otherwise provided in WAC 314-55-102(6) for Board-approved extraction/remediation. A sample not above action levels but showing a disallowed pesticide may still be subject to an administrative violation if the disallowed pesticide was applied.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

11.7.1 Trade samples — WAC 314-55-096, opened directly on 2026-11-01

WSR 25-08-032, filed 26 March 2025, effective 26 April 2025; this amendment replaced the former “vendor, educational” sample regime with “trade samples” and changed monthly limits to calendar-quarter limits. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/wac/default.aspx?cite=314-55-096 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) Trade samples are samples of usable cannabis, infused products and concentrates provided free for negotiating a sale and providing education to budtenders engaged in selling cannabis at retail. They may be provided by a producer to a processor, or by a processor to a retailer. They must be provided free of charge to the receiving licensee and only to a licensee that requested or is consenting to receive the sample. Permitted uses: (i) negotiating a sale on product not currently carried; (ii) educating budtenders on new products the retailer has not previously offered for sale to the public; (iii) educating new budtenders on existing products the retailer sells.
  • A trade-sample unit must be representative of the product offered for sale; not larger than the smallest unit offered for individual sale at retail; and not exceed per unit: (A) 3.5 grams of cannabis; (B) 1 gram of cannabis concentrate; (C) 100 milligrams of infused product in solid or liquid form meant to be ingested orally, applied topically, or otherwise taken into the body, containing not more than 10 mg active delta-9 THC per serving consistent with WAC 314-55-095.
  • Calendar-quarter limits: producers may not provide any one licensed processor more than 96 trade-sample units of cannabis flower per calendar quarter; processors may not provide any one licensed retail business more than 120 trade-sample units of any combination of usable cannabis, concentrates or infused products per calendar quarter.
  • Producer/processor must: record the amount of each trade sample by product type and the trade name of the receiving licensee in the state traceability system; clearly label each outgoing sample as a “trade sample” and itemise trade samples on a separate transport manifest from products intended for resale; package/label per chapter 314-55 WAC plus an additional label in bold, clear and conspicuous lettering, not obscuring other required labelling: “TRADE SAMPLE – NOT FOR RESALE OR DONATION.”; locate/store all trade samples in a designated area separate from nonsample products for sale to another licensee; not provide trade samples as a condition for a retailer to purchase the producer/processor's products or in any manner causing undue influence over another licensee/industry member; provide the receiving licensee with the certificate of analysis for all QA/QC tests on the lot/batch from which the sample was derived; disclose all chemicals applied to the growing medium and plants during production/processing, including pesticides, fungicides, herbicides, rodenticides, nutrients, fertilisers and pH control; and disclose all solvents and other chemicals used during processing of a concentrate/infused product, including additives and ingredients.
  • Receiving licensee must receive the trade sample in the traceability system before sampling. Trade samples may only be given to and used by licensees or current paid employees of the licensee as detailed in a written business policy. They may not be sold, nor provided as employee compensation, incentive or reward; they may only be given free of charge to paid employees.
  • Retailers: must not require a producer/processor to provide free trade samples as a condition for purchasing or in any manner causing undue influence; must locate trade samples in a designated area separate from nonsample products for resale and clearly identified as trade samples (sample jars as described in subsection (4) may be displayed/located on the counter for customers to smell); may provide free trade samples to current paid employees; must track all incoming/outgoing trade-sample inventory in the traceability system by product type; must record the sample amount provided to each employee in the traceability system including product type and employee name; must not provide more than 30 trade-sample units to any one employee within a calendar quarter.
  • (2) Retailers may not provide free samples to customers.
  • (3) Internal quality-control sampling: producers/processors may conduct limited self-sampling for internal QC. Internal QC samples are not the same as WAC 314-55-101 lab-testing samples. All sample limits are calendar-quarter based. Consuming internal QC samples may not take place on the licensed premises. Internal QC samples do not need to comply with chapter 314-55 packaging/labelling requirements. Only the producer, processor or their paid employees may sample flower, usable cannabis, infused products, concentrates and edible infused product. The producer/processor must record the amount of each sample and the employee(s) conducting sampling in the traceability system. A unit means: (i) 1 gram cannabis flower; (ii) 1 gram usable cannabis; (iii) 10 mg THC in an infused product in edible or liquor form; (iv) 1 gram cannabis concentrate, including infused joints, vapor products and dabs. Producers may provide up to 50 sample units per employee per calendar quarter; processors up to 50 internal QC units per employee per quarter in any combination, with no more than 25 concentrate units per employee per quarter; a 1-gram concentrate unit may be a combination of concentrate products; licensees holding producer + processor at the same location are limited to 50 combined units per employee per quarter.
  • (4) Sample jars for retail display: must be labelled as a display sample jar, with the providing licensed entity's UBI number and trade name, and weight in ounces/grams or volume as applicable. A processor may provide a retailer free usable cannabis in a transparent sample jar protected by a plastic/metal mesh screen to allow customers to view/smell before purchase; the jar may contain no more than 3.5 grams; the mesh must be sealed onto the container, free of rips/tears/holes greater than 2 mm in diameter. Neither the sample jar nor the usable cannabis within may be sold, transferred, given away, or otherwise provided to a customer. Leftover display-jar cannabis may either be given to paid retail employees free of charge (recorded in traceability, counting toward the 30-unit quarterly employee maximum in (1)(j)(vi)); disposed of per WAC 314-55-097 and recorded per WAC 314-55-083; or returned to the providing processor for disposal per WAC 314-55-097 and recorded per WAC 314-55-083.
  • (5) Outgoing/return trade samples and sample jars must adhere to WAC 314-55-085 transport requirements.
  • (6) By 1 September 2025, all samples previously identified as vendor or educational samples must be disposed of per WAC 314-55-097.

Do not use the pre-April 2025 vendor/educational limits (e.g. 8 grams per month) as current law; those were superseded by WSR 25-08-032.

11.7.2 Advertising, coupons, giveaways — WAC 314-55-155 + RCW 69.50.369

From official search excerpts; full current page not opened in this run. Sources: http://app.leg.wa.gov/WAC/default.aspx?cite=314-55-155&pdf=true — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://lcb.wa.gov/enforcement/cannabis_advertising_faqs — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/WALCB/bulletins/2c824f7 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

General prohibitions in the rule: advertising/labels must not contain any statement or illustration that is false or misleading; promotes overconsumption; represents cannabis use as having curative or therapeutic effects; or is designed to be especially appealing to children or persons under 21. Advertising on publicly owned/operated property is prohibited under subsection (3)(c) in the excerpts. Outdoor advertising is restricted, including billboard/sign rules and the 1,000-foot proximity context in secondary summaries — the exact current outdoor-advertising subsection text must be confirmed from the current WAC 314-55-155 page.

Sign count conflict — preserve, do not resolve by picking the older text: Run 1 Part 0/Part 1 recorded ESB 5206 advertising expansion from 2 to 4 signs, WSR 26-12-082, effective 4 July 2026. The LCB advertising FAQ excerpt found on 2026-11-01 still states the older rule: each retail premises limited to two signs identifying the outlet by business/trade name, affixed/hanging in windows or on the outside of the premises visible from the public right-of-way, limited to 1,600 square inches. A 2026 CR-103 excerpt states WAC 314-55-155(1)(d) was added so trade-name signs may include a business logo/artwork that does not depict cannabis plants/products and complies with other requirements, and WAC 314-55-155(3)(a) was added so the 1,600-square-inch limitation refers to the actual sign itself, not text/content within it, enforced by measuring the physical perimeter. At assembly time, open the current WAC 314-55-155 text and the WSR 26-12-082 rule text; if they cannot be opened, present the sign count as Verify with both the Run 1 (4 signs) and FAQ (2 signs) versions flagged.

Coupons: from the CR-103 excerpt, coupons may not discount the price of a product below the retailer's acquisition cost. Incidental items provided must not be subject/conditional on other purchases as described in WAC 314-55-017(2). Promotional items / giveaways / branded or unbranded merchandise distribution is prohibited under the older WAC 314-55-155(4) as summarised in LCB Enforcement and Education Bulletin No. 21-01, including t-shirts, stickers, drinking glasses, lanyards and general swag; requiring purchase of a non-cannabis item (pipes, bongs, rolling papers, lighters, branded merchandise) to obtain cannabis is prohibited as a conditional sale under WAC 314-55-017; licensees may sell paraphernalia together with cannabis as separate items or in branded packaging, but cannabis purchase cannot be contingent on purchasing the paraphernalia item. RCW 69.50.585 allows producers/processors to provide retailers and their employees with branded promotional items of nominal value, bearing only the providing producer/processor's advertising matter, and not to be forwarded to retail customers through purchase or giveaway (per the same bulletin).

“Giveaway” does not include representative samples of products (edible products and topicals only) carried by a licensed retailer that are not infused with cannabis and are offered to customers on licensed retail premises for sampling purposes only.

Medical-endorsement retailers: WAC 314-55-155(6) as excerpted — retail licensees holding a medical cannabis endorsement may donate or provide free of charge products only to qualifying patients or designated providers who hold a valid recognition card; they may only advertise “free” or “donated” cannabis concentrates, usable cannabis or infused products identified by the Department as a compliant product in chapter 246-70 WAC and tested to chapter 246-70 WAC standards; they may only advertise “free”/“donated” products to qualifying patients.

Advertising/marketing that specifically targets persons residing outside Washington is prohibited (from the rule summaries in search results — confirm subsection number from the current WAC page).

Direct mail / inserts: LCB FAQ states inserts may not contain coupons; advertising on community college/university publications, premises and dorms/sorority/shared housing is not permitted in the FAQ because a significant portion of that demographic is under 21, and WAC 314-55-155(3)(c) prohibits advertising on/in publicly owned/operated property. Sponsorships are not specifically prohibited except that sponsorship activity may not violate WAC 314-55-155 prohibitions. Customers may register with a store for future emails for announcements/promotions.

11.7.3 Discounts in general

No general “discounts are prohibited” rule was located. The concrete discount rules found are the coupon floor (not below acquisition cost) and the conditional-sale / tied-house prohibitions above. Do not assert a broader discount ban. Price promotions to consumers must still comply with advertising, giveaway, coupon and conditional-sale rules; medical donations are the only free-product-to-consumer route found, and only to recognition-card holders at endorsed retailers.

11.8 Drug testing, background checks, employee rules

11.8.1 SB 5123 → RCW 49.44.240 (Run 1 verbatim, to repeat)

Source: https://apps.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=49.44.240 — Accessed 2026-11-01; history [2023 c 359 §2; signed 9 May 2023; effective 1 January 2024]; legislative finding (2023 c 359 §1): tests showing nonpsychoactive metabolites from use up to 30 days in the past “have no correlation to an applicant's future job performance.”

  • (1) It is unlawful for an employer to discriminate against a person in initial hiring if discrimination is based on: (a) the person's use of cannabis off the job and away from the workplace; or (b) an employer-required drug screening test finding nonpsychoactive cannabis metabolites in hair, blood, urine or other bodily fluids.
  • (2) Nothing in the section: (a) prohibits basing initial hiring decisions on scientifically valid drug screening through methods that do not screen for nonpsychoactive cannabis metabolites; (b) affects rights/obligations to maintain a drug- and alcohol-free workplace or any rights/obligations required by federal law/regulation; or (c) applies to testing for controlled substances other than preemployment, such as post-accident testing or testing because of suspicion of impairment or being under the influence of alcohol, controlled substances, medications or other substances.
  • (3) The section does not apply to an applicant seeking: (a) a position requiring a federal government background investigation or security clearance; (b) a position with a general-authority Washington law-enforcement agency as defined in RCW 10.93.020; (c) a position with a fire department, fire protection district, or regional fire protection service authority — plus, per secondary summaries of the full subsection in Run 1, other first responders including 911 dispatchers, corrections positions with direct access to persons in custody, airline/aerospace industry positions, and safety-sensitive positions where impairment presents a substantial risk of death, which the employer must identify before the applicant applies. The latter list was secondary in Run 1 — verify the full RCW 49.44.240(3) text at assembly time before presenting it as verbatim.
  • (4)(a) The section does not preempt state/federal laws requiring testing for controlled substances, including as a condition of federal funding, licensing-related benefits, or a federal contract. (b) Employers may require testing for a spectrum of controlled substances, which may include cannabis, as long as cannabis results are not provided to the employer.
  • (5) “Cannabis” has the meaning in RCW 69.50.101.

Scope limits to state plainly: initial hiring only; does not protect current employees from discipline for on-the-job impairment or post-hire testing; does not require tolerating workplace use/impairment; does not override federal DOT / federal-contract testing; an employer may still use a scientifically valid test that does not screen for nonpsychoactive metabolites. Washington has no general statute protecting current employees' lawful off-duty cannabis use — Run 1 recorded this as Verify whether any 2025–2026 bill proposed an expansion, with none listed as enacted in Run 1 §1.9.

Interaction for licence holders: SB 5123 governs employers generally, including cannabis licensees as employers, but it does not displace LCB licence-holder background-check, badge, age and transport-occupant rules below, nor federal testing obligations. A cannabis licensee cannot use SB 5123 to avoid LCB applicant/true-party/financier checks.

11.8.2 Licence-holder background checks — WAC 314-55-020 / 314-55-040 / 314-55-045

Qualification includes a background check consistent with WAC 314-55-040 and 314-55-045. The check consists of a personal/criminal history form provided by the Board and submission of fingerprints to a Board-approved vendor; the applicant pays all vendor fingerprinting fees; fingerprints are submitted to the Washington State Patrol and the FBI for comparison to their criminal records; the applicant pays all WSP/FBI fees. Financiers are subject to criminal-history investigations equivalent to the licence applicant and pay the associated fees.

The Board will conduct random background checks at renewal requiring the licensee to submit fingerprints for evaluation from the approved vendor, with the licensee responsible for all fees (from WSR 22-16-119 / WSR 22-21-058 excerpts; the fee figure in those excerpts, $1,381, is the pre-July 2026 licence fee and is stale for fee purposes).

WAC 314-55-040 is titled “Cannabis applicant or licensee background checks” and WAC 314-55-045 is titled “What marijuana law or rule violation history might prevent an applicant from receiving a marijuana license?” in the chapter index. The specific disqualifying criminal-history / violation-history thresholds were not opened in full in this run — do not invent a list of disqualifying offences; mark the thresholds Verify unless the current WAC 314-55-040 / 314-55-045 pages are opened.

RCW 69.50.331(1) (from a 2018 Justia reproduction, secondary for current text): for considering an application or renewal, the Board must conduct a comprehensive, fair and impartial evaluation; may inspect premises and inquire into construction/operation; may consider prior criminal conduct including administrative violation history and a criminal-history record check submitted to WSP and FBI; must require fingerprinting of any applicant whose check is submitted to the FBI; RCW 9.95.240 and chapter 9.96A RCW do not apply to these cases; the Board may in its discretion grant/deny renewal or the licence applied for, and denial may be based without limitation on chronic illegal activity documented in objections under subsections (7)(c) and (10). Local/tribal/port objections: the incorporated city/town, county legislative authority, tribal government or port authority may file written objections within 20 days after transmittal of notice for applications, or at least 30 days before expiration for renewals (from the same excerpt — verify current subsection numbering at assembly time).

Source for chapter index: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/law/WACArchive/2022/pdf/WAC%20314%20TITLE/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20CHAPTER/WAC%20314%20-%2055%20COMBINEDCHAPTER.backup_2022_01_26_12_15_03.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.

11.8.3 Employee rules on premises

From WAC 314-55-083, opened: badge with trade name, full legal name and photograph; state ID available; visitors (other than retail customers) badged and logged; logs kept 3 years; ID to LCB enforcement on request. Transport occupants must be 21+ (WAC 314-55-085(5)(b)). Retail sales are to persons 21+ (and registered patients at endorsed stores) per Run 1. A general “all cannabis employees must be 21” rule, a budtender permit, and a minors-on-premises rule were not confirmed from an opened WAC 314-55-079 / 314-55-015 page in this run — the budtender permit appears only in the HB 1932 proposal (see Part 12), not current law. Mark any general employee-age statement beyond transport/retail-sale age as Verify.

11.8.4 Recordkeeping anchor for operations

WAC 314-55-087 is cited throughout for records (transport 3 years; visitor logs 3 years; waste evaluation/rendering/disposal records; excise-tax records). The full WAC 314-55-087 retention schedule was not opened in this run — do not state a single universal retention period; state the period only where the specific rule states it (3 years for transport and visitor logs as above).

11.9 Research and university licences

11.9.1 RCW 69.50.372, cannabis research licence

From official PDF excerpts; certified 1 April 2026 in one excerpt and 9 June 2026 in another — use the current app.leg.wa.gov text at assembly time. Sources: https://lawfilestestext.leg.wa.gov/lawtest/RCWPDF/RCW%20%2069%20%20TITLE/RCW%20%2069%20.%2050%20%20CHAPTER/RCW%20%2069%20.%2050%20.372.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01; http://apptest.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=69.50.372&pdf=true — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) A cannabis research licence permits a licensee to produce, process and possess cannabis for the following limited research purposes only: (a) to test chemical potency and composition levels; (b) to conduct clinical investigations of cannabis-derived drug products; (c) to conduct research on the efficacy and safety of administering cannabis as part of medical treatment; and (d) to conduct genomic or agricultural research.
  • (2) As part of the application, the applicant must submit to the Board's designated scientific reviewer a description of the intended research. The Board must select a scientific reviewer to review project quality, study design, value or impact, among other criteria in the full subsection. If the scientific reviewer determines the project does not meet subsection (1), the application must be denied.
  • (3) A research licensee may only sell cannabis grown within its operation to other cannabis research licensees. The Board may revoke a research licence for violations of this subsection.
  • (4) A research licensee may contract with the University of Washington or Washington State University to perform research in conjunction with the university. All research projects, not including those conducted under a contract entered into under RCW 28B.20.502(3), must be approved by the scientific reviewer and meet subsection (1).
  • (5) In establishing the licence, the Board may adopt rules on application requirements, renewal requirements (including whether additional projects may be added/considered), amounts of plants / usable cannabis / concentrates / infused products a licensee may hold, and other matters listed in the full subsection — the full subsection (5) list was not reproduced in this run; mark its details Verify unless opened.
  • (6) Production, processing, possession, delivery, donation and sale in accordance with the section, RCW 69.50.366(3) and implementing rules, by a validly licensed cannabis researcher, shall not be a criminal or civil offence under Washington state law (from the FindLaw excerpt — verify the exact subsection (6) wording from the official text).
  • (7) Fees: a 2016 Justia reproduction states application fee $250 and annual issuance/renewal fee $1,000. Conflict to preserve: Run 1 recorded the LCB licence-types page as stating a $250 application fee but the annual fee as Verify / Unavailable, and former secondary figures as stale/conflicting and not to be used. Do NOT present $1,000 as the confirmed current annual fee unless the current RCW 69.50.372(7) text is opened at assembly time; if it cannot be opened, keep the annual fee as Verify and note the 2016 $1,000 figure as historical text only.
  • (9) “Scientific reviewer” means an organisation that convenes or contracts with persons who have training/experience in research practice and research methodology to determine whether a project meets the licence criteria and to review reports submitted by research licensees; scientific reviewers include, but are not limited to, educational institutions and research institutions (from the FindLaw excerpt — verify full definition).

LCB research-licence administration (Run 1, LCB licence-types page, opened in Run 1): application $250, submitted through Business Licensing Services; applicant then receives an LCB letter directing submission of additional materials directly to the designated scientific reviewer; if the reviewer does not approve, the application is withdrawn with no refund. Research facilities must be completely separate and distinct from other cannabis businesses, except actively licensed producer/processor licensees, where a shared entrance/exit is allowed but all cannabis grown/possessed must be for research purposes. Labs certified by LCB for quality-assurance testing may apply for a research licence.

11.9.2 University authority — RCW 28B.20.502

Official text excerpt. Source: https://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?Cite=28B.20.502 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) “The University of Washington and Washington State University may conduct scientific research on the efficacy and safety of administering cannabis as part of medical treatment. As part of this research, the University of Washington and Washington State University may develop and conduct studies to ascertain the general medical safety and efficacy of cannabis, and may develop medical guidelines for the appropriate administration and use of cannabis.”
  • (2) UW and WSU may, in accordance with RCW 69.50.372, contract with cannabis research licensees to conduct research permitted under this section and RCW 69.50.372.
  • (3) UW and WSU may contract to conduct cannabis research with an entity licensed to conduct such research by a federally recognised Indian tribe located within the geographical boundaries of Washington.
  • (4) “Cannabis” has the meaning in RCW 69.50.101.

No specific UW or WSU research programme, project list, or count of university research licences was located in this run — state as Unavailable, do not invent a university programme name or project count. Hemp research is a separate matter (WSDA / USDA hemp programme) and belongs to Part 7 in the parent's combined file, not to this research-licence section.

PART 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke shops

12.1.1 RCW 69.50.412 (official text excerpt, app.leg.wa.gov)

Source: http://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=69.50.412 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) It is unlawful to use drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, or prepare a controlled substance other than cannabis. Violation is a misdemeanor.
  • (2) It is unlawful to deliver, possess with intent to deliver, or manufacture with intent to deliver drug paraphernalia, knowing or under circumstances where one reasonably should know it will be used to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, or prepare a controlled substance other than cannabis. Violation is a misdemeanor.
  • (3) Any person 18 or older who violates subsection (2) by delivering drug paraphernalia to a person under 18 who is at least three years younger is guilty of a gross misdemeanor.
  • (4) It is unlawful to place in any newspaper, magazine, handbill or other publication any advertisement, knowing or under circumstances where one reasonably should know the purpose is to promote sale of objects designed/intended for use as drug paraphernalia. Violation is a misdemeanor.
  • (5) It is lawful for any person over 18 to possess sterile hypodermic syringes and needles for the purpose of reducing blood-borne diseases.
  • History note in the excerpt: [2022 c 16 §91; 2021 c 311 §14; 2019 c 64 §22; prior 2013 c 3 §22 (Initiative Measure No. 502, approved 6 November 2012)].

Key distinction: RCW 69.50.102 still defines drug paraphernalia broadly (including bongs, water pipes, glass pipes, carburetion tubes and similar items, per secondary summaries), but RCW 69.50.412's offences as amended by I-502 apply only to controlled substances other than cannabis. For adults 21+, owning/using a bong for cannabis is therefore not a RCW 69.50.412 paraphernalia offence on the statute's own terms; selling paraphernalia for non-cannabis controlled substances remains covered.

Age distinction — do not collapse: The paraphernalia statute itself uses 18 in subsections (3) and (5). Separately, secondary summaries state Washington tobacco/vapor sales to persons under 21 are prohibited under RCW 26.28.080 (Engrossed Substitute Senate Bill 1074, effective 1 January 2020) and federal Tobacco 21 law, and that licensed cannabis retailers sell only to persons 21+. A smoke shop selling a bong alongside tobacco/vapor products therefore faces a 21-year practical floor from the tobacco/vapor framework, even though RCW 69.50.412 does not itself impose 21 for paraphernalia. Present both ages with their separate statutes; do not state a single “paraphernalia age is 21” rule as RCW 69.50.412 law.

Retailer sale of paraphernalia: Run 1 retailer privileges include selling usable cannabis, concentrates, infused products, paraphernalia and lockable boxes at retail to persons 21+ (and registered patients at endorsed stores). That is the licensed-retailer route; it is distinct from a general smoke shop.

Local regulation example only: A City of Sumner ordinance (Ord. 2769, amending Sumner Municipal Code chapter 9.20) recites that RCW 69.50.412 does not expressly regulate possession of drug paraphernalia, which is left to local governments, and adopts local rules including: no owner/manager/proprietor of a business selling/displaying smoking/injection paraphernalia may allow a person under 18 to be/remain in/enter/visit such a room unless accompanied by a parent/legal guardian; display for sale must be in a separate room/enclosure from which unaccompanied minors are excluded, with each entrance signposted. This is a local example, not a statewide smoke-shop licensing rule. Source: https://sumner.civicweb.net/document/22070 — Accessed 2026-11-01. No statewide smoke-shop licence was located in this run — mark a statewide smoke-shop licence as Unavailable / not located if no such licence is found at assembly time.

12.2 THC driving limits and impairment testing — per se vs impairment

12.2.1 RCW 46.61.502, opened directly on 2026-11-01 (verbatim operative text)

Source: http://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=46.61.502 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) “A person is guilty of driving while under the influence of intoxicating liquor, cannabis, or any drug if the person drives a vehicle within this state: (a) And the person has, within two hours after driving, an alcohol concentration of 0.08 or higher as shown by analysis of the person's breath or blood made under RCW 46.61.506; or (b) The person has, within two hours after driving, a THC concentration of 5.00 or higher as shown by analysis of the person's blood made under RCW 46.61.506; or (c) While the person is under the influence of or affected by intoxicating liquor, cannabis, or any drug; or (d) While the person is under the combined influence of or affected by intoxicating liquor, cannabis, and any drug.”
  • (2) “The fact that a person charged with a violation of this section is or has been entitled to use a drug under the laws of this state shall not constitute a defense against a charge of violating this section.”
  • (3)(b) Affirmative defence to (1)(b), which the defendant must prove by a preponderance of the evidence, that the defendant consumed a sufficient quantity of cannabis after driving and before blood analysis to cause THC concentration to be 5.00 or more within two hours after driving. The court shall not admit evidence of this defence unless the defendant notifies the prosecution before the omnibus or pretrial hearing of intent to assert it.
  • (4)(b) Analyses of blood samples obtained more than two hours after alleged driving may be used as evidence that within two hours the person had a THC concentration of 5.00 or more in violation of (1)(b), and in any case in which the analysis shows a THC concentration above 0.00 may be used as evidence that the person was under the influence of or affected by cannabis in violation of (1)(c) or (d).
  • (5) Except as provided in subsection (6), a violation is a gross misdemeanor.
  • (6) It is a class B felony punishable under chapter 9.94A RCW, or chapter 13.40 RCW if the person is a juvenile, if: (a) the person has three or more prior offences within 15 years as defined in RCW 46.61.5055; or (b) the person has ever previously been convicted of vehicular homicide while under the influence (RCW 46.61.520(1)(a)), vehicular assault while under the influence (RCW 46.61.522(1)(b)), a comparable out-of-state offence, or a violation of subsection (6) or RCW 46.61.504(6).

Per se vs permissible inference — the distinction required:

  • Per se prong, RCW 46.61.502(1)(b): THC concentration of 5.00 or higher in blood within two hours after driving, as shown by analysis under RCW 46.61.506, is itself sufficient for guilt under that prong; the State does not need to prove observable impairment under (1)(b). Secondary sources express the threshold as 5.00 nanograms per millilitre (ng/mL) in whole blood; the RCW 46.61.502 text opened states “THC concentration of 5.00” without spelling out the unit in that subsection — at assembly time, confirm the unit definition from RCW 46.61.506 / the THC-concentration definition before presenting “ng/mL” as statutory text, or present it as the commonly stated unit with that caveat.
  • Impairment prongs, RCW 46.61.502(1)(c)–(d): driving while under the influence of or affected by cannabis (alone or combined) is a separate basis for guilt. A driver below 5.00 can still be charged/prosecuted under (1)(c) if other evidence shows being under the influence/affected — officer observations, driving behaviour, statements, field-sobriety performance, and blood results. Conversely, a late blood sample above 0.00 may be used as evidence for (1)(c)/(d) even if it does not establish (1)(b).
  • Not a “permissible inference” in the (1)(b) prong itself: do not describe (1)(b) as merely an inference the jury may draw; (1)(b) is a per se definition of guilt on the stated blood result. The inference-type language applies to the evidentiary use of late samples under (4)(b) and to impairment proof under (1)(c)/(d). If the parent's combined file elsewhere uses “permissible inference” for a different state's 5 ng rule, do not import that label into Washington's (1)(b) without this qualification.

State v. Fraser, 509 P.3d 282 (Wash. 2022) — secondary summary only, not the opinion opened: the Washington Supreme Court upheld the per se THC prong as constitutional and a legitimate exercise of police power; the defendant's blood level was 9.4 ± 2.5 ng/mL within two hours; the court reasoned the 5.00 ng/mL minimum appears related to recent consumption for most people (including chronic users), linked to impaired driving/highway safety, although there is no scientific correlation to impairment akin to the 0.08% alcohol standard; the limit was held not vague because it avoids arbitrary enforcement, and not facially unconstitutional because some people are impaired at 5.00 ng/mL. Source found: https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/state-v-fraser-98896-908159810 — Accessed 2026-11-01 — label as secondary summary at assembly time unless the opinion itself is opened.

12.2.2 Under-21 driving — RCW 46.61.503 (official text excerpt)

Source: http://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=46.61.503 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) A person is guilty of driving or being in physical control of a motor vehicle after consuming alcohol or cannabis if the person operates or is in physical control within Washington and: (a) is under 21; and (b) has, within two hours after operating/being in physical control, either (i) an alcohol concentration of at least 0.02 but less than the RCW 46.61.502 concentration, by breath/blood analysis under RCW 46.61.506, or (ii) a THC concentration above 0.00 but less than the RCW 46.61.502 concentration, by blood analysis under RCW 46.61.506.
  • (2) Affirmative defence (preponderance) that the person consumed sufficient alcohol/cannabis after driving/physical control and before analysis to cause the concentration to be in violation within two hours.
  • (3) No conviction under the section for being in physical control, and an affirmative defence to a RCW 46.20.308 suspension/revocation/denial action, if before being pursued by law enforcement the person moved the vehicle safely off the roadway.
  • (4) Analyses obtained more than two hours after operating/being in physical control may be used as evidence of a within-two-hours violation.
  • (5) A violation is a misdemeanor (official excerpt numbering; some secondary summaries number the misdemeanor paragraph as (4) — use the official numbering and flag the discrepancy only if both versions are reproduced).
  • At 5.00 or higher, an under-21 driver may instead be prosecuted under the adult RCW 46.61.502 per se rule.

12.2.3 Physical control and test admission — Verify verbatim text

Physical control, RCW 46.61.504: secondary summaries state the same 5.00 THC threshold for actual physical control of a vehicle. The RCW 46.61.504 text was not opened in this run — mark its verbatim text Verify before quoting it.

Impairment-testing method, RCW 46.61.506: this section governs how blood/breath analyses are made and admitted. Its full text was not opened in this run. Do not assert a roadside THC breath-test standard, a specific blood-draw procedure, or an implied-consent consequence for cannabis blood testing beyond what RCW 46.61.506 / RCW 46.20.308 state on opening. Secondary summaries state blood tests are not the only evidence and that officer observations, driving behaviour and field-sobriety performance can prove or disprove impairment under the (1)(c) prong — present that as the impairment-prong evidentiary point, not as a statement that a blood number alone proves impairment under (1)(c).

12.2.4 Vehicle possession/consumption — RCW 46.61.745 (official text excerpt)

Sources: https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Law/RCW/RCW%20%2046%20%20TITLE/RCW%20%2046%20.%2061%20%20CHAPTER/RCW%20%2046%20.%2061%20.745.htm — Accessed 2026-11-01; also http://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=69.50.445 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1)(a) It is a traffic infraction: (i) for the registered owner, or the driver if the owner is not present, or passengers, to keep cannabis in a motor vehicle on a highway unless it is (A) in the trunk, (B) in some other area not normally occupied or directly accessible by driver/passengers if the vehicle has no trunk, or (C) in a package/container/receptacle that has not been opened or the seal broken or contents partially removed — a utility compartment or glove compartment is deemed within the area occupied by driver/passengers; (ii) to consume cannabis in any manner, including smoking or ingesting, in a motor vehicle on the public highway; or (iii) to place cannabis in a container specifically labelled by its manufacturer as containing a noncannabis substance and then violate (a)(i).
  • (1)(b) Rebuttable presumption of a traffic infraction if the original container is incorrectly labelled and there is a subsequent violation of (a)(i).
  • (2) “Cannabis” means all parts of the plant Cannabis, whether growing or not, the seeds, resin extracted from any part, and every compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture or preparation of the plant, its seeds or resin; it does not include mature stalks, fibre produced from stalks, oil/cake made from seeds, any other compound/manufacture/salt/derivative/mixture/preparation of mature stalks except resin extracted therefrom, fibre, oil or cake, or sterilised seed incapable of germination.

Run 1 noted RCW 46.61.745 as a secondary summary to be opened at build time; the excerpt above now supplies the operative text, but the current app.leg.wa.gov page should still be opened at assembly time to confirm no later amendment.

12.3 Vacation of convictions and Governor's pardons — do not conflate

12.3.1 Governor's Marijuana Justice Initiative

Governor's office page, found via search; the search-result URL mapping in that search was mismatched — the governor.wa.gov URL listed did not resolve to the Initiative page in the result list, so at assembly time open the current Governor's Marijuana Justice Initiative page directly and cite its actual URL. Accessed 2026-11-01 (search finding; full page to be re-opened at assembly).

  • The Initiative intends, within existing capacity, to provide clemency relief to some people with these convictions. It provides an expedited process to grant pardons to people with a single misdemeanor conviction on their criminal record for adult marijuana possession prosecuted under Washington state law, with the conviction occurring between 1 January 1998 and 5 December 2012, when I-502 legalised marijuana possession.
  • “Records indicate that roughly 3,500 individuals are eligible under this Initiative.” This is an eligibility estimate, NOT a count of pardons granted. Do not write “3,500 pardoned” or “3,500 convictions pardoned.”
  • Process: the person completes an online petition form to the Office of the Governor; the Governor's office reviews materials and compares them against Administrative Office of the Courts (AOC) data to confirm eligibility; if confirmed eligible, the Governor issues a pardon forgiving the marijuana conviction; if not confirmed eligible, the person is directed to the standard Clemency and Pardons Board channel.
  • Effect limits stated on the page excerpt: the Governor's office will ask the Washington State Patrol to remove pardoned convictions from the criminal-history report available to the public; a pardoned offence remains on a separate criminal history available to law enforcement and others entitled to non-conviction data under chapter 10.97 RCW; pardons do not automatically remove the conviction from court files and do not grant the petitioner the legal authority stated in the truncated excerpt — open the full page at assembly time before completing that sentence.
  • The same page notes a new law allowing individuals to vacate multiple marijuana offence convictions (see vacation statute below) and points to Washington State Courts resources, including a vacation application form (CrRLJ 09.0100) and a court directory.
  • Number of pardons actually granted under the Initiative was not located in this run — state as Unavailable, not as 3,500.

12.3.2 Seattle municipal vacatur (secondary — Verify)

In September 2018, Seattle Municipal Court judges approved a plan, requested by City Attorney Pete Holmes with Mayor Jenny Durkan, to vacate cannabis convictions for up to 542 people sentenced for offences between 1996 and 2010. Under the court's 11 September order as summarised, the City Attorney's office would provide last known addresses, the court would mail notice giving each defendant an opportunity to object or ask for individual consideration, and if individuals did not respond within 33 days of notice being sent, convictions would be vacated automatically. Present the 542 figure as “up to 542 people” under a municipal-court plan, secondary-sourced, Verify against the court order before presenting as a final vacated count.

12.3.3 Vacation statute — RCW 9.96.060(5) (SB 5605, 2019, chapter as passed)

From the enrolled bill text. Sources: https://lawfilestestext.leg.wa.gov/BienniumTest/2019-20/Pdf/Bills/Senate%20Bills/5605.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Biennium/2019-20/Pdf/Amendments/House/5605%20AMH%20GOOD%20H2997.1.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.

“Every person convicted of a misdemeanor marijuana offense, who was twenty-one years of age or older at the time of the offense, may apply to the sentencing court for a vacation of the applicant's record of conviction for the offense.”

A misdemeanor marijuana offence includes, but is not limited to: any offence under RCW 69.50.4014 from 1 July 2004 onward and its predecessor statutes, including RCW 69.50.401(e) from 21 March 1979 to 1 July 2004 and RCW 69.50.401(d) from 21 May 1971 to 21 March 1979, and any offence under an equivalent municipal ordinance. If an applicant qualifies, the court shall vacate (per the enrolled text's “Notwithstanding RCW 9.94A.640, the court shall vacate” language for this subsection).

Vacation is effected by permitting withdrawal of a guilty plea and entry of a not-guilty plea, or setting aside a guilty verdict after a not-guilty plea, and dismissing the information/indictment/complaint/citation and vacating judgment and sentence.

Multiple-conviction question — preserve as Verify: The Governor's page excerpt says a new law allows vacating one or more marijuana possession misdemeanors regardless of when the offence occurred or whether conviction was under state or local jurisdiction. The SB 5605 (2019) text excerpt does not by itself establish that broader “one or more / any date / state or local” formulation.
