At assembly time, open the current RCW 9.96.060 text; if the current text differs from the 2019 enrolled text, cite the current text and its amending act, and do not attribute the broader formulation to SB 5605 alone.

12.3.4 Related vacation routes (secondary summaries — Verify against current statutes before presenting as law)

  • General misdemeanor/gross-misdemeanor vacation under RCW 9.96.060(2): discretionary, with completion of sentence terms and no pending charges / violent-offence / DUI-offence bars as summarised in the bill text — do not reproduce the full bar list without opening current RCW 9.96.060.
  • Felony vacation under RCW 9.94A.640 (Class C felonies after five years if criteria met, per National Reentry Resource Center summary).
  • Treatment-completion vacatur summarised by the same secondary source: if a person convicted under RCW 69.50.4011(1)(b) or (c), 69.50.4013, 69.50.4014, or 69.41.030(2)(b) or (c) completes a substance-use-disorder programme and files proof, or obtains an assessment from a recovery-navigator / arrest-and-jail-alternative / law-enforcement-assisted-diversion programme, with six months' substantial compliance with recommended treatment, the court must vacate the conviction(s). Verify this provision's current citation and scope before including it as statute.
  • No statewide total of marijuana convictions vacated was located — state as Unavailable. Do not add the Governor's ~3,500 eligible figure to the Seattle up-to-542 figure, and do not convert people into convictions or vice versa.

12.4 Consumption rules, lounges, events

12.4.1 Public consumption — RCW 69.50.445 (official text excerpt)

Source: http://app.leg.wa.gov/RCW/default.aspx?cite=69.50.445 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) “It is unlawful to open a package containing cannabis, useable cannabis, cannabis-infused products, or cannabis concentrates, or consume cannabis, useable cannabis, cannabis-infused products, or cannabis concentrates, in view of the general public or in a public place.”
  • (2) “Public place” has the same meaning as defined in RCW 66.04.010, but the exclusions in RCW 66.04.011 do not apply.
  • (3) A violator “is guilty of a class 3 civil infraction under chapter 7.80 RCW.”

This covers opening/consuming in public view or a public place, in any form — it is not limited to smoking. Separately, chapter 70.160 RCW (Smoking in Public Places) prohibits smoking in public places and places of employment (per Seattle special-events guidance, secondary for the chapter's scope — verify chapter 70.160's application to cannabis vapor/smoking at assembly time if the distinction is stated).

12.4.2 No licensed consumption lounge exists in current Washington law

Run 1 and the HB 1932 bill report both state: “No Washington cannabis license currently permits the consumption of cannabis by persons age 21 or over in a public place.”

12.4.3 Events — current law

City of Seattle Special Events guidance (official city page): “Under Washington State Law, marijuana is allowed to be bought or sold in licensed retail marijuana stores only. No marijuana sales are allowed at special events outside of these stores. Washington state law protects private marijuana use, but marijuana cannot be consumed in public view, such as on streets or sidewalks or in public parks.” Source: https://www.seattle.gov/special-events/policies-and-compliance/alcohol-and-marijuana — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Secondary event-market summary (FriendlyTreeVenues, not official): Washington has no licensed cannabis-event pathway; events run as private-property / BYOC gatherings only. Present that characterisation as secondary only, not as LCB guidance.

Do not list a Washington “cannabis lounge” or “consumption event” as operating. Any event listing must be framed as private, non-sales, non-public-view activity, or as a proposal (below).

12.4.4 Lounge / consumption-event proposal — HB 1932, NOT law

Title: “Authorizing cannabis consumption in regulated environments” / “An act relating to authorizing the consumption of cannabis in regulated environments while including protections for public health and safety.”

Sponsors: Representatives Morgan, Waters, Donaghy, Reeves, Walen, Hill, Reed, Fitzgibbon, Simmons, Ormsby, Scott. First read 10 February 2025, referred to Consumer Protection & Business.

Substance from the 2026 House bill report (HBR CPB 26) and 2025 analysis: creates a cannabis consumption event organizer licence, authorising the licensee to organise/conduct a limited number of consumption events, each under its own temporary on-site consumption event permit, at which certain cannabis products may be sold to adults 21+ for on-premises consumption in approved consumption areas. The organiser may prepare/sell certain products; participating producers, processors or retailers may prepare/sell certain products for on-premises consumption at approved events. Only single-use and ready-to-consume products purchased from the organiser or participating licensees may be consumed on the event premises. Conditions include: local permit/approval demonstrated; consumption areas separated from retail sales and other areas; consumption not visible from any public place not authorised for the event; a limited-access area accessible only to the licensee, participating licensees, designated staff and LCB; no employees under 21; employee training on paraphernalia, single-use and ready-to-consume products; a budtender permit/training requirement in the substitute bill; compliance with local ordinances and LCB rules.

Limits/fees in the proposal (reconcile at assembly time, do not merge two versions silently): 2026 bill report — maximum one event per calendar month, each event maximum three consecutive days, except that two years after the effective date LCB by rule may increase the monthly maximum and per-event duration; application fee $500, annual licence renewal $200. 2025 analysis — LCB may by rule set the temporary permit fee, not to exceed $500 per event; the event fee for a social-equity licensee is waived through 1 July 2032. Fee-deposit split in the 2026 report: 83% to the Dedicated Cannabis Account; 10% to the Community Reinvestment Account; 5% to a newly created Youth Cannabis Cessation Account (account name to be confirmed from the bill text).

Status as of 1 November 2026, 09:40 a.m., per the leg.wa.gov bill page: HApprops (House Appropriations). History: 12 January 2026 — by resolution, reintroduced and retained in present status; 30 January 2026 — executive action in House Consumer Protection & Business, majority report: 1st substitute bill be substituted, do pass; minority report: do not pass (committee vote reported secondarily as 11–4); 3 February 2026 — referred to Appropriations. HB 1932 is pending, not enacted. Do not describe a consumption-event organiser licence, permit, or lounge as available.

Sources: https://app.leg.wa.gov/billsummary?BillNumber=1932&Year=2025&Initiative=False — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/Biennium/2025-26/Pdf/Bill%20Reports/House/1932%20HBR%20CPB%2026.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://lawfilesext.leg.wa.gov/biennium/2025-26/Pdf/Bills/House%20Bills/1932.pdf — Accessed 2026-11-01.

Related dead proposal from Run 1, for contrast only: HB 1449 / SB 6204 home grow are dead (HB 1449 in HApprops; SB 6204 in Senate Rules “X” 26 February 2026 per Run 1). Do not present home grow as authorised by HB 1449.

12.5 Cannabis tourism and visitor purchase rules

  • Adult-use purchase by visitors: The adult-use rule is age-based (21+), not residency-based, in the statute excerpts located. Secondary retailer/legal guides state consistently: out-of-state visitors 21+ with valid government-issued photo ID may purchase at licensed retailers; Washington residency is not required; minors cannot enter licensed stores. No official LCB visitor/tourist guidance page was opened in this run — present the visitor rule as: age 21+ at a licensed retailer, with ID verification, and mark the official LCB visitor-guidance wording Verify.
  • Accepted ID (secondary retailer/legal-guide lists — Verify against WAC 314-55-079 / LCB ID rules before presenting as the official list): driver's licence, instruction permit or identification card from any U.S. state, Canadian province, U.S. territory or Washington, D.C.; Washington Department of Licensing ID; U.S. Armed Forces ID; Merchant Marine ID; passport / passport card; NEXUS card; government-issued Washington tribal enrollment card. An expired ID cannot be used to verify age (retailer secondary statement).
  • Visitor transaction limits are the same adult limits as Run 1: 1 oz flower; 16 oz solid infused; 72 oz liquid infused (200 mg THC total for low-dose ≤4 mg units); 7 g concentrate per transaction. Do not apply patient limits to visitors.
  • Out-of-state medical patients — reconcile with Run 1: NO reciprocity. A visitor's home-state medical card does not confer Washington patient purchase/possession limits, database entry, recognition-card status, or the sales/excise-tax exemption. A visitor may buy only within adult-use limits at a licensed retailer. This is the Run 1 §1.4 conclusion and must be repeated identically.
  • Where visitors may consume: Only where consumption is lawful — private, non-public settings. Public consumption is unlawful under RCW 69.50.445 (class 3 civil infraction); consumption in a vehicle on a public highway is a traffic infraction under RCW 46.61.745; consumption on federal land, on cruise ships, and on SeaTac Airport property is prohibited per retailer guidance summaries (secondary for airport/cruise-ship specifics — the federal-land point follows federal law, not a Washington statute). Retailer guides also state consumption is permitted only in private residences or in hotel smoking rooms where the property explicitly permits it — that is a property-policy point, not a statute; present it as such and do not cite it as RCW/WAC law.
  • What visitors may NOT do: Purchase for, or give access to, anyone under 21; purchase on behalf of another person (retailer guidance lists this as prohibited conduct at licensed premises — verify the exact WAC provision at assembly time); transport cannabis across state lines, even into another state where adult-use cannabis is legal (federal prohibition; retailer and legal-guide summaries agree, but cite it as federal law, not as a Washington export offence); take cannabis out of Washington, onto federal land, or onto cruise ships (same caveat).
  • Payment / tourism infrastructure: No official Washington tourism-board cannabis itinerary was located. Do not invent a cannabis-tourism programme. The frequently repeated practical point in secondary guides — cash/debit because card networks avoid cannabis while federally illegal — is secondary and, if included at all, must be labelled secondary and not presented as a Washington rule.

12.6 Landlord and tenant rules

No cannabis-specific statewide landlord-tenant statute was located in this run. The governing framework found is the general Residential Landlord-Tenant Act, chapter 59.18 RCW, plus lease terms and, where applicable, federal housing law (federal subsidised-housing consequences were not opened — mark Verify before stating any federal-housing eviction rule).

12.6.1 RCW 59.18.180 (official text excerpt)

Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=59.18.180 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

  • (1) If a tenant fails to comply with RCW 59.18.130 or 59.18.140 and the noncompliance can substantially affect health/safety or substantially increase fire/accident hazards and can be remedied by repair/replacement/cleaning, the tenant must comply within 30 days after written notice specifying noncompliance (or as promptly as conditions require in an emergency); if not remedied, the landlord may enter, cause the work to be done, and submit an itemised bill for actual/reasonable repair cost, payable on the next rent due date, on mutually agreed terms, or immediately if the rental agreement has terminated. A tenant has a defence to an unlawful-detainer action filed solely on this ground if found in substantial compliance at the chapter 59.12 RCW hearing.
  • (2) Any other substantial noncompliance with RCW 59.18.130 or 59.18.140 is grounds for an unlawful-detainer action under chapter 59.12 RCW, commencing at any time after written notice under chapter 59.12 RCW.
  • (3) If drug-related activity is alleged as a basis for termination under RCW 59.18.130(6), 59.12.030(5), or 59.20.140(5), the compliance provisions of this section do not apply and the landlord may proceed directly to an unlawful-detainer action.
  • (4) If criminal activity on the premises as described in RCW 59.18.130(8) is alleged and the tenant is arrested as a result, the compliance provisions do not apply and the landlord may proceed directly to unlawful detainer against the arrested tenant.
  • (5) If gang-related activity as prohibited in RCW 59.18.130(9) is alleged, the compliance provisions do not apply and the landlord may proceed directly to unlawful detainer under chapter 59.12 RCW at any time after written notice.
  • (6) A landlord may not be held liable in any cause of action for bringing an unlawful-detainer action for drug-related activity, for creating an imminent hazard to physical safety, or for engaging in gang-related activity rendering people in two or more dwelling units/residences insecure in life/use of property or injuring/endangering safety/health in two or more units/residences, under the conditions stated in the full subsection (excerpt truncated — open the full subsection before reproducing the liability shield in full).

12.6.2 RCW 59.18.400 (official text excerpt)

Source: http://apps.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=59.18.400 — Accessed 2026-11-01.

On or before the appearance date, the defendant may appear and answer, asserting any legal/equitable defence or set-off arising from the tenancy. If the complaint alleges termination because the tenant/subtenant/sublessee/resident engaged in drug-related activity, or allowed another person to engage in drug-related activity at the premises with knowledge/consent, no set-off shall be allowed as a defence.

12.6.3 What this does and does not establish

The statutes above create a direct unlawful-detainer route where drug-related activity is alleged; they do not by themselves state that lawful adult-use consumption, lawful patient use, or lawful patient/cooperative growing is “drug-related activity” for termination purposes, nor that a smoke-free lease clause is unenforceable. Lease smoke-free / no-growing clauses, building smoking policies, and local housing rules were found only in secondary lease templates (e.g. a Seattle-area template prohibiting growing medically approved or other marijuana in/around the premises and smoking/vaporising inside or in unauthorised outside areas, citing RCW 69.51A.040). Present lease-clause practice as contractual/local-template material, not as a statewide statutory ban, and mark the question “does lawful use alone support termination?” as Verify / no statewide answer located.

Commercial cannabis tenancy points that ARE established: A cooperative registration requires, if the property is leased/rented, a sworn owner statement granting permission to engage in a cooperative, with owner contact information for verification (WAC 314-55-410(2)(c)). LCB licensed-premises rules (location, access for law enforcement, no connection to a residence, not on federal land, not within another business, not within a federally recognised Tribe's exterior boundaries without written tribal consent — from the LCB 2870 applicant-resources page) affect commercial leases and must be checked before signing, but those location rules are from the social-equity applicant page and should be confirmed against WAC 314-55-050 / licence-type rules at assembly time.

Case note (secondary case summary, unpublished — do not present as a landlord-tenant rule for lawful cannabis): State v. Meland, No. 35208-1-III (Wash. Ct. App. Div. III, filed 13 September 2018, unpublished), affirming a manufacturing conviction, discusses Roberts (80 Wn. App. 342, 345): a landlord knowing a tenant possesses contraband but failing to evict does not thereby exercise dominion/control over the contraband, and cannot be convicted as an accomplice on failure to evict/intervene alone, with reference to chapter 59.18 RCW limits on landlord access. This is a criminal-accomplice point about an unlawful grow, not a holding that landlords may or may not evict for lawful cannabis use. Source found: https://law.justia.com/cases/washington/court-of-appeals-division-iii/2018/35208-1.html — Accessed 2026-11-01.

12.7 Gun ownership conflicts

12.7.1 Federal statute, unchanged in the sources located

18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) makes it unlawful for a person “who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance (as defined in section 102 of the Controlled Substances Act)” to ship, transport, possess, or receive any firearm or ammunition. Congress has not amended §922(g)(3) in the sources located. A knowing violation of §922(g) carries up to 15 years' federal imprisonment and a fine up to $250,000 under 18 U.S.C. §924(a)(8) as summarised secondarily (penalty increased from 10 years by the Bipartisan Safer Communities Act of 2022, per the same secondary source).

Do NOT present the pre-April 2026 secondary claim “marijuana is still Schedule I, therefore every medical user is an unlawful user” as current federal law for state-licensed medical use. The dossier's given federal baseline is that state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026. How “unlawful user” applies after that move, and to which medical users, is Verify — see the draft-form and interim-rule points below.

12.7.2 ATF Form 4473 — current vs draft, preserve the distinction

  • Current form (as described in secondary sources): Question 21.f asks whether the buyer is an unlawful user of, or addicted to, marijuana or any depressant, stimulant, narcotic drug, or any other controlled substance, with a bold warning that use/possession of marijuana remains unlawful under federal law regardless of whether it has been legalised/decriminalised for medicinal or recreational purposes in the buyer's state. Answering “no” while knowingly using marijuana is a separate federal false-statement offence (18 U.S.C. §922(a)(6) per secondary sources; one secondary source cites 18 U.S.C. §1001 perjury with up to 5 years — do not pick between §922(a)(6) and §1001 without opening the current Form 4473 / statute).
  • Draft revision (secondary, U.S. Law Shield summary): ATF published a draft revision to Form 4473 on 8 May 2026 that recasts the question to remove references to medical marijuana and focus on recreational marijuana use, with a warning that “Federal law does not permit the use or possession of marijuana for recreational purposes.” The draft has not taken effect, remains subject to public comment and formal rulemaking, and the current form remains in force; FFLs must continue using the current form. Do not present the draft form as operative.
  • Interim “unlawful user” definition (secondary, FedLaws summary): In January 2026, ATF published an interim final rule rewriting the regulatory definition so an “unlawful user” is someone who regularly uses a controlled substance over an extended period continuing into the present, without a lawful prescription or in a manner substantially different from what a physician prescribed, dropping earlier single-incident triggers (one failed test, arrest or conviction in the past year). Verify against the Federal Register text before stating this definition as current, and reconcile it with the April 2026 Schedule III order at assembly time.
  • ATF 2011 open letter to all federal firearms licensees (secondary summaries): medical-marijuana patients are unlawful users and are prohibited from acquiring/possessing firearms, regardless of state medicinal legislation. That letter predates the April 2026 Schedule III order; do not present it as post-Schedule III guidance without checking for a superseding ATF letter/guidance.

12.7.3 Washington state firearms law — what was and was not found

No Washington statute specifically addressing medical-cannabis patients' firearm rights was located; a secondary Washington cannabis site states there is no specific legislation on medical-marijuana patients' right to possess/own firearms, while also asserting (contradictorily) that DOH asserts registration does not affect gun rights and that Washington complies with federal law. Do not adopt either half of that secondary contradiction as law; mark the Washington-specific interaction Verify.

Washington Concealed Pistol Licence (CPL) application materials (official city/county application pages): applicants are asked “Are you an unlawful user of, or addicted to marijuana, or any depressant, stimulant, or narcotic drug, or any other controlled substance?” and are cautioned: “ALTHOUGH STATE AND LOCAL LAWS DO NOT DIFFER, FEDERAL LAW AND STATE LAW ON THE POSSESSION OF FIREARMS DIFFER. IF YOU ARE PROHIBITED BY FEDERAL LAW FROM POSSESSING A FIREARM, YOU MAY BE PROSECUTED IN FEDERAL COURT. A STATE LICENSE IS NOT A DEFENSE TO FEDERAL PROSECUTION.” East Wenatchee's official CPL page lists, among requirements under RCW 9.41.070, not having been an unlawful user of / addicted to depressants, narcotics, stimulants or any other controlled substance within the past year (marijuana is listed separately in the application question). The full current RCW 9.41.070 eligibility text was not opened — mark CPL eligibility consequences for cannabis users as Verify against RCW 9.41.070 / 9.41.040.

Secondary Washington gun-law summaries state RCW 9.41.040 prohibits firearm possession if the person is an unlawful user of controlled substances, including marijuana despite state legality. Treat that as secondary until RCW 9.41.040 is opened.

Wilson v. Lynch, 9th Circuit: secondary summaries state the court held Second Amendment rights are not violated when a firearm-purchase request is refused for a medical-marijuana cardholder. In the Colorado audit context in the parent's memory, Wilson was narrowed to a dealer-sale denial, not a possession ban. For Washington, present Wilson only as a dealer-sale case, secondary-sourced, Verify citation/holding, and do not extend it to a general possession holding.

United States v. Hemani: secondary sources state the Supreme Court heard oral arguments in early 2026 on §922(g)(3) as applied to marijuana users, with a decision expected by late June 2026. As of the 2026-11-01 cut-off, the outcome was not confirmed in this run — mark Hemani's outcome Verify; do not state it as decided for or against the prohibition.

12.8 Pet and veterinary products

No Washington veterinary cannabis prescribing/recommending law was located in this run. Do not state that Washington veterinarians may prescribe, recommend, or dispense cannabis/CBD for animals, and do not import another state's veterinary guidance (e.g. Wisconsin VEB-GD-002, California discussion law) as Washington law.

Federal / general background (secondary, label as such): FDA has not approved cannabis for use in animals and cannot assure safety/effectiveness; FDA recommends discussing treatment options with a veterinarian. FDA-listed signs of adverse effects from cannabis ingestion in animals, per secondary guides, include unusual lethargy, heavy drooling, vomiting, agitation, tremors and convulsions; xylitol in some human sugar-free products can poison dogs. These are veterinary-safety points, not Washington statutes.

Washington product-channel points that do apply to pet products sold in Washington (from Run 1, to repeat, not expand):

  • Cannabis health and beauty aids (CHABA), RCW 69.50.575: topical-only, ≤0.3% THC, not crossing the blood-brain barrier, not for ingestion — exempt from the cannabis-product rules in chapter 69.50. This is the route for general-retail CBD topicals; it does not authorise ingestible pet CBD.
  • Hemp-derived THC products with any THC are cannabis products — LCB-licensed retail only (Run 1, SB 5367 channel rule). Ingestible hemp CBD: a secondary Washington CBD guide states WSDA says cannabinoid oils such as CBD cannot be sold in ingestible hemp products (gummies, tinctures, foods, drinks, joints, vapes). Verify that WSDA statement against the WSDA page at assembly time; do not present the secondary guide as WSDA text.
  • A Cashmere Veterinary Clinic article (secondary, Washington-based clinic) states veterinarians have seen a rise in marijuana exposure/intoxication in pets since 2012 legalisation; that it remains illegal for veterinarians to prescribe or even recommend marijuana to treat patients; that Seattle-based companies sell CBD pet products marketed as nutraceuticals not regulated by FDA, with no uniform standards, so a product marketed as containing CBD/cannabinoids may or may not contain those ingredients; and that most veterinarians continue traditional pain/seizure/nausea treatment due to legalities. Present this as a clinic article, not as a statute or WSDA rule.

Federal hemp-cap warning — advertorial, NOT authority: A PawOrigins advertorial dated 12 October 2026 claims the federal 0.4 mg total-THC-per-container cap effective 11 December 2026 “catches most full-spectrum CBD products, including non-intoxicating pet oils,” and that bottles already owned stay owned because the rule restricts sale, not ownership. Do not present advertorial claims as law or as Washington guidance. At most, note in the Verify log that the interaction of the post-11 December 2026 federal total-THC / 0.4 mg cap with full-spectrum pet CBD oils is Verify after 11 December 2026, and that Washington's own response to the federal hemp definition was already Verify in Run 1.

12.9 Medical cannabis in schools

No statewide statute was located in this run authorising school staff to store, administer, or assist with medical cannabis, or requiring schools to permit it. Do not present school administration as a statewide mandate.

12.9.1 WSSDA Model Policy 3423, “Parental Administration of Marijuana for Medical Purposes”

Washington State School Directors' Association model policy, adopted by multiple districts — local policy, not statute.

  • The district will permit a student who meets Washington's statutory requirements for medical marijuana to consume marijuana-infused products for medical purposes on school grounds, aboard a school bus, or while attending a school-sponsored event, in accordance with the policy.
  • The district will first verify that the student and parent/guardian meet statutory requirements by requiring presentation of valid Washington recognition cards for medical marijuana under RCW 69.51A.220.
  • The district will not store or administer marijuana-infused products for any purpose. Although the school nurse may oversee compliance with the policy, the nurse will not provide, administer, or assist the student with consumption. The parents/guardian are the only persons who may provide, administer, or assist the student with consumption.
  • Students will not self-carry or self-administer marijuana for medical purposes or any other purpose.
  • Administration by smoking is strictly prohibited.
  • The superintendent/designee will consult building principals to identify a location on school grounds where a parent/guardian can administer the product, considering feasibility and privacy. Parental administration on a school bus is discouraged in the Richland/Stanwood-Camano versions. After administration, the parent/guardian must remove any remaining marijuana from school/district grounds, bus, or event. When a school-sponsored event occurs at another Washington public school, that school's identified location serves for parental administration.
  • Legal references cited in the model: Chapter 69.51A RCW (Medical Cannabis) and RCW 28A.210.260 (Public and private schools — Administration of medication — Conditions). RCW 28A.210.260 itself was not opened — do not state that it authorises cannabis administration; it is cited in the model policy as a general medication-administration reference.
  • Model-policy status conflict in the search results: some district versions classify Policy 3423 as “Encouraged” (adoption dates e.g. Richland, Stanwood-Camano 21 April 2020, Goldendale, Waitsburg 16 January 2020), while a WSSDA model copy hosted for Central Kitsap states “Not recommended for adoption” (model dated 1 July 2019 in that copy). Preserve the conflict; do not state a single statewide WSSDA recommendation without opening the current WSSDA model.
  • District examples found (all local policies, not statewide law): Richland School District Policy 3423; Stanwood-Camano School District Policy 3423; Goldendale Schools Policy 3423; Waitsburg School District Policy 3423.
  • Sources found (district/model PDFs, secondary for statewide purposes): https://app.eduportal.com/documents/view/731859 (Richland) — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://resources.finalsite.net/images/v1695832199/goldendaleschoolsorg/p6gpuffl9dacsasvhbtw/3423-ParentalAdministrationofMarijuanaforMedicalPurposes.pdf (Goldendale) — Accessed 2026-11-01; https://cdnsm5-ss19.sharpschool.com/Userfiles/DBFiles/server_73157214/202211/73775722/3423_parental_administration_of_marijuana_for_medical_purposes.pdf (Stanwood-Camano) — Accessed 2026-11-01.

What remains Verify for schools: whether any Washington statute or DOH/OSPI guidance requires or prohibits a district from adopting Policy 3423; the current WSSDA model status; and whether RCW 69.51A.220 recognition-card verification is the only statutory hook or whether another provision governs school-ground possession by a minor patient/designated provider. State all three as open.

Cross-cutting items carried into the combined file — do not smooth over

  • LCB Schedule III guidance claim (secondary, do not adopt without opening): A FriendlyTreeVenues market summary states that on 16 June 2026 LCB published guidance concluding the April Schedule III order “does not appear to apply” to Washington licensees because of the state's single recreational/medical system. This was not opened on lcb.wa.gov in this run and conflicts in tone with the dossier's given baseline (Schedule III for state-licensed medical). At assembly time, either open the LCB guidance and quote it accurately with its scope, or list the claim as Verify and repeat only the dossier baseline plus the Run 1 Verify flag for endorsement sales. Do not present the FriendlyTree summary as LCB's position.
  • DOH medical-product proposal (WSR 26-20-094): Filed 6 October 2026; hearing 10 November 2026 at 11:00 a.m. virtual; written comments by 10 November 2026 at 11:59 p.m. (and a 27 October 2026 date in the filing for a related step); intended adoption 17 November 2026 (not the effective date). The proposal amends WAC 246-70-010 through 246-70-090. It is a proposal, not law, as of the 2026-11-01 cut-off.
  • Sign-count and advertising-rule currency: See §11.7.2 (Run 1: 4 signs, WSR 26-12-082 effective 4 July 2026; LCB FAQ excerpt: 2 signs, 1,600 sq in). Resolve only by opening the current rule text.
  • Research-fee conflict: See §11.9.1 (superseded — current RCW 69.50.372(7): $250 application / $1,300 annual (audit-resolved)). Resolve only by opening current RCW 69.50.372.
  • Lab-count conflict: See §11.1.5 (LCB list dated 29 October 2026, not counted; WSDA accredited table 3 June 2026 = 4 labs; CannBus FY2025 = 6 certified; DOH proposal = 4 labs testing as of 29 September 2026). Give counts only with source and date, and never publish a single “number of certified labs” without explaining the basis.

Research notes — both runs: every gap, unverified item, conflicting figure, and recommended follow-up

Read this before quoting anything from this file as settled. Items are grouped by theme; the part that contains the full discussion is named in each line. “Re-open” means open the official page directly and read the passage — a search excerpt does not close the item.

A. Federal / Schedule III / 280E — still Verify after both runs

  1. Whether Washington medical-endorsement sales count as “state-licensed medical” for Schedule III / 280E — Verify (Parts 0–1, 10). Follow-up: federal rule text (91 FR 22714) + IRS / LCB / DOR guidance, if any, after 28 Apr 2026.
  2. Whether tribal-compact medical activity counts as “state-licensed” — Verify, including whether a renegotiation-on-rescheduling clause survives in the Oct 2025 compact template / current compacts (Part 10).
  3. Washington response to the federal total-THC definition and 0.4 mg/container cap after 11 Dec 2026 — Verify. WSDA ch. 16-306 rulemaking (CR-101, 17 Nov 2025, WSR 25-23-059) is proposed only, adoption date “[To be determined]” (Part 7). Re-check after 11 Dec 2026.
  4. USDA DEA-registered lab testing enforcement (delayed to 31 Dec 2026) and the federal medical rider (through 11 Dec 2026) — given baseline, no Washington implementation guidance located; re-check after those dates.

G. Method limits of this dossier

  1. Some direct page opens failed transiently in both runs (rate limits, tool/network errors, .docx with no extractable content, list rows not exposed in text fetch). Those facts are flagged Verify in the track text and again here, and were not upgraded from search excerpts.
  2. No parcel-by-parcel zoning-map or LCB-premises check was done for any Part 8 row; no systematic sweep of every listing site was claimed — Part 8 Table (c) removes snippet counts rather than presenting them as a census.
  3. No bulk downloadable AMCO-style licence register issue arises for Washington, but LCB lists carried a data-transfer-error warning in Run 1 and “Pending (Issued)” must be included when counting active licences (Part 5).
  4. Polling in Part 9 is secondary throughout (Civiqs Dec 2025 Washington subsample size/wording Verify; Gallup Oct 2025 is national, not Washington-specific; YouGov Apr 2020 is legal-states, not Washington-only).
  5. Cut-off: 1 November 2026 throughout. No 3 November 2026 election result, no post-11 December 2026 federal-hemp consequence, and no post-cut-off rulemaking adoption is in this file.
